Ultimate Matching Conjecture

Prove or refute Talagrand's Ultimate Matching Conjecture (Conjecture 5.8 of The Status of my favorite problems): for \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2>0\) with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\), with probability tending to one as \(N\to\infty\) there is a permutation \(\pi\) of \(\{1,\dots,N\}\) such that, for \(j=1,2\), \(\sum_{i\le N}\exp\big[\big(\frac{\sqrt N}{L\sqrt{\log N}}|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\big)^{\alpha_j}\big]\le2N\), where \(X_1,\dots,X_N,Y_1,\dots,Y_N\) are independent and uniform on \([0,1]^2\), superscripts denote coordinates and \(L\) does not depend on \(N\).

Starting point

The source is Talagrand's survey The Status of my favorite problems (22 pages, PDF dated 1 October 2026; its hash is in research/references/redistribution.json), Section 5, pages 11–14; a personal copy and its text extraction are held in the ignored private folder. It defers all details and references to [T4]: Talagrand, Upper and lower bounds for stochastic processes: decomposition theorems, Ergeb. Math. Grenzgeb. (3) 60, Springer, 2021, not yet read here. Its introduction states that the conjecture has seen no progress since it was formulated. The thread starts from no earlier result of this repository.

Where we stand

The conjecture is open. The monograph reading fixed the statement (conventions in the working context); the evenly-spread form suffices by the two-sample transfer. The below-curve exclusion shows, in both settings and for every constant, that no pair with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2<1/2\) holds (it contains the common exponent cap). On and above the curve the conjecture's maximum-displacement consequence holds (maximum-displacement threshold, from Leighton–Shor on rectangles of aspect up to \((\log N)^{1/2}\)), so no argument using only maximal displacements refutes a pair of the curve; more generally each level of the condition common to both coordinates holds there alone (per-level counts), so a refutation must use mixed levels or levels coupled in one matching; mixed levels hold too when the first box is larger by a factor tending to infinity and the second level is low, \(e^{2t_2}\lesssim\log N\,t_1^{1/\alpha_1}t_2^{1/\alpha_2}\) (low-level mixed counts).

Reduction. The Hopf–Lax reduction proves that the conjecture at every admissible pair, endpoints included and with one universal constant, follows from one discrepancy bound: conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy, a bound \(\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N}\) for the cell-averaged discrepancy over the class \(\int\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}(|\partial_jG|)\le2\), \(\psi^*\) the conjugate of the exponential cost; its order is forced at every pair (Lipschitz baseline), it is false below the curve (class test), and up to constants it follows from the conjecture whenever a good matching has bounded tube density (tube converse). In primal form (flow bound) any flow bounds the matching cost; the dyadic flow gives \((2,2)\) and fails above exponent \(2\), its per-point values being Gaussian (obstruction).

Route reviews. The first found no mechanism for lighter-than-Gaussian per-point tails; per-level Hall conditions do not glue (counterexample). The second: up to the constant the conjecture is one assignment-cost bound for one matching; the obstacle is coupling the levels. Superposing large-gradient test functions, Leighton–Shor ones at several aspects included, gains only \(1+o(1)\) in the class form (aligned superposition, band overlap). The third keeps the class bound as core, through a candidate coarea mechanism (gradient levels, Leighton–Shor truncated at their scale), and tests it at two gradient levels.

Known results. Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády and Leighton–Shor control the sum \(L\sqrt{N\log N}\) and the maximum \(L(\log N)^{3/4}/\sqrt N\) of the distances, both optimal; the conjecture at \((4,4)\) gives both at once (consequences), which is the survey's Problem 5.3, solved by [C5]; its matching chooses which points leave each coarse cell blind to position, and every such scheme fails \((4,4)\) (position-blind cell exits). [T4] proves \((\alpha,\infty)\) for \(\alpha<1/2\) (Theorem 17.1.3) and \((2,2)\) with probability \(9/10\); Talagrand–Yukich (1993) prove \((2,2)\) for two samples; both lie off the curve. The three-dimensional analogue is proved (Theorem 18.1.1 of [T4]).

Why the method stops. [T4] reduces Shor's theorem to a discrepancy bound for a class \(\mathcal H\) of anisotropic gradient-penalized functions, decomposes \(\mathcal H\) into Lipschitz classes \(\mathcal H_j\) with small support, and bounds the supremum of the sum by the sum of the suprema; "This entails a loss which seems to prevent reaching the correct value \(\alpha=2\)". The right chaining functional for \(\mathcal H\) is unknown.

Open statements

  1. Ultimate Matching Conjecture — open, the full target. The statement of the goal above (conj:ultimate-matching), for every pair \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in(0,\infty]\) with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\), with one universal \(L\). Sufficient: the same with evenly spread \(Y\) (Research Problem 17.1.2 of [T4]), by the two-sample transfer. It cannot be strengthened at any pair below the curve, in either setting (thm:below-curve-matching-exclusion).
  2. Conjugate-class discrepancy — open, sufficient for the full target. conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy: there is \(L\ge132\) such that for every pair \(\alpha\in[2,\infty]^2\) on the curve, with probability tending to one every Lipschitz \(G\) with \(\int_\Omega\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}(|\partial_jG|)\le2\) has \(\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\le\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N}\) (notation in the working context). Implies the conjecture at every admissible pair by the Hopf–Lax reduction. Its order cannot be lowered at interior pairs of the curve (lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound); no upper-bound mechanism exists yet.
  3. Conjecture 5.5 — open, a special case. With high probability some matching has \(\sum_{i\le N}\exp\big[\big(\frac{\sqrt N}{L\sqrt{\log N}}|X_i^1-Y^1_{\pi(i)}|\big)^2\big]\le2N\) and \(\max_i|X_i^2-Y^2_{\pi(i)}|\le L\sqrt{\log N}/\sqrt N\). The printed (5.1) places the square outside the exponential; the intended reading \(\exp(x^2)\) is the one consistent with Conjecture 5.8 and with Conjecture 17.1.4 of [T4], which is this case in the evenly-spread setting.
  4. Grid discrepancy — open, a route to the special case. Research Problem 17.2.2 of [T4] at \(\theta_1(x)=x(\log(3+x))^{1/2}\), \(\theta_2(x)=x\), which [T4] says would, with "significant extra work", prove Conjecture 17.1.4. The survey's Conjectures 5.6–5.7 use \(\theta(x)=x\log(x+3)\), a smaller class, whose sufficiency the survey calls "plausible" without the details; see the working context.

The remaining route

  1. Mechanism for the full conjecture. Find a discrepancy formulation of the anisotropic exponential-moment condition for general \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\), analogous to the one [T4] gives for Conjecture 5.5, and bound the size of the resulting function class; or find a construction showing the conjecture fails for some pair. The reduction to a discrepancy statement is done (Hopf–Lax reduction); what remains is to bound the conjugate class's discrepancy at Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády size without [T4]'s lethal sum-of-suprema loss, or to show that Leighton–Shor-type functions exceed it on the curve. The exponent sum \(1/2\) is necessary (below-curve exclusion); a refutation on the curve needs more than maximal displacements (maximum-displacement threshold) and more than counts at one level common to both coordinates (per-level counts): it must use mixed levels outside the region of the low-level mixed counts (bounded box ratio, or a high second level such as \(t_1=\log N\), \(t_2=\sqrt{\log N}\) at \((4,4)\)) or the coupling of levels within one matching.
  2. Close Conjecture 5.5. Raise the proved exponent from \(\alpha<1/2\) to \(2\), through Research Problem 17.2.2 or otherwise. The obstacle is the unknown geometry of \(\mathcal H\), the right functional for \(\gamma_2(\mathcal H,d_2)\).
  3. Remaining sources. The statements of [T4] are read. Talagrand–Yukich (reference [134]) is read in its statements, not its proofs. [C5] is read in its construction (Lemmas 2.6–2.9 unread, not needed). Unread: the proof of Theorem 17.1.3 ([T4]'s reference [129]).

Proposed next step

Route item: side-ultimate-matching. The fourth route review is due (six research entries since the third). It weighs the line's state since the third route review: the coarea mechanism's tools (mesh, two-mesh and local-factor discrepancy, the cellwise split), the class lower bound's witness within the split's reach (dominant-level split), and the open cases, many thin bands of one small gradient level and the ring condition at several levels. It takes no queue item; the queue head stays sandpile-odometer-flow.

Working mathematical context

Setting

Goal: \(X_1,\dots,X_N\) and \(Y_1,\dots,Y_N\) independent, uniform on \([0,1]^2\); \(X_i^j\) is the \(j\)-th coordinate; a matching is a permutation \(\pi\). Sufficient form ([T4]): \(Y\) evenly spread, that is \([0,1]^2\) covered by \(N\) rectangles of area \(1/N\) with disjoint interiors, each containing exactly one \(Y_i\) and inside \(B(Y_i,10/\sqrt N)\). \(L\) is universal, independent of \(N\) and of \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\) ([T4]'s convention; \(K\) may depend on parameters). "With high probability" means probability tending to \(1\), no rate. \(d\) is Euclidean. Scale \(\sqrt{\log N/N}\). Call \(\pi\) \((L,\alpha)\)-good in coordinate \(j\) if \(\sum_i\exp[(\frac{\sqrt N}{L\sqrt{\log N}}|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|)^\alpha]\le2N\), or for \(\alpha=\infty\) \(\max_i|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\le L\sqrt{\log N/N}\).

The curve's parameters

Pairs \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in[2,\infty]\) with \(\beta_1+\beta_2=1/2\), \(\beta_j=1/\alpha_j\); scale \(s=L\sqrt{\log N/N}\) with one universal \(L\); level \(t\in[1,\log N]\) allows at most \(2Ne^{-t}\) pairs beyond \(st^{\beta_j}\) in each coordinate \(j\), so at most \(4Ne^{-t}\) outside the box \(st^{\beta_1}\times st^{\beta_2}\), of area \(s^2\sqrt t\) at every pair; the top level \(t=\log N\) is the maximum bound \(\max_i|\Delta_i^j|\le su_j\), \(u_j=(\log N)^{\beta_j}\).

Proved tools

Two-sample transfer: good matchings of \(X\) and of \(Y\) to one common \(Z\) compose into a \((\max(2,2^{1/\alpha})L,\alpha)\)-good matching of \(X\) to \(Y\); evenly-spread theorems with failure \(\varepsilon_N\) give two-sample ones with failure \(2\varepsilon_N\). Consequences: \(\max_i\Delta^j_i\le L\sqrt{\log N}(\log2N)^{1/\alpha_j}/\sqrt N\); for \(\alpha_j\ge1\), \(\sum_i\Delta^j_i\le2L\sqrt{N\log N}\); at \((4,4)\) both Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády and Leighton–Shor bounds with \(4L\); at \((2,\infty)\) Shor's bounds with \(2L\). Common exponent cap: in the evenly-spread setting no pair in \((4,\infty]^2\) holds with probability tending to one, from Theorems 4.7.1 and 4.8.1 of [T4] by a reverse-Markov step; contained in the below-curve exclusion: for \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2<1/2\), every \(L\), both settings, with probability tending to one no matching is good in both coordinates (scaled Leighton–Shor subgraph with \(\kappa=(\log N)^{1/\alpha_2-1/\alpha_1}\), anisotropic boundary layer). Anisotropic Leighton–Shor bound: on a rectangle of sides \(a\ge b\), \(a/b\le(\log N)^{1/2}\), \(N\) uniform points match a fixed tiling set within \(K\sqrt{ab/N}(\log N)^{3/4}\) per coordinate (quantile strips, one boundary point per strip moved off the grid); hence the threshold: \(\max_i|\Delta_i^j|\le K(\log N)^{1/\alpha_j}\sqrt{\log N/N}\) is attainable exactly when \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2\ge1/2\). Level count: an \((L,\alpha)\)-good coordinate has at most \(2Ne^{-t}\) pairs with \(|\Delta_i^j|\ge L\sqrt{\log N/N}\,t^{1/\alpha}\). Position-blind cell exits: for each fixed rule sending a label-chosen excess of each cell of side \(s\to0\), \(s\sqrt N\ge\delta(\log N)^{1/2+\gamma}\), out of its cell, the matching fails every pair and constant with probability tending to one; [C5]'s matching is one. Per-level counts: on and above the curve, for each \(t\in[1,\log N]\) separately, common to both coordinates, some matching has at most \(2Ne^{-t}\) pairs beyond \(L\sqrt{\log N/N}\,t^{1/\alpha_j}\) in some coordinate (rescaled cells of \(e^{2t}(\log N)^2\) points, Leighton–Shor inside, leftovers counted). Low-level mixed counts: for every pair and \(L>0\), levels with \(t_1^{1/\alpha_1}/t_2^{1/\alpha_2}\to\infty\) and \(e^{2t_2}\le\frac{L^2}8\log N\,t_1^{1/\alpha_1}t_2^{1/\alpha_2}\), some matching has no pair beyond the first level and at most \(2Ne^{-t_2}\) beyond the second (cells, then column-sorted leftovers; Doob for the strip discrepancy).Hopf–Lax matching bound: for a separable convex finite cost \(C\) with \(C(0)=0\) and cell counts at least \(\lambda|Q|\) on a grid of side \(\delta\), \(\min_\pi\sum C(X_i-Y_{\pi(i)})\le\sup_g[\int g\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)-(1-2h)\lambda\int C^*(\nabla g)]+2hN\sum_j\Psi_j(\delta/h)\), \(\mu\) the cell-smeared measures. Reduction: costs \(\psi_\alpha(t/s)\), \(\psi_\alpha(t)=e^{t^\alpha}-1\), \(\psi_\infty(t)=N(t-1)_+\), \(s=L\sqrt{\log N/N}\), grid side \(1/\lceil32/s\rceil\), \(L\ge132\), \(h=1/4\); then \(D_N\le\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N}\) gives the pair. Replacing \(\psi^*\) by \(\max(\psi^*,x)\ge x\) changes \(D_N\) by at most a factor \(e\).

Discrepancy classes

Grid \(G=\{1,\dots,2^p\}^2\), \(2^{-p}\approx L^*\sqrt{\log N/N}\), \(n(\tau)\) evenly-spread points per cell with \(m_0\le n(\tau)\le2m_0\), law \(\mu(\{\tau\})=n(\tau)/N\). [T4]'s Research Problem 17.2.2: for which \(\theta_1,\theta_2\ge x\) does the class with \(\sum\theta_1(|h(k+1,\ell)-h(k,\ell)|)+\sum\theta_2(|h(k,\ell+1)-h(k,\ell)|)\le2^{2p}\) satisfy \(\mathbb E\sup_h\sum_{i\le N}(h(U_i)-\int h\,d\mu)\le K\sqrt{pm_0}\,2^{2p}\)? Shor's theorem uses horizontal increments at most \(1\) and vertical sum at most \(2^{2p}\) (Theorem 17.2.1, probability \(1-e^{-46p}\)); the duality step is Proposition 17.2.4. The survey's (5.2) has \(\theta(x)=x\log(x+3)\), uniform law, mean zero, and Conjecture 5.7 its Gaussian form \(L\sqrt p\,2^{2p}\). With \(\theta_1=\theta_2=x\) the best Gaussian bound is \(Lp^{3/4}2^{2p}\).

Dimension three

Theorem 18.1.1 of [T4]: for \(\varphi\) with sublevel boxes \(\{\varphi\le N_k\}=S_k\) of volume \(2^{k+3}\), probability \(1-LN^{-10}\), universal \(L\); includes \(\sum_j1/\alpha_j=1\). Necessity of the volume growth by an empty box of volume about \(\log N/N\) (Proposition 18.1.3). In dimension two empty boxes give no constraint on the exponent sum, by a heuristic count recorded with the cap.

Gaps identified by formalization

No formalization is assigned, and no recorded formalization gap applies to this thread.

Lead and bridge queue

Passed Outside leads and Absurd bridges and listed sub-ideas awaiting development, oldest first (FIFO), each sub-idea under the item it belongs to; rules in tools/lead_queue.py.

  1. Simulate the divisible-sandpile odometer flow of the excess of \(X\) over the background on a grid and compare its per-point tails with the dyadic flow's; a nonlinear flow that moves mass only where needed is a candidate against the Gaussian per-point obstacle.
  2. Route each unit of dyadic excess along the less loaded of two candidate paths, keeping the divergence, and test whether the per-point loads lose their Gaussian tails as two choices do for balls into bins.
  3. Test whether Leighton–Shor subgraphs whose vertical scaling varies across the tent scales gain an unbounded factor over constant scaling on the curve, which would refute a pair of the curve; a test of the conditional maximal-displacement barrier.
  4. Decide whether a per-level Hall condition with completion lengths (vertices unmatched at level \(k\) matchable within \(G_{k+1}\)) suffices to glue one matching at a constant factor, beginning with random one-dimensional samples where the monotone optimum is known.
  5. Unverified reviewer lead from the position-blind exits entry: with each coarse cell's exits chosen nearest the side they cross, decide whether the \((4,4)\) level counts at one scale follow from a \(\psi_4\) tail bound on the cell flow's edge loads normalized by \(\sqrt{\lambda\log N}\) (prove the sufficient direction in the setting of the dyadic flow bound), then whether an Orlicz version of [C5]'s Lipschitz truncation (its Lemma 2.10) gives such a flow at one scale, and what the sum over dyadic scales loses. (source)
  6. Research record

Conjecture statement fixed; diagonal exponent cap in the evenly-spread setting

16 October 2026 · Working proofs, elementary, with a source reading · Monograph statements. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Question. The thread's goal is Conjecture 5.8 of the survey The Status of my favorite problems, which defers every detail to [T4], Talagrand's Upper and Lower Bounds for Stochastic Processes, 2nd edition, Springer 2021 (author-hosted at michel.talagrand.net/ULBSPRINGER.pdf, 727 pages; a personal copy and its text extraction are held in the ignored private folder; hash in research/references/redistribution.json). The missing implication for the goal is a matching construction for every admissible pair \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\); before any attempt, the exact statement to be proved has to be fixed. This entry reads the monograph's statements, settles the four ambiguities the setup recorded, and records with proofs the two elementary facts the setup used without proof. It ends when each ambiguity is settled from a read statement or recorded as open.

What the monograph states

Sources read. Read in the rendered or extracted text of [T4]: Section 4.3 (the evenly spread points and the duality Proposition 4.3.2), the statements of Theorems 4.5.1, 4.7.1 and 4.8.1, Section 4.9 and the Notes 4.10, all of Sections 17.1–17.3, Section 18.1 through Exercise 18.1.4 and its consequence, Appendix E.2, and the convention on the constants \(L\) and \(K\) in Chapter 2. The proofs of Theorem 17.1.3 (deferred to [T4]'s reference [129], The Generic Chaining, 2005), of Proposition 17.3.2 (reference [132]) and of the Talagrand–Yukich bound (reference [134]) are not in [T4] and were not read. The reviewer received bounded excerpts; the statements it could not see in them (the law \(\mu\), the support condition (17.40), the loss sentence after (17.42), the hypotheses of Theorem 18.1.1 and Proposition 18.1.3) were reread in the full text after the review, (17.40) from the rendered page.

The conjecture. Research Problem 17.1.2 of [T4] (page 550) asks to prove or disprove: for \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2>0\) with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\), with high probability there is a matching \(\pi\) with \(\sum_{i\le N}\exp\big(\big(\sqrt{N/\log N}\,|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|/L\big)^{\alpha_j}\big)\le2N\) for \(j=1,2\). The normalization is the survey's. The setting differs: in [T4] the \(X_i\) are i.i.d. uniform on \([0,1]^2\) and the \(Y_i\) are nonrandom evenly spread points (Section 4.3: \([0,1]^2\) is covered by \(N\) rectangles of area \(1/N\) with disjoint interiors, each containing exactly one \(Y_i\) and contained in \(B(Y_i,10/\sqrt N)\)); the survey takes two independent i.i.d. samples. The survey's two-sample form is the thread's goal; the lemma below shows that the monograph's form implies it, so the monograph's form is a sufficient target, not a necessary one.

Ambiguity one: the constant. Settled. In [T4], "\(L\) denotes a universal constant", "a number that do[es] not depend on anything, while \(K\) might depend on some parameters, such as \(\alpha\)" (Chapter 2). Research Problem 17.1.2 writes \(L\), so the constant may not depend on \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\); the partial result writes \(K(\alpha)\). The survey agrees: "\(L\) is a universal constant (i.e. a number independent of \(N\))", and of its partial result it says "\(L\) is replaced by a number depending on \(\alpha\)".

Ambiguity two: the endpoint. Settled. [T4] states that for "\(\alpha_1=2,\alpha_2=\infty\)" one may interpret (17.3) for \(j=2\) as \(\sup_i|X_i^2-Y^2_{\pi(i)}|\le L\sqrt{\log N/N}\) (its (17.2)), and Conjecture 17.1.4 (Theorem 17.1.3 at \(\alpha=2\)) is that case; the survey's Conjecture 5.5 states the maximum bound directly. The text after Exercise 18.1.4 uses the convention \(|x|^\infty=0\) for \(|x|<1\), \(\infty\) otherwise; the strict inequality differs from the maximum bound's \(\le\) only by a change of constant.

Ambiguity three: the probability. Settled by the survey, which says after its Theorem 5.1: "Here and below … 'with high probability' means 'with probability going to 1 as \(N\to\infty\)'"; this governs its Conjecture 5.8, the goal. No rate is required. Research Problem 17.1.2 of [T4] says only "with high probability". The related proved theorems state explicit rates: \(1-LN^{-10}\) in Theorems 17.1.1, 17.1.3 and 18.1.1, \(1-L\exp(-(\log N)^{3/2}/L)\) in Theorem 4.7.1, and \(9/10\) in the Talagrand–Yukich bound (4.131); Theorem 4.5.1 bounds an expectation instead.

Ambiguity four: the discrepancy class. Recorded as a discrepancy between the sources, not settled. Research Problem 17.2.2 of [T4] asks for which \(\theta_1,\theta_2\ge x\) the class \(\mathcal H\) of \(h\) on \(G=\{1,\dots,2^p\}^2\) with \(\sum\theta_1(|h(k+1,\ell)-h(k,\ell)|)+\sum\theta_2(|h(k,\ell+1)-h(k,\ell)|)\le2^{2p}\) satisfies \(\mathbb E\sup_{h\in\mathcal H}\sum_{i\le N}(h(U_i)-\int h\,d\mu)\le K\sqrt{pm_0}\,2^{2p}\), where the \(U_i\) are i.i.d. with the nonuniform law \(\mu(\{\tau\})=n(\tau)/N\) and \(m_0\le n(\tau)\le2m_0\). It names \(\theta_1(x)=x(\log(3+x))^{1/2}\), \(\theta_2(x)=x\): "A positive answer (and significant extra work) would allow one to prove Conjecture 17.1.4." The survey's (5.2) instead uses \(\theta(x)=x\log(x+3)\), the uniform law, and a bound in probability. Since \(x\log(x+3)\ge x(\log(3+x))^{1/2}\) for \(x\ge0\), the survey's class is contained in the monograph's; apart from the law of the sample and the form of the bound, its Conjecture 5.6 therefore asks less. [T4] attributes the route to Conjecture 17.1.4 only to the larger class, so the survey's claim that Conjecture 5.6 suffices for Conjecture 5.5 is not backed by the monograph's stated reduction; the survey itself footnotes it: "It might require some work to figure out all the details, but the claim is plausible!" Which \(\theta\) the survey intends is not decided here.

Known exponent pairs. In the monograph's setting: Theorem 17.1.3 gives the pair \((\alpha,\infty)\) for every \(0<\alpha<1/2\), with constant \(K(\alpha)\) and probability \(1-LN^{-10}\); the text says "We do not know how to prove Theorem 17.1.3 for \(\alpha\ge1/2\)". The Talagrand–Yukich bound (4.131), \(\frac1N\sum_i\exp(Nd(X_i,Y_{\pi(i)})^2/(L\log N))\le2\) with probability at least \(9/10\), gives the isotropic pair \((2,2)\) in each coordinate with constant \(\sqrt L\), since \(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\le d(X_i,Y_{\pi(i)})\), but only with probability \(9/10\), not with high probability in the survey's sense. Both lie off the conjectured curve \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\). The simultaneous sum and maximum bound, the conjecture's consequence at \((4,4)\) below, is the survey's Problem 5.3, which the survey reports solved by a construction [C5] it says does not touch the real issues; [C5] was not read. Shor's Theorem 17.1.1 is the sum bound in coordinate one with the maximum bound in coordinate two.

Why the method stops. Section 17.3 of [T4] calls it the "lethal weakness": the class \(\mathcal H\) is decomposed as \(h=\sum_jh_j\) with \(h_j\) in classes \(\mathcal H_j\) of functions with horizontal increments at most \(1\), vertical increments at most \(2^j\), and at most \(2^{2p-j}\) points where \(h_j\ne0\) (Proposition 17.3.1, (17.39)–(17.40)), and the supremum over \(\mathcal H\) is bounded by the sum of the suprema over the \(\mathcal H_j\) (17.42). The text says "This entails a loss which seems to prevent reaching the correct value \(\alpha=2\)", and that the right functional for \(\gamma_2(\mathcal H,d_2)\) "can be discovered only by understanding the underlying geometry, which the author does not". Appendix E.2 adds: "Possibly, it is only a hard combinatorial problem to crack, to understand the geometry of certain classes of functions".

Dimension three. Theorem 18.1.1 of [T4] proves the three-dimensional analogue for every \(\varphi\) whose sublevel sets \(\{\varphi\le N_k\}\) are boxes \(S_k\) of volume \(2^{k+3}\), with a universal constant and probability \(1-LN^{-10}\); by Exercise 18.1.4 this includes \(\psi=\exp(\frac13\sum_j|x_j|^{\alpha_j})-1\) for \(\alpha_j\in(0,\infty]\) with \(\sum_j1/\alpha_j=1\), which the text calls the ultimate matching conjecture in that setting. Proposition 18.1.3 shows the volume growth is necessary there, by an empty box of volume about \(\log N/N\). The sections read give no corresponding lower-bound argument for the two-dimensional exponent sum \(1/2\); the survey says only that the powers of \(\log N\) in Theorems 5.1 and 5.2 are optimal. The diagonal case is proved below.

General statement. lem:two-sample-matching-transfer: for every \(N\ge2\), every exponent pair in \((0,\infty]^2\) and every point sets, matchings of both samples to one common point set compose into a matching of the two samples whose exponential moments are bounded with the constant multiplied by \(\max(2,2^{1/\alpha_j})\).

Two-sample matching transfer

Setting. Fix \(N\ge2\), \(L>0\) and \(\alpha\in(0,\infty]\). For finite sequences \(P=(P_i)_{i\le N}\), \(Q=(Q_k)_{k\le N}\) of points of \(\mathbb R^2\), a bijection \(\sigma\) of \(\{1,\dots,N\}\) and a coordinate \(j\), say that \(\sigma\) is \((L,\alpha)\)-good in coordinate \(j\) if \(\sum_{i\le N}\exp\big[\big(\frac{\sqrt N}{L\sqrt{\log N}}|P_i^j-Q^j_{\sigma(i)}|\big)^{\alpha}\big]\le2N\) when \(\alpha<\infty\), and \(\max_i|P_i^j-Q^j_{\sigma(i)}|\le L\sqrt{\log N}/\sqrt N\) when \(\alpha=\infty\). Put \(\kappa(\alpha)=\max(2,2^{1/\alpha})\), with \(\kappa(\infty)=2\).

Statement. (1) Let \(X,Y,Z\) be sequences of \(N\) points and \(\sigma_1,\sigma_2\) bijections such that \(\sigma_1\) is \((L,\alpha)\)-good for \((X,Z)\) and \(\sigma_2\) is \((L,\alpha)\)-good for \((Y,Z)\) in coordinate \(j\). Then \(\pi=\sigma_2^{-1}\circ\sigma_1\) is \((\kappa(\alpha)L,\alpha)\)-good for \((X,Y)\) in coordinate \(j\). (2) Consequently, suppose that for some pair \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\in(0,\infty]^2\), some \(L\) and some \(\varepsilon_N\), for i.i.d. uniform \(X\) of size \(N\) and some fixed evenly spread sequence \(Z\) of size \(N\) there is with probability at least \(1-\varepsilon_N\) a matching that is \((L,\alpha_j)\)-good in both coordinates. Then for two i.i.d. uniform samples \(X,Y\) of size \(N\), with no independence between them assumed, there is with probability at least \(1-2\varepsilon_N\) a matching that is \((\kappa(\alpha_j)L,\alpha_j)\)-good in coordinate \(j\) for \(j=1,2\).

Proof. (1) Write \(c=\sqrt N/(L\sqrt{\log N})\), \(a_i=|X_i^j-Z^j_{\sigma_1(i)}|\) and \(b_k=|Y_k^j-Z^j_{\sigma_2(k)}|\). Since \(\sigma_2(\pi(i))=\sigma_1(i)\), the triangle inequality gives \(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\le a_i+b_{\pi(i)}\). If \(\alpha=\infty\), then \(\max_i(a_i+b_{\pi(i)})\le2L\sqrt{\log N}/\sqrt N\). If \(\alpha<\infty\), we claim \((c(a+b)/\kappa)^\alpha\le\frac12((ca)^\alpha+(cb)^\alpha)\) for \(a,b\ge0\). For \(\alpha\ge1\), \(\kappa=2\) and this is convexity of \(t\mapsto t^\alpha\). For \(\alpha<1\), \(\kappa^\alpha=2\) and \((a+b)^\alpha\le a^\alpha+b^\alpha\) (subadditivity of the concave function \(t^\alpha\) with value \(0\) at \(0\)). Applying the increasing function \(\exp\) and then the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means, \(\exp((c(a+b)/\kappa)^\alpha)\le\big(e^{(ca)^\alpha}e^{(cb)^\alpha}\big)^{1/2}\le\frac12\big(e^{(ca)^\alpha}+e^{(cb)^\alpha}\big)\). Since \(t\mapsto\exp((ct/\kappa)^\alpha)\) is nondecreasing, summing over \(i\) gives \(\sum_i\exp\big[\big(\frac{c}{\kappa}|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\big)^\alpha\big]\le\frac12\sum_ie^{(ca_i)^\alpha}+\frac12\sum_ie^{(cb_{\pi(i)})^\alpha}\le\frac12(2N)+\frac12(2N)=2N\), where the second sum runs over all \(k=\pi(i)\) because \(\pi\) is a bijection. As \(c/\kappa=\sqrt N/(\kappa L\sqrt{\log N})\), this is the claim. (2) Let \(Z\) be the fixed evenly spread points of the hypothesis. Each of \(X\) and \(Y\) is an i.i.d. uniform sample of size \(N\), so each event "some matching to \(Z\) is good in both coordinates" has probability at least \(1-\varepsilon_N\); their intersection has probability at least \(1-2\varepsilon_N\) by the union bound, whatever the joint law of \(X\) and \(Y\). On it, (1) applied in each coordinate with the same \(\pi=\sigma_2^{-1}\circ\sigma_1\) gives the claim. \(\square\)

Scope. Only the direction from evenly spread points to two samples is proved. The converse, deducing the monograph's form from the survey's, is not addressed. Applied to Theorem 17.1.3 of [T4] (the pair \((\alpha,\infty)\), \(0<\alpha<1/2\)), part (2) gives its two-sample form with constants \(2^{1/\alpha}K(\alpha)\) and \(2K(\alpha)\) and probability at least \(1-2LN^{-10}\); this is the setting in which the survey states the partial result. For the conjecture itself the constant becomes \(2L\) at every admissible pair, since there \(\alpha_j\ge2\).

Exponential matching consequences

Statement. Let \(N\ge2\), \(L>0\), and let \(\pi\) be \((L,\alpha_j)\)-good for \((X,Y)\) in coordinate \(j\), with \(\alpha_j\in(0,\infty]\), for any point sequences; write \(\Delta_i^j=|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\). (a) \(\max_i\Delta_i^j\le L\sqrt{\log N}\,(\log 2N)^{1/\alpha_j}/\sqrt N\), which is at most \(2L(\log N)^{1/2+1/\alpha_j}/\sqrt N\) when \(\alpha_j\ge1\). (b) If \(\alpha_j\ge1\), then \(\sum_i\Delta_i^j\le2L\sqrt{N\log N}\). (c) If \(\pi\) is good in both coordinates for the pair \((4,4)\), then with \(d\) the Euclidean distance, \(\sum_id(X_i,Y_{\pi(i)})\le4L\sqrt{N\log N}\) and \(\max_id(X_i,Y_{\pi(i)})\le4L(\log N)^{3/4}/\sqrt N\): one matching meets the bounds of both the Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády and the Leighton–Shor theorems. (d) If \(\pi\) is good in both coordinates for the pair \((2,\infty)\), it meets both bounds of Shor's theorem with constant \(2L\). (e) For every pair with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\), the product of the two bounds in (a) is \(L^2(\log N)(\log2N)^{1/2}/N\).

Proof. Write \(t_i=\frac{\sqrt N}{L\sqrt{\log N}}\Delta_i^j\). If \(\alpha_j=\infty\), (a) is the definition. Otherwise every summand \(e^{t_i^{\alpha_j}}\) is at least \(1\), so each is at most \(2N-(N-1)\le2N\), whence \(t_i\le(\log2N)^{1/\alpha_j}\). For \(N\ge2\), \(\log2N\le2\log N\), so \((\log2N)^{1/\alpha_j}\le2^{1/\alpha_j}(\log N)^{1/\alpha_j}\le2(\log N)^{1/\alpha_j}\) when \(\alpha_j\ge1\). For (b), \(t\le\max(1,t^{\alpha_j})\le e^{t^{\alpha_j}}\) for \(t\ge0\) and \(\alpha_j\ge1\), so \(\sum_it_i\le2N\); if \(\alpha_j=\infty\), \(\sum_i\Delta_i^j\le N\max_i\Delta_i^j\le L\sqrt{N\log N}\). For (c), \(d\le\Delta^1+\Delta^2\); add (b) for both coordinates, and add (a) at \(\alpha_j=4\). For (d), (b) at \(\alpha_1=2\) gives the sum bound in coordinate one with \(2L\) and the definition at \(\alpha_2=\infty\) gives the maximum bound. For (e), the exponents of \(\log2N\) add to \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\). \(\square\)

Scope. These are the deductions [T4] sketches after Research Problem 17.1.2 and the survey after Conjecture 5.8, with explicit constants.

Common exponent cap

Statement. In the monograph's setting (\(X\) i.i.d. uniform on \([0,1]^2\), \(Y=Y^{(N)}\) any evenly spread sequence for each \(N\)), let \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in(4,\infty]\) and \(L'>0\). Then it is false that, with probability tending to one as \(N\to\infty\), some matching is \((L',\alpha_j)\)-good in coordinate \(j\) for \(j=1,2\). Hence on the diagonal \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2\) the conjecture's exponent \(4\), the point where \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\), cannot be increased.

Proof. This argument was proposed by the reviewer and checked here. Let \(M=\inf_\pi\max_id(X_i,Y_{\pi(i)})\) and \(a_N=(\log N)^{3/4}/\sqrt N\). Theorem 4.7.1 of [T4] gives a universal \(L_1\) with \(P(M>L_1a_N)\le L_1\exp(-(\log N)^{3/2}/L_1)\); Theorem 4.8.1 gives a universal \(L_2\) with \(\mathbb EM\ge a_N/L_2\). Always \(M\le\sqrt2\). Let \(G=\{M\le L_1a_N\}\). Then \(\mathbb E[M1_G]\ge a_N/L_2-\sqrt2P(G^c)\ge a_N/(2L_2)\) for all large \(N\), since \(\exp(-(\log N)^{3/2}/L_1)\) decays faster than every power of \(N\) while \(a_N\ge N^{-1/2}\). With \(b=a_N/(4L_2)\), \(\mathbb E[M1_G]\le b+L_1a_NP(M>b)\), so \(P(M>a_N/(4L_2))\ge1/(4L_1L_2)\) for all large \(N\). Now suppose the matching statement held. Put \(\beta=\max_j1/\alpha_j<1/4\). On its event, part (a) of the consequences lemma (with \(\alpha_j\ge1\)) and \(d\le\Delta^1+\Delta^2\) give \(M\le4L'(\log N)^{1/2+\beta}/\sqrt N\), which is below \(a_N/(4L_2)\) once \((\log N)^{1/4-\beta}>16L'L_2\). So for large \(N\), \(P(M>a_N/(4L_2))\) is at most the failure probability, which tends to zero, contradicting the bound \(1/(4L_1L_2)\). \(\square\)

Scope. The cap concerns the monograph's evenly-spread form only: excluding the survey's two-sample form would need a two-sample Leighton–Shor lower bound, or the converse of the transfer lemma, neither available here. It excludes every pair with both exponents above \(4\), which includes every diagonal pair with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2<1/2\), but not the off-diagonal pairs with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2<1/2\) and one exponent at most \(4\); whether those are excluded is open. Empty boxes do not supply one, by a heuristic count, not a proof: the largest empty axis-parallel box has area about \(\log N/N\) (the count in the informal proof of Lemma 18.1.2 of [T4], in dimension two), and a point of \(Y\) at its centre violates both coordinate bounds only if its sides exceed \(2L\sqrt{\log N}(\log2N)^{1/\alpha_j}/\sqrt N\), an area at least \(4L^2\log N/N\), whatever the exponent sum. So an off-diagonal cap would need an anisotropic form of the Leighton–Shor construction.

Degenerate cases. \(N=1\) makes \(\log N=0\) and the normalization undefined; the first two lemmas assume \(N\ge2\), and the cap concerns large \(N\) only. Equal samples \(X=Y=Z\) with identity bijections give all distances zero and sums \(N\le2N\): both lemmas hold trivially, and the transfer returns the identity. The exponent \(\alpha=\infty\) in both coordinates is not an admissible pair of the conjecture, but the first two lemmas hold for it, and the cap excludes it: it would bound the maximum distance by \(2L'\sqrt{\log N}/\sqrt N\), below the Leighton–Shor order. For \(\alpha<1\) the midpoint convexity of \(t\mapsto t^\alpha\) fails (\(((a+b)/2)^\alpha>(a^\alpha+b^\alpha)/2\) for \(a\ne b\)), which is why the proof uses subadditivity and the larger factor \(2^{1/\alpha}\) there; part (b) of the consequences needs \(\alpha_j\ge1\) and states it.

Nonvacuity. Theorem 17.1.3 of [T4] supplies, for i.i.d. uniform \(X\) and evenly spread \(Y\) and every \(0<\alpha<1/2\), matchings that are \((K(\alpha),\alpha)\)-good in coordinate one and \((K(\alpha),\infty)\)-good in coordinate two with probability at least \(1-LN^{-10}\), so the hypothesis of the transfer's part (2) holds at the pair \((\alpha,\infty)\) with \(\varepsilon_N=LN^{-10}\) at every \(N\ge2\). For the consequences, the witness \(X=Y\) above meets every hypothesis at every pair. For the cap, the excluded class is nonempty: every pair in \((4,\infty]^2\), for instance \((5,5)\) or \((\infty,\infty)\), and its two cited theorems hold at every \(N\ge2\).

New mathematics. The transfer lemma, an elementary composition argument, shows the monograph's form of the conjecture implies the survey's two-sample form, with constant \(2L\) at every admissible pair. The consequences lemma records with constants the deductions both sources state. The common exponent cap proves, from the stated Leighton–Shor upper and lower bounds, that no pair with both exponents above \(4\) holds in the monograph's setting, so the conjecture cannot be strengthened on its diagonal there; off the diagonal the exponent sum \(1/2\) has no recorded lower-bound argument. The four ambiguities: the constant is universal, the endpoint is the maximum bound, and the probability tends to one with no rate (all settled); the two sources state different discrepancy classes, and only the monograph's is attributed a route to the special case (recorded, not settled).

Goal progress. Unchanged. No implication toward a matching at any admissible pair was obtained: the transfer moves a statement between two settings and the consequences derive weaker statements from the conjecture. The cap shows the conjecture cannot be strengthened on its diagonal in the monograph's setting, which leaves the implication itself untouched. The entry fixes a statement the thread may prove: the monograph's form with a universal \(L\) suffices, through the transfer, for the survey's.

Record search. tools/search-claims.py for "matching theorem Ajtai Komlos Tusnady", "ultimate matching conjecture Talagrand" and "evenly spread points transportation exponential" returned no record on random matchings, transportation costs or discrepancy of empirical measures; the registry's matches concern pigeonhole and polynomial-calculus objects. tools/claim_resemblance.py on the claim spec found no registered claim scoring above 0.23; the nearest are counting and Tseitin-degree lemmas unrelated to matchings.

Its open-point answer, a reverse-Markov argument, became the common exponent cap. Follow-up pass by the same reviewer on the cap and the corrections: pass, with wording changes made (any evenly spread \(Y\), the name \(Z\) in the transfer, "cannot be strengthened" for "sharp"). The empty-box remark, which the reviewer gave unverified, was rechecked by the author and is labelled heuristic. Process audit. The reviewer proposed the title "Conjecture statement fixed; diagonal exponent cap" and found that the body supports reading, two elementary lemmas and the cap, but not "sharp" or a necessity claim without "monograph's setting, diagonal only"; the title below carries that qualifier. It found the probability verdict reached from one source while the other, already excerpted, settled it; unasked questions about [C5] and the survey's footnote, now recorded; and a reading task scheduled for a question the cited statements answer in a few lines, now proved as the cap.

Prevention. No committed check can judge whether a verdict used every source read. The existing reviewer brief, which checks each attributed statement against excerpts of every source, caught it, so no new rule is added. Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • The factor \(2^{1/\alpha}\) of the transfer for \(\alpha<1\) can probably be lowered to about \((\log3/\log2)^{1/\alpha}\), via \(\max(e^u,e^v)\le e^u+e^v-1\) and Hölder's inequality. Immaterial for the conjecture, where \(\kappa=2\). Record. tools/search-claims.py "Holder exponential moment constant composition" returned no matching or transport record.

Exponential matchings reduced to a conjugate-penalty discrepancy conjecture

16 October 2026 · Working proof of the lemma; conditional reduction, endpoints included; conjecture stated · Hopf–Lax reduction. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Chosen. This entry develops the queued check entry-2026-10-16-monograph-statements:s3 rather than the head, s1. Both bear on the open statement ultimate-matching. The head asks whether some pairs below the curve are excluded off the diagonal, a necessity question; s3 asks for a mechanism that would give pairs on the curve, which is the route's highest-risk item (side-ultimate-matching) and the missing implication itself. The head is not skipped; it stays at the head of the queue.

Owed attempt. This item has no earlier development; none was named.

Question. The missing implication for the goal is a construction, for each admissible pair \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\), of a matching with both exponential moments bounded. [T4] proves the special case of Shor's theorem by reducing the matching to a discrepancy bound through linear-programming duality (Proposition 4.3.2) and a regularity step (Proposition 17.2.4, proved in Section B.6, read in this cycle). The question is whether that reduction extends to exponential costs, and to which class of functions. The test ends with a proved reduction or with the step where it fails.

From the regularity step to a semigroup. Lemmas B.6.1 and B.6.2 of [T4] bound the variation of the inf-convolution \(h(k,\ell)=\inf\{u(r,s)+|k-r|:|\ell-s|\le1\}\) by \(\sum n(\tau)(u(\tau)-h(\tau))\), using that the horizontal cost is linear and the vertical cost is \(0\) within distance \(1\) and infinite beyond. For a convex cost \(\Psi\) the corresponding quantity is the conjugate: if \(u(x)=Mx\) on the line, then \(u(x)-\inf_r[u(r)+\Psi(|x-r|)]=\sup_d(Md-\Psi(|d|))=\Psi^*(M)\) for \(M\ge0\). The lemma below replaces the discrete regularity estimate by a Hopf–Lax interpolation, in which this conjugate appears as the penalty \(C^*(\nabla g)\).

General statement. lem:hopf-lax-matching-bound: for every separable convex finite cost vanishing at \(0\) and every two \(N\)-point configurations in the unit square whose cell counts on some square grid are all at least \(\lambda\) times the cell area, the minimal matching cost is at most the supremum over Lipschitz \(g\) of the cell-averaged discrepancy of \(g\) minus \((1-2h)\lambda\int C^*(\nabla g)\), plus a cell-size error \(2hN\sum_j\Psi_j(\delta/h)\).

Hopf–Lax matching bound

Setting. \(\Omega=[0,1]^2\). \(\Psi_1,\Psi_2:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)\) are convex, nondecreasing, finite, with \(\Psi_j(0)=0\). \(C(z)=\Psi_1(|z_1|)+\Psi_2(|z_2|)\) for \(z\in\mathbb R^2\); \(C\) is convex, finite, \(C\ge0\), \(C(0)=0\). Its conjugate \(C^*(\xi)=\sup_q(q\cdot\xi-C(q))=\Psi_1^*(|\xi_1|)+\Psi_2^*(|\xi_2|)\), with \(\Psi^*(x)=\sup_{t\ge0}(xt-\Psi(t))\), is convex with values in \([0,\infty]\). \(\mathcal Q\) is the partition of \(\Omega\) into \(k^2\) squares of side \(\delta=1/k\), each half-open \([\cdot,\cdot)\times[\cdot,\cdot)\) except that the squares of the last row and column contain their top or right edges. Points \(X_1,\dots,X_N\) and \(Y_1,\dots,Y_N\) lie in \(\Omega\); \(a(Q)\) and \(b(Q)\) count the \(X_i\) and the \(Y_j\) in \(Q\); \(\mu_X\) and \(\mu_Y\) are the measures with density \(a(Q)/|Q|\) and \(b(Q)/|Q|\) on each \(Q\). Assume \(a(Q),b(Q)\ge\lambda|Q|\) for every \(Q\), with \(\lambda>0\).

Statement. For every \(h\in(0,1/2)\), \[\min_\pi\sum_{i\le N}C(X_i-Y_{\pi(i)})\le\sup_g\Big[\int_\Omega g\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)-(1-2h)\lambda\int_\Omega C^*(\nabla g)\,dx\Big]+2hN\big(\Psi_1(\delta/h)+\Psi_2(\delta/h)\big),\] the supremum over Lipschitz functions \(g\) on \(\Omega\) (\(\nabla g\) exists almost everywhere; a term \(\int C^*(\nabla g)=\infty\) counts as \(-\infty\)).

Proof. Duality. By Proposition 4.3.2 of [T4] with \(c_{ij}=C(X_i-Y_j)\), the minimum equals \(\sup\sum_i(w_i+w'_i)\) over families with \(w_i+w'_j\le C(X_i-Y_j)\). Fix such a family and put \(f(y)=\max_i[w_i-C(X_i-y)]\) for \(y\in\Omega\); \(f\) is Lipschitz on \(\Omega\), \(C\) being convex and finite, hence Lipschitz on the compact \([-1,1]^2\). Then \(w'_j\le\min_i[C(X_i-Y_j)-w_i]=-f(Y_j)\).

Hopf–Lax functions. For \(t>0\) and \(x\in\mathbb R^2\) set \(Q_t(x)=\min_{y\in\Omega}[f(y)+tC((x-y)/t)]\), a minimum of a continuous function on a compact set. (i) \(Q_1(X_i)\ge w_i\), since \(f(y)\ge w_i-C(X_i-y)\). (ii) For \(t,s>0\), \(x,q\in\mathbb R^2\): \(Q_{t+s}(x+sq)\le Q_t(x)+sC(q)\). Indeed, if \(y\) attains \(Q_t(x)\), then \(\frac{x+sq-y}{t+s}=\frac t{t+s}\cdot\frac{x-y}t+\frac s{t+s}\,q\), and convexity gives \((t+s)C(\frac{x+sq-y}{t+s})\le tC(\frac{x-y}t)+sC(q)\). (iii) For \(y'\in\Omega\), \(Q_s(y')\le f(Y_j)+sC((y'-Y_j)/s)\), taking \(y=Y_j\). (iv) On \(U\times[h,1-h]\), with \(U=(-1,2)^2\supset\Omega\), \((x,t)\mapsto Q_t(x)\) is Lipschitz. In \(x\): for \(x\in U\), \(y\in\Omega\), \(t\ge h\), the point \((x-y)/t\) lies in \([-2/h,2/h]^2\), so each \(x\mapsto tC((x-y)/t)\) has the Lipschitz constant \(\Lambda\) of \(C\) on that square, and so has their minimum \(Q_t\). In \(t\): (ii) with \(q=0\) gives \(Q_{t+s}\le Q_t\). Conversely, if \(y\) attains \(Q_{t+s}(x)\) and \(z=x-y\), put \(x'=y+\frac t{t+s}z\), a convex combination of \(x\in U\) and \(y\in\Omega\subset U\), so \(x'\in U\); then \(Q_{t+s}(x)=f(y)+tC(\frac z{t+s})+sC(\frac z{t+s})\ge Q_t(x')\ge Q_t(x)-\Lambda|x-x'|\ge Q_t(x)-3\Lambda s/h\), using \(C\ge0\), \((x'-y)/t=z/(t+s)\) and \(|x-x'|=\frac s{t+s}|z|\le3s/h\).

Hamilton–Jacobi inequality. By Rademacher's theorem, \(Q\) is differentiable at almost every \((x,t)\in\operatorname{int}\Omega\times(h,1-h)\). At such a point, (ii) with \(s\downarrow0\) gives \(\partial_tQ+q\cdot\nabla_xQ\le C(q)\) for every \(q\), so \(C^*(\nabla_xQ)\le-\partial_tQ\); in particular \(C^*(\nabla_xQ)\) is finite there.

Interpolation. Let \(\rho_t=\frac{(1-h-t)\mu_Y+(t-h)\mu_X}{1-2h}\) for \(t\in[h,1-h]\), with density at least \(\lambda\) on \(\Omega\), and \(F(t)=\int_\Omega Q_t\,d\rho_t\). \(F\) is Lipschitz, and for almost every \(t\), by Fubini \(Q\) is differentiable at \((x,t)\) for almost every \(x\), so dominated convergence (difference quotients in \(t\) bounded by \(3\Lambda/h\)) gives \(F'(t)=\int\partial_tQ_t\,d\rho_t+\frac1{1-2h}\int Q_t\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\le-\lambda\int_\Omega C^*(\nabla Q_t)\,dx+\frac1{1-2h}\int Q_t\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\), using \(C^*\ge0\). Integrating over \([h,1-h]\), an interval of length \(1-2h\), \(F(1-h)-F(h)\le\sup_g[\int g\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)-(1-2h)\lambda\int C^*(\nabla g)]\), each \(Q_t\) being Lipschitz on \(\Omega\).

Smearing. By (i) and (ii) with \(t=1-h\), \(s=h\), \(q=(X_i-x)/h\): \(w_i\le Q_1(X_i)\le Q_{1-h}(x)+hC((X_i-x)/h)\) for every \(x\); averaging over \(x\) in the cell of \(X_i\), whose points differ from \(X_i\) by at most \(\delta\) in each coordinate, and summing, \(\sum_iw_i\le\int Q_{1-h}\,d\mu_X+hN(\Psi_1(\delta/h)+\Psi_2(\delta/h))\). By (iii) with \(s=h\), \(-f(Y_j)\le-Q_h(y')+hC((y'-Y_j)/h)\); averaging over \(y'\) in the cell of \(Y_j\), \(\sum_jw'_j\le-\int Q_h\,d\mu_Y+hN(\Psi_1(\delta/h)+\Psi_2(\delta/h))\). The sum of the two main terms is \(F(1-h)-F(h)\). Taking the supremum over dual families gives the statement. \(\square\)

Scope. The lemma is deterministic and uses the points only through the cell counts. Superlinearity of the cost is not needed: with \(C(z)=|z_1|+|z_2|\) it gives a smeared Kantorovich–Rubinstein bound over functions with \(|\partial_jg|\le1\). Only this direction is proved: a matching of small cost need not give a small value of the right-hand side. The continuous form, for densities bounded below by \(\lambda\), appears to be, by the reviewer's unverified recollection, the dual of the flux bound for the linear interpolation of the two measures (Benamou–Brenier), with the semigroup proof of Bobkov, Gentil and Ledoux; neither source was read in this cycle. What is written here is that bound for point configurations, through cell smearing and the finite duality of [T4]; no novelty is claimed.

Exponential matching from discrepancy

Setting. For \(\alpha\in[1,\infty)\) put \(\psi_\alpha(t)=e^{t^\alpha}-1\), and for \(N\ge2\) put \(\psi_\infty(t)=N(t-1)_+\); each is convex, nondecreasing, finite and \(0\) at \(0\). Their conjugates \(\psi_\alpha^*(x)=\sup_{t\ge0}(xt-\psi_\alpha(t))\) include \(\psi_\infty^*(x)=x\) for \(0\le x\le N\) and \(+\infty\) for \(x>N\). For \(L>0\) put \(s=L\sqrt{\log N/N}\), \(k=\lceil32/s\rceil\), \(\delta=1/k\), and use the grid \(\mathcal Q\) of side \(\delta\). For points \(X,Y\) let \(\mu_X,\mu_Y\) be as above and define \[D_N=\sup\Big\{\int_\Omega G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y):G\text{ Lipschitz},\ \int_\Omega\big(\psi^*_{\alpha_1}(|\partial_1G|)+\psi^*_{\alpha_2}(|\partial_2G|)\big)dx\le2\Big\}.\]

Statement. Let \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in[1,\infty]\), \(L\ge132\), \(X\) i.i.d. uniform on \(\Omega\) and \(Y\) evenly spread, and suppose \(P(D_N\le\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N})\to1\). Then with probability tending to one there is a matching \(\pi\) that, for \(j=1,2\), satisfies \(\sum_{i\le N}\exp[(\frac{\sqrt N}{L\sqrt{\log N}}|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|)^{\alpha_j}]\le2N\) when \(\alpha_j<\infty\) and \(\max_i|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\le2L\sqrt{\log N/N}\) when \(\alpha_j=\infty\). By part (1) of lem:two-sample-matching-transfer, applied in each coordinate to the same composed matching and with the union bound, the same follows for two i.i.d. samples with each coordinate's constant doubled.

Proof. Apply the bound with \(\Psi_j(t)=\psi_{\alpha_j}(t/s)\) and \(h=1/4\). Then \(C^*(\xi)=\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}(s|\xi_j|)\), since \(\Psi_j^*(x)=\psi_{\alpha_j}^*(sx)\).

Cell counts. \(\delta\le s/32\) and, for large \(N\), \(\delta\ge s/33\), so \(N\delta^2\ge L^2\log N/1089\ge16\log N\). Each \(a(Q)\) is binomial with mean \(N\delta^2\), so by the multiplicative Chernoff bound \(P(a(Q)<N\delta^2/2)\le e^{-N\delta^2/8}\le N^{-2}\); there are \(k^2\le N\) cells for large \(N\), so all \(a(Q)\ge N\delta^2/2\) with probability at least \(1-1/N\). For evenly spread \(Y\), let \(Q'\) be \(Q\) shrunk by \(21/\sqrt N\) on each side; the rectangles of the definition that meet \(Q'\) have diameter at most \(20/\sqrt N\), so they lie in the interior of \(Q\) with their points \(Y_j\), and they cover \(Q'\). Hence \(b(Q)\ge N(\delta-42/\sqrt N)^2\ge N\delta^2/2\) for large \(N\), since \(\delta\sqrt N\to\infty\). So \(\lambda=N/2\) and \((1-2h)\lambda=N/4\).

Error. \(\delta/h=4\delta\le s/8\), so \(\Psi_j(4\delta)=\psi_{\alpha_j}(4\delta/s)\le\psi_{\alpha_j}(1/8)\), which is \(0\) for \(\alpha_j=\infty\) and at most \(e^{1/8}-1<0.14\) otherwise; the error is at most \(2\cdot\frac14\cdot N\cdot0.28=0.14N\).

Main term. For Lipschitz \(g\) put \(G=sg\), so \(\int C^*(\nabla g)=\int\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}(|\partial_jG|)=:I(G)\) and \(\int g\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)=\frac1s\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\). On the event \(D_N\le\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N}=\frac{sN}2\), every \(G\) with \(I(G)\le2\) has \(\frac1s\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\le N/2\). If \(2<I(G)<\infty\), then \(G'=(2/I(G))G\) has \(I(G')\le\frac2{I(G)}I(G)=2\) by convexity of \(\psi^*\) with \(\psi^*(0)=0\), so \(\frac1s\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\le\frac N4I(G)\). In all cases \(\frac1s\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)-\frac N4I(G)\le N/2\).

Conclusion. With probability tending to one, the minimal cost is at most \(N/2+0.14N<N\), so some \(\pi\) has \(\sum_i\sum_j\psi_{\alpha_j}(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|/s)<N\). For finite \(\alpha_j\), \(\sum_i\exp[(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|/s)^{\alpha_j}]=N+\sum_i\psi_{\alpha_j}(\cdot)<2N\), and \(|\cdot|/s=\frac{\sqrt N}{L\sqrt{\log N}}|\cdot|\). For \(\alpha_j=\infty\), each term satisfies \(N(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|/s-1)_+<N\), so \(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|<2s\). \(\square\)

The reduced statement. conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy: there is \(L\ge132\) such that for every pair \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in[2,\infty]\) with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\), \(P(D_N\le\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N})\to1\), \(D_N\) being defined with that \(L\) (which fixes the grid). By the corollary it implies the ultimate matching conjecture at every admissible pair with one universal constant (\(L\) for finite exponents, \(2L\) for the maximum bound; doubled again for two samples). A version with \(L\) depending on the pair would give only the pair-dependent form. At the endpoint \((2,\infty)\) the class is \(\int\psi_2^*(|\partial_1G|)+|\partial_2G|\le2\) with \(|\partial_2G|\le N\), a continuous counterpart of the class of Research Problem 17.2.2 of [T4] (weights \(x(\log(3+x))^{1/2}\) and \(x\)). Here the step from this class to the matching is complete, whereas [T4] says a positive answer to its problem would still need "significant extra work". The two statements differ: continuous and cell-smeared here, grid-based there; a bound in probability here, an expectation there; and the weights agree only up to constants. It is stated as a conjecture to be tested, not believed.

Comparison with weights at least linear. Taking \(t=1\) in the conjugate gives \(\psi_\alpha^*(x)\ge x-(e-1)\) for every \(\alpha\in[1,\infty)\); for \(\alpha=\infty\), \(\psi^*_\infty(x)\ge x\). Put \(\theta_\alpha=\max(\psi_\alpha^*,x)\), convex and \(0\) at \(0\). Then \(\psi^*_\alpha\le\theta_\alpha\le\psi^*_\alpha+e-1\), so on the unit square \(\{\int\sum_j\theta_{\alpha_j}\le2\}\subset\{\int\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}\le2\}\subset\{\int\sum_j\theta_{\alpha_j}\le2e\}\), and by the homogeneity step the last class is contained in \(e\) times the first. So replacing \(\psi^*\) by the weight \(\theta\ge x\), as [T4]'s Research Problem 17.2.2 requires of its weights, changes \(D_N\) by at most the factor \(e\), uniformly in the pair. For large \(x\), \(\psi^*_\alpha(x)\ge x(\frac12\log x)^{1/\alpha}-\sqrt x\) (take \(t=(\frac12\log x)^{1/\alpha}\)) and \(\psi_\alpha^*(x)\le x\max(1,(\log(1+x))^{1/\alpha})\) (for \(t>T=\max(1,(\log(1+x))^{1/\alpha})\), \(\frac d{dt}(xt-\psi_\alpha(t))=x-\alpha t^{\alpha-1}e^{t^\alpha}<x-(1+x)<0\)); at \(\alpha=2\) this is the weight \(x(\log(3+x))^{1/2}\) of Research Problem 17.2.2 up to a factor between about \(2^{-1/2}\) and \(1\) for large \(x\).

Degenerate cases. \(X=Y\): the minimal cost is \(0\), and the right-hand side is at least \(0\) (take \(g=0\)), so the bound holds. A cell with no point forces \(\lambda=0\), which the hypothesis \(\lambda>0\) excludes; with \(\lambda=0\) the supremum may be infinite and the bound empty. \(h\to0\) removes the error but the Lipschitz constant \(\Lambda\) in (iv) blows up; the proof needs \(h>0\) only there. An infinite cost is excluded (\(C\) must be finite for the duality and for \(\Lambda\)); the maximum constraint enters the corollary only through the finite cost \(\psi_\infty\), whose slope \(N\) makes a violation of the bound \(2s\) cost more than the total. \(N=1\) is excluded by \(\log N>0\).

Nonvacuity. For the bound: \(N=n^2\) points \(X_i=Y_i\) at the centres of the \(n\times n\) subsquares, cells of side \(\delta=m/n\) for \(m\mid n\), so \(a(Q)=b(Q)=m^2=N|Q|\) and \(\lambda=N\); any \(\Psi_j=\psi_{\alpha_j}\). For the reduction: at every \(N\ge2\) and \(L\ge132\) the class defining \(D_N\) contains every \(G\) with \(|\partial_jG|\le1\) (from the upper bound above, \(\psi^*_\alpha(x)\le1\) for \(x\le1\)), and the cell-count event has probability at least \(1-1/N\) for large \(N\). No instance of its hypothesis on \(D_N\) is known: that hypothesis is the open conjecture, and the corollary is a conditional result.

New mathematics. The Hopf–Lax matching bound, a point-configuration and cell-smeared form of what the reviewer recalls as a standard dynamic duality bound, turns any separable convex finite matching cost into a supremum of discrepancies penalized by the conjugate cost; its proof uses the finite duality of [T4], a semigroup inequality proved here, Rademacher's theorem and Fubini's theorem. Its corollary reduces the ultimate matching conjecture, in the evenly-spread and the two-sample forms, at every admissible pair including the endpoints, with one universal constant, to a single discrepancy bound of Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády size for the class with gradient penalty \(\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}\); up to the factor \(e\), the penalty may be taken at least linear, as in [T4]'s Research Problem 17.2.2.

Goal progress. Advanced, by a conditional reduction: the necessary implication for ultimate-matching changed from the existence of a matching to the discrepancy statement conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy, by cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction. The reduction is one-directional, and nothing indicates that the reduced statement is easier: it is the exponential analogue of Research Problem 17.2.2, which [T4] leaves open at \((2,\infty)\), and the reviewer's heuristic (below) suggests that Leighton–Shor functions meet it exactly at its threshold on the whole curve. Its merit is that the reduction is complete, with explicit constants and no further matching-theoretic work.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • Leighton–Shor test, heuristic: a smoothed Leighton–Shor set of boundary length about \(1\), tiled with aspect ratio \((\log N)^{1/\alpha_1-1/\alpha_2}\) (corrected by the reviewer in its follow-up), would give a discrepancy about \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4-(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2)/2}\) under the penalty, so exactly \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{1/2}\) on the curve and more below it; the finest Leighton–Shor scale in the compressed direction is of the order of the grid side, so the outcome turns on constants. Scaled Leighton–Shor lower bound for the penalized class

16 October 2026 · Working proof; endpoints of the curve not covered · Leighton–Shor class test. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Chosen. This entry continues the queued check entry-2026-10-16-monograph-statements:s3, whose previous development, the Hopf–Lax reduction (read before planning), produced conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy, rather than the head s1. The open statement is ultimate-matching through its sufficient statement conjugate-class-discrepancy; a reduced statement that is false would make the reduction useless, so testing it against the known extremal construction comes before any attempt to prove it. The head s1 asks the analogous question for matchings and stays at the head.

Owed attempt. The previous entry named: "redo the Leighton–Shor lower bound of Section 4.8 of [T4] for the penalized class in an anisotropic tile, with explicit scale bookkeeping and the finest scale compared to the grid side". Made, with one change: the anisotropy is obtained by scaling the Leighton–Shor function vertically by a factor \(\kappa\) rather than by tiling, which keeps the monograph's probabilistic argument in one piece. Outcome below.

Question. Does the Leighton–Shor construction violate conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy on the curve \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\)? If it did, the reduced statement would be false and the reduction useless; if it reaches the bound's order exactly, the order \(\sqrt{N\log N}\) is necessary and an upper-bound proof must be sharp there.

Sources read. Section 4.8 of [T4] in full (Theorem 4.8.1, Lemmas 4.8.2–4.8.3, the proof of (4.119) and the counting bound (4.120)), the definitions (4.81)–(4.82) of the tent functions and Lemma 4.6.2 ((4.83)–(4.88)).

General statement. lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound: for every exponent pair with \(|1/\alpha_1-1/\alpha_2|<1/2\) and every \(L\ge132\), with probability tending to one the penalized discrepancy \(D_N\) of the reduction is at least a universal constant times \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4-(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2)/2}\).

Leighton–Shor class lower bound

Setting. As in cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction: \(X\) i.i.d. uniform on \(\Omega=[0,1]^2\), \(Y\) evenly spread, \(L\ge132\), \(s=L\sqrt{\log N/N}\), grid side \(\delta=1/\lceil32/s\rceil\), \(\psi_\alpha\) and \(\psi^*_\alpha\) for \(\alpha\in[1,\infty]\), and \(D_N=\sup\{\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y):\int_\Omega\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}(|\partial_jG|)\le2\}\). Write \(\beta_j=1/\alpha_j\in[0,1]\).

Statement. There is a universal constant \(L_1\) such that for all \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in[1,\infty]\) with \(|\beta_1-\beta_2|<1/2\) and all \(L\ge132\), with probability tending to one as \(N\to\infty\), \[D_N\ge\frac{\sqrt N\,(\log N)^{3/4-(\beta_1+\beta_2)/2}}{4L_1}.\] Consequently: (a) for every pair below the curve (\(\beta_1+\beta_2<1/2\), which forces \(|\beta_1-\beta_2|<1/2\)), \(D_N/\sqrt{N\log N}\to\infty\) in probability, so the bound \(\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N}\) fails for every \(L\); (b) for every pair on the curve other than the endpoints \((2,\infty)\) and \((\infty,2)\), \(D_N\ge\sqrt{N\log N}/(4L_1)\) with probability tending to one, so the order \(\sqrt{N\log N}\) in conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy cannot be lowered there. The same order may already follow, at every pair, from the Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády lower bound for the functions with \(|\partial_jG|\le1\), which the class contains, if that bound survives the cell smearing; this baseline was not checked, so (b) is not claimed as the lemma's specific contribution.

Proof. By the symmetry of the setting under exchanging the coordinates (the evenly spread definition, the uniform law and the grid are symmetric), assume \(\beta_1\ge\beta_2\). Put \(\kappa=(\log N)^{-(\beta_1-\beta_2)}\in((\log N)^{-1/2},1]\).

Step 1: a scaled Leighton–Shor excess. We claim: with probability tending to one there is a function \(f:[0,1]\to[0,1]\), piecewise linear, with \(f(0)=f(1)=0\), \(\int_0^1f'^2\le\kappa^2\), \(|f'|\le\kappa\sqrt r\) with \(r\le\log N\), and \[\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in S(f)\}\ge N\lambda(S(f))+\sqrt{\kappa N}(\log N)^{3/4}/L_1,\qquad S(f)=\{(x,y)\in\Omega:y\le f(x)\}.\] This is (4.119) of [T4] with every tent multiplied by \(\kappa\), and its proof is the monograph's with that factor carried. Take \(f=\kappa\sum_{k\le r}f_k\), \(f_k=\sum_{\ell\le2^{ck}}x_{k,\ell}\tilde f_{k,\ell}\), \(\tilde f_{k,\ell}=f_{ck,\ell}/\sqrt r\), \(x_{k,\ell}\in\{0,1\}\), with \(r\) the largest integer such that \(2^{cr}\le N^{1/100}\). By (4.83)–(4.84), \(\int f'^2=\kappa^2\sum x_{k,\ell}2^{-ck}/r\le\kappa^2\); \(f\ge0\), \(f(0)=f(1)=0\); by (4.88) each \(\tilde f_{k,\ell}\) has slope \(\pm1/\sqrt r\) on disjoint supports within a level, so \(|f'|\le\kappa r/\sqrt r=\kappa\sqrt r\), and \(f\le\kappa\sum_k2^{-ck-1}/\sqrt r\le1\). In the inductive step the regions \(R_\ell\) now have \(\lambda(R_\ell)=\kappa2^{-2c(q+1)}/(4\sqrt r)\) (by (4.121) scaled), the favorable threshold becomes \(\sqrt{N\lambda(R_\ell)}/L^*=\sqrt\kappa\,2^{-c(q+1)}\sqrt N/(2r^{1/4}L^*)\), and (4.128) still applies because \(N\lambda(R_\ell)\ge\kappa N^{49/50}/(4\sqrt{\log N})\to\infty\), using \(\kappa\ge(\log N)^{-1/2}\). The counting of possible \(g\), the Poissonization and the choice of \(c\) do not involve \(\kappa\). [T4]'s bound on the failure probability at level \(q\) is of order \(2^{-2^{cq+1}}\), which is not small at the lowest levels, so it gives "overwhelming" probability rather than probability tending to one. Every chosen region has \(\delta_\ell\ge0\), so each level contributes a nonnegative excess whether or not its favorable count succeeds; we use the favorable count only at the levels \(r/2\le q<r\), whose total failure probability is at most \(\sum_{q\ge r/2}2^{-2^{cq+1}}\to0\) (with the Poissonization error at those levels, which also tends to zero as \(N\lambda(R_\ell)\to\infty\)). So with probability tending to one the excess is at least \(\frac r2\cdot\sqrt{\kappa N}/(16r^{1/4}L^*)=\sqrt{\kappa N}r^{3/4}/(32L^*)\); with \(r\ge\log N/(L'c)\) this is the claim for a universal \(L_1\). (Repair proposed by the reviewer and checked here.)

Step 2: the test function. Let \(w=\delta\), \(A=(\log N)^{-\beta_2}/2\) and \(G(x,y)=A\min\{1,\max\{0,(f(x)+w-y)/w\}\}\). \(G\) is Lipschitz, \(G=A\) on \(S(f)\), \(G=0\) where \(y\ge f(x)+w\), and on the band \(B=\{f(x)<y<f(x)+w\}\cap\Omega\), of area at most \(w\), \(\partial_2G=-A/w\) and \(\partial_1G=Af'(x)/w\); elsewhere \(\nabla G=0\) almost everywhere. Put \(M=A/w\); for large \(N\), \(M\le N\) and \(M\kappa\sqrt r\le N\).

Step 3: the penalty. From the previous entry, \(\psi^*_\alpha(x)\le x\max(1,(\log(1+x))^{1/\alpha})\) for \(\alpha<\infty\), and \(\psi^*_\infty(x)=x\) for \(x\le N\). Since \(\max(1,(\log(1+x))^{\beta})\le(2\log N)^\beta\le2(\log N)^\beta\) for \(x\le N\), \(N\ge2\), \(\log N\ge1\) and \(\beta\le1\), \[\int\sum_j\psi^*_{\alpha_j}(|\partial_jG|)\le w\,\psi^*_{\alpha_2}(M)+w\int_0^1\psi^*_{\alpha_1}(M|f'(x)|)dx\le2A(\log N)^{\beta_2}+2A(\log N)^{\beta_1}\int_0^1|f'|\le2A\big((\log N)^{\beta_2}+\kappa(\log N)^{\beta_1}\big)=4A(\log N)^{\beta_2}=2,\] using \(\int|f'|\le(\int f'^2)^{1/2}\le\kappa\).

Step 4: the discrepancy. With probability tending to one every cell has \(a(Q)\le2N\delta^2\) (upper Chernoff tail \(P(a(Q)\ge2N\delta^2)\le e^{-N\delta^2/3}\le N^{-5}\), \(N\delta^2\ge16\log N\), and at most \(N\) cells). The graph of \(f\) has length at most \(\int_0^1(1+|f'|)\le2\), so it meets at most \(12/\delta\) cells (cover it by at most \(2/\delta+1\) arcs of length at most \(\delta\), each meeting at most four cells). A cell meeting \(S(f)\) but not contained in it meets the graph. Hence \[\int G\,d\mu_X\ge A\sum_{Q\subset S(f)}a(Q)\ge A\big(\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in S(f)\}-24N\delta\big).\] The set \(\{G>0\}\) lies in the vertical, hence Euclidean, \(w\)-neighbourhood of \(S(f)\), so every cell meeting it lies in the \(\varepsilon\)-neighbourhood \(S(f)_\varepsilon\), \(\varepsilon=w+\sqrt2\delta\le3\delta\), and \(\int G\,d\mu_Y\le A\operatorname{card}\{j:Y_j\in S(f)_\varepsilon\}\le A(N\lambda(S(f))+L_2N\varepsilon+L_2\sqrt N)\) by (4.120) of [T4], which holds for every \(f\) in its class \(\mathcal C\) of (4.117), and \(f\in\mathcal C\) since \(\int f'^2\le\kappa^2\le1\). So \[\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\ge A\Big(\frac{\sqrt{\kappa N}(\log N)^{3/4}}{L_1}-(24+3L_2)N\delta-L_2\sqrt N\Big).\] Here \(N\delta\le Ns/32=\frac L{32}\sqrt{N\log N}\), while \(\sqrt{\kappa N}(\log N)^{3/4}=\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4-(\beta_1-\beta_2)/2}\), whose exponent exceeds \(1/2\) because \(\beta_1-\beta_2<1/2\). So for \(N\ge N_0(L,\beta_1-\beta_2)\) the bracket is at least half its first term, and \(D_N\ge\frac{(\log N)^{-\beta_2}}4\cdot\frac{\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4-(\beta_1-\beta_2)/2}}{L_1}=\frac{\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4-(\beta_1+\beta_2)/2}}{4L_1}\). (a) and (b) follow from the exponent: \(3/4-(\beta_1+\beta_2)/2>1/2\) below the curve, \(=1/2\) on it. \(\square\)

Scope. The constant \(L_1\) is universal; the threshold \(N_0\) depends on \(L\) and on \(\beta_1-\beta_2\) and blows up as \(|\beta_1-\beta_2|\to1/2\), so this proof does not cover the endpoints \((2,\infty)\), \((\infty,2)\): there its crude error terms (the X-mass dropped in cells meeting the graph and the term \(L_2N\varepsilon\) on the Y side) are of the order of the scaled excess. That is a limit of these bounds, not evidence that the lower bound fails there; the reviewer's suggestion below would remove it. The lemma concerns the discrepancy class, not matchings: without a converse of the Hopf–Lax bound it says nothing about whether the matching conjecture holds below the curve. On the curve it gives a lower bound of the conjectured order with a universal constant, which does not contradict the conjecture's \(\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N}\) for any \(L\ge132\).

Degenerate cases. \(\beta_1=\beta_2\) gives \(\kappa=1\) and the unscaled construction of [T4]; at \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2=4\) the lower bound is \(\sqrt{N\log N}/(4L_1)\), at the conjecture's order. \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2=\infty\) (\(\beta=0\)) gives \(D_N\gtrsim\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4}\), the Leighton–Shor order for the linear weight, consistent with the survey's remark (Section 5) that for \(\theta_1=\theta_2=x\) its Gaussian bound cannot be better than \(Lp^{3/4}2^{2p}\). \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2=1\) (\(\beta=1\)) gives only \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{-1/4}\), below the conjectured order, so the lemma gives no information there. The endpoint \(|\beta_1-\beta_2|=1/2\) is excluded.

Nonvacuity. The pair \((4,4)\) satisfies \(|\beta_1-\beta_2|=0<1/2\), and every \(L\ge132\) is allowed; then \(\kappa=1\), \(A=(\log N)^{-1/4}/2\), and the conclusion holds for all large \(N\). Below the curve, \((5,5)\) or \((6,12)\) (\(\beta_1+\beta_2=1/4\)) are instances of (a).

New mathematics. The Leighton–Shor lower bound, scaled vertically to balance the two penalty directions and smoothed at the grid scale, gives a lower bound \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4-(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2)/2}\) for the penalized discrepancy class of the reduction, whenever \(|1/\alpha_1-1/\alpha_2|<1/2\). So the class bound fails at every pair below the curve, where the reduced conjecture makes no claim, and the construction does not refute the reduced conjecture on the curve, where it reaches the conjectured order exactly. This confirms, with a proof and away from the endpoints, the heuristic recorded under the previous entry's reviewer's suggestions.

Goal progress. Unchanged. No implication toward a matching changed: the lemma neither proves nor refutes conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy on the curve. It removes one way the reduction could have been useless (a refutation by the known extremal functions) and shows that, if the reduced statement holds, its order is attained at every interior pair of the curve; whether a simpler Lipschitz baseline already shows this is unchecked. Below the curve it is the class analogue of lem:common-exponent-cap, extended off the diagonal, which the matching cap is not.

Verification gate. Dependencies read: Section 4.8 of [T4] in full, (4.81)–(4.88), and the previous entry's definition of \(D_N\) and its conjugate bounds. One fresh-context medium reviewer received the draft, the claim spec and those excerpts. It checked Steps 1–4 (the scaled regions and threshold, \(N\lambda(R_\ell)\to\infty\), \(f\in\mathcal C\), the band gradient and the penalty ending at exactly \(2\) including \(\alpha=\infty\), the cell counts and (4.120), the final constant), the optimality of \(\kappa\) within the family, and the symmetry reduction. Required changes made: [T4]'s argument gives overwhelming, not tending-to-one, probability at low levels, repaired by using only the levels \(q\ge r/2\) (its repair, checked here; the excess constant halves); consequence (b) was not compared with the Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády baseline on the Lipschitz subclass, now stated as unchecked and (b) not claimed as specific; the endpoint exclusion is a limit of the crude error terms, not of the order; "the reduced conjecture is false below the curve" corrected (it makes no claim there); an unread attribution to [T4] replaced by the survey's remark, read in the first entry; "sharp" removed. No follow-up pass was made: the corrections weaken or qualify statements and replace one proof step by the reviewer's own repair. Process audit. The reviewer proposed the title "Scaled Leighton–Shor lower bound for the penalized class", without "sharp" or "survives", and the title below follows it. It found the inherited probability claim unchecked at low levels, the missing comparison with the elementary baseline, the endpoint exclusion presented as intrinsic, one claim beyond the supplied sources, and that the outcome confirms the previous reviewer's heuristic, which the entry now says.

Process assessment. A probability statement was carried over from the source's "overwhelming probability" without checking its lowest levels, and a consequence was claimed without comparing it with the simplest function class that might already give it. Prevention. The reviewer brief already asks for the source's exact quantifiers; both were caught there. No mechanical check can compare a result with an unstated baseline, and no rule is added.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • Signed comparisons in Step 4 (the Y side through the perimeter of the union of interior cells and the evenly spread property, the X side by Bernstein's inequality and a union bound over the at most \(2^{2N^{1/100}}\) possible \(f\)) would make the error \(o(A\sqrt N)\) and extend the lower bound to every \(|\beta_1-\beta_2|\le1\), endpoints included, with \(\kappa\ge(\log N)^{-1}\). Record. tools/search-claims.py "signed comparison evenly spread perimeter Bernstein union bound" returned only lem:two-sample-matching-transfer, unrelated.
  • Upper bound, no mechanism found: by the coarea formula the penalty becomes a perimeter of level sets weighted by \((\log)^\beta\) of the local level-set density; the Leighton–Shor bound times total variation, with the Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády bound on bounded gradients, controls only gradients above \(N^\varepsilon\), and the missing ingredient is a scale-limited Leighton–Shor bound for nested level-set families. Record. tools/search-claims.py "coarea level sets perimeter weighted discrepancy" returned no matching record.
