Flow form of the Hopf–Lax bound; the dyadic flow gives square-exponential matchings

16 October 2026 · Working proofs; off-curve pair (2,2), coordinatewise · Dyadic flow bound. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Chosen. This entry continues the queued check entry-2026-10-16-monograph-statements:s3, whose latest development, the Leighton–Shor class test, left conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy unrefuted on the curve and named an upper-bound mechanism as the next attempt. Its first development, the Hopf–Lax reduction (read before planning), recorded: "The reduction is proved, including the endpoints and the comparison with weights at least linear". The head s1 (an off-diagonal cap for matchings) stays at the head; it concerns necessity, while the open statement ultimate-matching needs a construction.

Owed attempt. The latest follow-up named "an upper-bound mechanism for the class at one interior pair, starting from the monograph's decomposition into small-support Lipschitz classes (Proposition 17.3.1) and asking whether the conjugate penalty allows a single chaining over all scales instead of [T4]'s sum of suprema", and the living Proposed next step refined it to a coarea decomposition with a scale-limited Leighton–Shor bound. Set aside after a sketch, not answered. Section 4.7 of [T4] was read: its Leighton–Shor bound comes from Lemma 4.7.9, \(\gamma_2(T,\sqrt d)\le m^{3/4}\gamma_{1,2}(T,d)^{1/2}\) over \(m\) chaining levels. The sketch: split \(G\) into gradient bands and apply that bound on a mesh adapted to each band, hoping for the order \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4-\beta}\) with \(\beta=\max_j1/\alpha_j\) as in the previous entry, which would be \(\sqrt{N\log N}\) at \((4,4)\); this order was not computed. Two obstacles were observed, not proved to be failures: a linear multiscale splitting such as Haar projections is expected to have, for the smoothed indicator of a curve, total variation of the order of the full variation at every scale, losing a factor \(\log N\); and in two dimensions the gradient restricted to a band is in general not a gradient (its curl need not vanish). The single chaining under the conjugate penalty that the item named was not attempted. The entry turns instead to the primal side, which by convex (Beckmann-type) duality should carry the same information; that duality is recalled by the reviewer, unverified and not used below.

Question. Does the Hopf–Lax bound give a usable primal criterion, and what does the simplest flow, the dyadic one behind the Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády theorem, give through it? A known exponent pair reached through the reduction would test the method; the place where the dyadic flow stops would locate what the conjecture needs.

General statement. lem:flow-matching-bound: for every separable convex finite cost, every two \(N\)-point configurations and any smearings of them by probability measures supported within \(\ell^\infty\)-distance \(\delta_X\), \(\delta_Y\) of the points, with densities at least \(\lambda\) on the unit square, and every bounded vector field \(\Phi\) whose weak divergence carries one smeared measure to the other, the minimal matching cost is at most \((1-2h)\lambda\int C(\Phi/((1-2h)\lambda))\) plus the smearing error.

Flow matching bound

Setting. \(\Omega\), \(\Psi_j\), \(C\), \(C^*\) as in lem:hopf-lax-matching-bound. Points \(X_i,Y_j\in\Omega\), \(i,j\le N\). For each \(i\) a probability measure \(\nu_i\) on \(\Omega\) supported in \(\{x:|x-X_i|_\infty\le\delta_X\}\), and for each \(j\) a probability measure \(\nu'_j\) supported in \(\{y:|y-Y_j|_\infty\le\delta_Y\}\); \(\nu_X=\sum_i\nu_i\) and \(\nu_Y=\sum_j\nu'_j\) are absolutely continuous, with densities at least \(\lambda>0\) on \(\Omega\) (the proof differentiates \(\int Q_t\,d\rho_t\) using differentiability of \(Q\) at Lebesgue-almost every point). Fix \(h\in(0,1/2)\) and put \(\lambda'=(1-2h)\lambda\).

Statement. (1) \(\min_\pi\sum_iC(X_i-Y_{\pi(i)})\le\sup_g[\int g\,d(\nu_X-\nu_Y)-\lambda'\int_\Omega C^*(\nabla g)]+hN\sum_{k=1,2}(\Psi_k(\delta_X/h)+\Psi_k(\delta_Y/h))\), the supremum over Lipschitz \(g\) on \(\Omega\). (2) If \(\Phi:\Omega\to\mathbb R^2\) is bounded and measurable with \(\int_\Omega\nabla g\cdot\Phi\,dx=\int g\,d(\nu_X-\nu_Y)\) for every Lipschitz \(g\), then \(\min_\pi\sum_iC(X_i-Y_{\pi(i)})\le\lambda'\int_\Omega C(\Phi/\lambda')\,dx+hN\sum_{k=1,2}(\Psi_k(\delta_X/h)+\Psi_k(\delta_Y/h))\).

Proof. (1) is the proof of lem:hopf-lax-matching-bound with one change in the smearing step: averaging \(w_i\le Q_{1-h}(x)+hC((X_i-x)/h)\) against \(\nu_i\) instead of the uniform measure on the cell of \(X_i\), and \(-f(Y_j)\le-Q_h(y')+hC((y'-Y_j)/h)\) against \(\nu'_j\); every point of the support of \(\nu_i\) differs from \(X_i\) by at most \(\delta_X\) in each coordinate, so, \(\Psi_k\) being nondecreasing, the errors are \(hN\sum_k\Psi_k(\delta_X/h)\) and \(hN\sum_k\Psi_k(\delta_Y/h)\); the interpolation uses \(\rho_t\) between \(\nu_Y\) and \(\nu_X\), whose density is at least \(\lambda\). (2) For Lipschitz \(g\), by the identity and the Fenchel–Young inequality \(\xi\cdot q\le C^*(\xi)+C(q)\) at \(\xi=\nabla g(x)\), \(q=\Phi(x)/\lambda'\): \(\int g\,d(\nu_X-\nu_Y)=\lambda'\int\nabla g\cdot\frac\Phi{\lambda'}\le\lambda'\int C^*(\nabla g)+\lambda'\int C(\Phi/\lambda')\). So each term of the supremum in (1) is at most \(\lambda'\int C(\Phi/\lambda')\). \(\square\)

Dyadic square flow

Statement. Let \(S=[0,\rho]^2\) (up to translation) with children \(S_{a,b}\), \(a,b\in\{0,1\}\) (\(a\) the horizontal and \(b\) the vertical half), and let \(e_{a,b}\) be reals with \(\sum e_{a,b}=0\). There is a bounded field \(\Phi^S\) on \(S\), linear in \((e_{a,b})\), with \(|\Phi^S|\le6\max|e_{a,b}|/\rho\) and \(\int_S\nabla g\cdot\Phi^S=\sum_{a,b}\frac{e_{a,b}}{|S_{a,b}|}\int_{S_{a,b}}g\) for every Lipschitz \(g\) on \(S\); each coordinate of \(\Phi^S(x)\) is \(\sum_{a,b}\beta_{a,b}(x)e_{a,b}\) with \(|\beta_{a,b}(x)|\le2/\rho\).

Proof. Let \(E_b=e_{0,b}+e_{1,b}\), so \(E_0+E_1=0\). Vertical part: \(V(x,y)=(0,v(y))\) with \(v(y)=-\int_0^yr\), \(r=E_b/(\rho^2/2)\) on the half \(b\); \(v(0)=v(\rho)=0\), \(|v|\le|E_0|/\rho\). Horizontal part on each half \(b\): with \(d_{a,b}=e_{a,b}-E_b/2\) (\(d_{0,b}+d_{1,b}=0\)), \(H(x,y)=(k_b(x),0)\), \(k_b(x)=-\int_0^x\frac{d_{a(x'),b}}{\rho^2/4}dx'\); \(k_b(0)=k_b(\rho)=0\), \(|k_b|\le2|d_{0,b}|/\rho\). For Lipschitz \(g\), integrating by parts in \(y\) on each vertical segment and in \(x\) on each horizontal segment, with the boundary values zero, gives \(\int\nabla g\cdot(V+H)=\int g\,(r+\sum_{a,b}\frac{d_{a,b}}{\rho^2/4}1_{S_{a,b}})=\sum\frac{e_{a,b}}{|S_{a,b}|}\int_{S_{a,b}}g\). Bounds: \(|E_0|\le2\max|e|\) and \(|d_{a,b}|=|e_{0,b}-e_{1,b}|/2\le\max|e|\), so \(|\Phi^S|\le(2+2)\max|e|/\rho\le6\max|e|/\rho\); \(\beta_{a,b}(x)\) denotes the explicit coefficients of \(e_{a,b}\) in \(v\) and \(k_b\) (the representation is not unique since \(\sum e=0\)), which are at most \(1/\rho\) in absolute value, hence at most \(2/\rho\). \(\square\)

Square-exponential matching by the dyadic flow

Statement. There is a universal \(L_0\) such that for every \(L\ge L_0\), if \(X_1,\dots,X_N\) are i.i.d. uniform on \(\Omega\) and \(Y\) is evenly spread, then with probability tending to one as \(N\to\infty\) there is a matching \(\pi\) with \(\sum_{i\le N}\exp\big[\frac{N}{L^2\log N}|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|^2\big]\le2N\) for \(j=1,2\). This is the conjecture's conclusion at the pair \((2,2)\), which lies off the curve (\(1/2+1/2=1\)).

Proof. Parameters. \(s=L\sqrt{\log N/N}\), \(\Psi_j(t)=\psi_2(t/s)\), \(\psi_2(u)=e^{u^2}-1\), \(h=1/4\). Let \(J\) be the least integer with \(2^{-J}\le s/32\), \(\delta=2^{-J}\ge s/64\); for large \(N\), \(J\le\log N\) and \(N\delta^2\ge L^2\log N/4096\ge16\log N\) for \(L\ge256\). Smear each \(X_i\) uniformly over its level-\(J\) dyadic cell (\(\delta_X=\delta\)) and each \(Y_j\) uniformly over its rectangle \(R_j\) from the definition of evenly spread (\(\delta_Y=20/\sqrt N\)); then \(\nu_Y=N\cdot\text{Lebesgue}\) on \(\Omega\).

Events. \(m(S)\) is the number of \(X_i\) in a dyadic square \(S\). \(E_1\): every dyadic square \(S\) of level \(j\le J\) has \(N|S|/2\le m(S)\le2N|S|\). By the multiplicative Chernoff bounds and \(N|S|\ge16\log N\), each fails with probability at most \(2N^{-2}\), and there are at most \(2\cdot4^J\le N\) squares for large \(N\), so \(P(E_1)\to1\). On \(E_1\), \(\nu_X\) has density at least \(N/2\), so \(\lambda=N/2\) and \(\lambda'=N/4\).

Error. \(\Psi_j(\delta/h)=\psi_2(4\delta/s)\le\psi_2(1/8)<0.02\) and \(\Psi_j(4\cdot20/\sqrt N)=\psi_2(80/(L\sqrt{\log N}))\to0\); the error term is at most \(\frac14N\cdot2(0.02+0.02)=0.02N\) for large \(N\).

The flow. For a dyadic square \(S\) of level \(j<J\) with children \(S_c\), let \(e_c=m(S_c)-m(S)/4\) and \(\Phi_j=\sum_S\Phi^S\) (dyadic square flow, the squares of a level being disjoint up to boundaries, where the normal components vanish). With \(\Phi=\sum_{j<J}\Phi_j\), the identities telescope: \(\int\nabla g\cdot\Phi=\sum_{j<J}\sum_S\sum_c\frac{e_c}{|S_c|}\int_{S_c}g=\int g\,d\nu_X-N\int g=\int g\,d(\nu_X-\nu_Y)\), since the level-\(J\) smearing of \(X\) has density \(m(\text{cell})/|\text{cell}|\) and \(m(\Omega)=N\). By the flow bound (2), the minimal matching cost is at most \(\frac N4\int_\Omega C(4\Phi/N)+0.02N\le\frac N2\int_\Omega\psi_2(4|\Phi|/(Ns))\,dx+0.02N\), using \(C(z)\le2\psi_2(|z|/s)\). With \(Ns=L\sqrt{N\log N}\), it suffices that \(I:=\int_\Omega\psi_2(4|\Phi|/(L\sqrt{N\log N}))\le1/2\) with probability tending to one: then the cost is below \(0.27N\), and each coordinate's sum of exponentials is at most \(N+0.27N\le2N\).

Martingale tail. Fix \(x\) and write \(S_j(x)\) for its level-\(j\) square, \(\rho_j=2^{-j}\). Let \(\mathcal F_j\) be generated by the counts of all dyadic squares of level at most \(j\). Given \(\mathcal F_j\), the points in each level-\(j\) square are i.i.d. uniform in it, so each coordinate of \(\Phi_j(x)=\sum_c\beta_c(x)e_c\) is a sum over the \(m(S_j(x))\) points of \(S_j(x)\) of the independent centred variables \(\beta_{c(\text{point})}(x)-\bar\beta\), each in an interval of length at most \(4/\rho_j\). By Hoeffding's lemma, given \(\mathcal F_j\) it is sub-Gaussian with variance proxy \(\sigma_j^2=4m(S_j(x))/\rho_j^2\), which is at most \(8N\) on \(E_1\); \(\sigma_j^2\) is \(\mathcal F_j\)-measurable and \(\Phi_j(x)\) is \(\mathcal F_{j+1}\)-measurable. For a coordinate \(B\) of a partial sum \(\sum_{j_0\le j<J}\Phi_j(x)\), the process \(\exp(\theta B_n-\frac{\theta^2}2\sum_{j_0\le j<n}\sigma_j^2)\) is a supermartingale, so \(P(|B|\ge t,\ \sum\sigma_j^2\le V)\le2e^{-t^2/(2V)}\); with \(V=8NJ\le8N\log N\) and both coordinates, \(P(|\sum_{j_0\le j<J}\Phi_j(x)|\ge t,E_1)\le4e^{-t^2/(32N\log N)}\). The same holds conditionally on \(\mathcal F_{j_0}\) with \(E_1\) replaced by \(E_{1,T}\) (defined below) for \(x\in T\): the supermartingale starts at level \(j_0\) under \(P(\cdot\mid\mathcal F_{j_0})\), and every square \(S_j(x)\), \(j\ge j_0\), lies in \(T\), so \(\sum\sigma_j^2\le V\) on \(E_{1,T}\).

Coarse levels. Let \(j_0=\lfloor(\log N)^{1/4}\rfloor\), \(A=\sum_{j<j_0}\Phi_j\), \(B=\sum_{j_0\le j<J}\Phi_j\). \(E_2\): every child excess at levels \(j<j_0\) has \(|e_c|\le\sqrt{m(S)j_0}\). By Hoeffding \(P(|e_c|>\sqrt{mj_0})\le2e^{-2j_0}\), and there are at most \(8\cdot4^{j_0}\) pairs, so \(P(E_2^c)\le16(4/e^2)^{j_0}\to0\). On \(E_1\cap E_2\), \(|\Phi_j|\le6\sqrt{2N\rho_j^2j_0}/\rho_j\), so \(|A|\le6\sqrt2\,j_0^{3/2}\sqrt N\) and \(8|A|/(L\sqrt{N\log N})\le70j_0^{3/2}/(L\sqrt{\log N})\to0\) uniformly in \(x\).

Fine levels. By convexity of \(\psi_2\), \(I\le\frac12\psi_2(8|A|_\infty/(L\sqrt{N\log N}))+\frac12\sum_TZ_T\), where \(T\) runs over the \(4^{j_0}\) squares of level \(j_0\) and \(Z_T=\int_T\psi_2(8|B|/(L\sqrt{N\log N}))\,1_{E_{1,T}}\), \(E_{1,T}\) being the part of \(E_1\) that concerns subsquares of \(T\). Given \(\mathcal F_{j_0}\), the \(Z_T\) are independent. Put \(U=8|B(x)|/(L\sqrt{N\log N})\); the tail above gives \(P(U\ge u,E_{1,T})\le4e^{-u^2L^2/2048}\), so for \(L^2>4096\), \(\mathbb E[\psi_2(U)1_{E_{1,T}}]=\int_0^\infty2ue^{u^2}P(U\ge u,E_{1,T})\,du\le\int_0^\infty8ue^{-(L^2/2048-1)u^2}du=4/(L^2/2048-1)=:\eta\), and, using \(\psi_2(U)^2\le e^{2U^2}-1\), \(\mathbb E[\psi_2(U)^21_{E_{1,T}}]\le\int_0^\infty4ue^{2u^2}4e^{-u^2L^2/2048}du=8/(L^2/2048-2)=:\eta_2\). Hence \(\mathbb E[Z_T\mid\mathcal F_{j_0}]\le|T|\eta\) and \(\mathbb E[Z_T^2\mid\mathcal F_{j_0}]\le|T|^2\eta_2\) (Cauchy–Schwarz over \(T\)), so \(\sum_TZ_T\) has conditional mean at most \(\eta\) and conditional variance at most \(4^{-j_0}\eta_2\to0\). By Chebyshev, \(\sum_TZ_T\le2\eta\) with probability tending to one. For \(L\) large enough that \(\eta\le1/8\), on \(E_1\cap E_2\) and that event, \(I\le\frac12\psi_2(o(1))+\eta\le1/2\) for large \(N\). \(\square\)

Scope. The theorem concerns the pair \((2,2)\), below the bar the conjecture sets on the curve (\(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1\), not \(1/2\)), coordinatewise and with probability tending to one. The Talagrand–Yukich bound (4.131) of [T4] states the isotropic Euclidean form with probability at least \(9/10\); since \(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\le d(X_i,Y_{\pi(i)})\), that bound already implies the theorem's coordinatewise conclusion for one matching with probability at least \(9/10\) (with \(L\) replaced by \(\sqrt L\)). The theorem's only addition is probability tending to one; [T4] calls [134]'s result "a stronger version" of (4.131), [134] was not read, and this too may be known. No novelty is claimed. Its role here is to show that the reduction, through one explicit flow, reaches a known exponent pair.

What the dyadic flow cannot give. Heuristic, unproved: \(\Phi(x)\) is a martingale over about \(\frac12\log_2N\) levels whose increments have conditional variance of order \(N\), so it is approximately Gaussian with variance of order \(N\log N\), with Gaussian lower tails up to \(t\) of order \(\sqrt{\log N}\) standard deviations. Then \(\int\psi_\alpha(4|\Phi|/(Ns))\) contains \(\exp((ct/L)^\alpha-Ct^2)\) at such \(t\), which grows without bound for \(\alpha>2\). The cost is separable, so one coordinate with exponent above \(2\) suffices: the dyadic flow is too costly at every pair of the curve, the endpoint \((2,\infty)\) included (there \(\psi_\infty\) requires a sup-norm bound that Gaussian tails also violate). So this route needs a flow whose value at a typical point has tails lighter than Gaussian at the scale \(\sqrt{N\log N}\); nothing here shows that the conjecture itself needs one, the flow bound running in one direction.

Degenerate cases. Flow bound: \(\Phi=0\) is admissible exactly when \(\nu_X=\nu_Y\), and then the bound is the smearing error, as for \(X=Y\) in lem:hopf-lax-matching-bound. A field with \(C(\Phi/\lambda')\) infinite somewhere is excluded only if \(C\) is infinite; here \(C\) is finite. Theorem: as \(N\to\infty\) with \(L\) fixed; \(N=1\) is excluded by \(\log N>0\). The dyadic square flow with all \(e_c=0\) is \(0\). A square with one child holding all the excess (\(e=(3m/4,-m/4,-m/4,-m/4)\)) gives a large field, within the bound \(6\max|e|/\rho\).

Nonvacuity. The flow bound applies to the dyadic flow of the theorem, with \(\lambda=N/2\) on \(E_1\). The theorem's hypotheses hold for every \(N\ge2\) and \(L\ge L_0\) with \(L_0\) from the proof (\(L\ge256\) and \(\eta=4/(L^2/2048-1)\le1/8\), so \(L\ge260\) suffices); it is a statement with probability tending to one, not conditional.

New mathematics. The Hopf–Lax bound gives a primal criterion: the matching cost is at most the conjugate-free cost \(\lambda'\int C(\Phi/\lambda')\) of any flow carrying the smeared configurations to each other. Through the dyadic Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády flow, treated as a vector martingale over the dyadic levels, this proves the conjecture's conclusion at the off-curve pair \((2,2)\) coordinatewise with probability tending to one. The same martingale structure is, heuristically, Gaussian, which is why the dyadic flow is not expected to reach any pair with an exponent above \(2\), that is, any pair of the curve.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The pair \((2,2)\) is not on the curve, and no implication toward a pair on the curve changed. The entry fixes what this route needs at a pair of the curve: a flow \(\Phi\) between the smeared configurations with \(\int\psi_{\alpha_j}\big(4|\Phi_j|/(L\sqrt{N\log N})\big)\) bounded, that is, per-point flow values with tails \(\exp(-t^{\alpha_j})\) at scale \(\sqrt{N\log N}\), lighter than the Gaussian tails of the dyadic martingale.

Record search. tools/search-claims.py for "dyadic flow martingale transport square exponential Talagrand Yukich" and "Fenchel Young flow divergence Beckmann bound matching" returned this notebook's conj:ultimate-matching and unrelated flow claims on charged graphs; tools/claim_resemblance.py on the claim spec matched only this notebook's claims: the flow bound to lem:hopf-lax-matching-bound (0.65, the lemma it extends), the theorem to lem:common-exponent-cap (0.46) and cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction (0.41), both related in the spec.

Verification gate. Dependencies read: lem:hopf-lax-matching-bound and its proof in the record; Section 4.7 of [T4]; Proposition 4.3.2, the definition of evenly spread points and (4.131) of [T4]. One fresh-context medium reviewer received the draft, the claim spec, the previous entry and those excerpts. It checked the flow bound (every step of the Hopf–Lax proof under general smearings, Fenchel–Young and the sign convention), the dyadic square flow (integrations by parts, internal line, boundary values) and every step and constant of the theorem (\(L\ge260\) works). Required changes made: the smearings must be absolutely continuous; the summation index of the error collided with \(Y_j\); the square-flow bound on \(d_{a,b}\) was loose (now \(\max|e|\)) and the coefficients are the explicit ones; the martingale tail is now stated conditionally on \(\mathcal F_{j_0}\) with \(E_{1,T}\), as used; the heuristic's range was understated (one exponent above \(2\) suffices, so the whole curve); "the conjecture needs" replaced by "this route needs"; the Scope now says that (4.131) already gives the coordinatewise conclusion at probability \(9/10\); the Owed attempt is now "set aside after a sketch" with its obstacles as observed, not proved, and the primal–dual equivalence as unverified; the Follow-up's variance target replaced, since \(\int|\Phi|\ge W_1\) fixes the scale. No follow-up pass: the corrections weaken or qualify statements and add one hypothesis. Process audit. The reviewer proposed the title "Flow form of the Hopf–Lax bound; the dyadic flow gives square-exponential matchings", which must not suggest a new matching theorem; the title below follows it. It found necessity stated where only sufficiency holds ("needs"), an unproved primal–dual equivalence asserted, an Owed attempt overstated as "made in part" with an undefined \(\beta\), the Scope silent on (4.131) implying the conclusion, a next test answerable by an elementary inequality, and the heuristic's range narrower than the draft's own cost structure supports.

Process assessment. Sufficiency was again written as necessity in two places, and a known result's implication for the new theorem was not checked before writing its Scope. Prevention. The reviewer brief asks for both; they were caught there. The repeated "needs" error is carried to the scheduled route review, which can decide whether a wording check is worth building.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • Every flow linear in the empirical measure (dyadic, electric \(\nabla\Delta^{-1}\), any fixed kernel) is at each point a sum of \(N\) i.i.d. terms bounded by about \(1/\delta\) with variance \(\gtrsim N\log N\); by moderate-deviation lower bounds it has Gaussian tails well past \(\sqrt{\log N}\) standard deviations, so this route needs an adaptive flow. Record. tools/search-claims.py "linear flow kernel Gaussian tail moderate deviations matching" returned no related record.
  • The tube flow of a cyclically monotone matching, \(\Phi=\sum_i\Delta_i\int_0^1\kappa(x-X_i-t\Delta_i)dt\), costs at most a constant times the matching cost if the interpolated density stays below a constant times \(N\) (joint convexity of the perspective), so the flow route would lose only a constant; the density bound for separable exponential costs is the unverified point. Record. tools/search-claims.py "tube flow cyclically monotone matching density displacement" returned no related record.

Suggestion checked. For the dyadic kernel the reviewer's linear-flow suggestion was checked in its arithmetic: one point \(z\) contributes to \(\Phi(x)\) only at the levels where \(z\) shares the square of \(x\), at most \(2/\rho_j\) per level, so each term is bounded by \(M\le\sum_j2/\rho_j\le4/\delta\), of order \(\sqrt N/(L\sqrt{\log N})\); its variance contribution at level \(j\) is of order \(\rho_j^{-2}\cdot\rho_j^2N=N\), so the total variance \(s^2\) is of order \(NJ\asymp N\log N\) where the coefficients do not vanish. The deviations needed are \(t\asymp\sqrt{\log N}\) standard deviations, and \(tM/s\asymp1/\sqrt{\log N}\to0\), which is the range of Kolmogorov's converse exponential inequality for bounded summands (recalled, not read in this cycle). Not checked: that the variance is of this order at most points for a general fixed kernel, and the inequality's exact hypotheses.

Follow-up. Continuing. The item now has a primal criterion and a calibration; the open question is a flow with sub-Gaussian per-point tails at fixed variance (the variance itself cannot be lowered much: \(\int|\Phi|\ge W_1(\nu_X,N\cdot\text{Lebesgue})\) for every admissible \(\Phi\), and an Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády-type lower bound for \(W_1\) would force mean square \(\gtrsim N\log N\); unverified for the smeared measure). Next attempt, the reviewer's suggestion: prove that every flow linear in the empirical measure, \(\Phi(x)=\sum_iK(x,X_i)-N\int K(x,z)dz\) with a fixed kernel, has Gaussian lower tails at a typical point up to the needed deviations (a sum of \(N\) bounded i.i.d. terms of variance \(\gtrsim N\log N\), by a moderate-deviation lower bound). That would turn this entry's heuristic into a lemma covering the dyadic and electric flows and every fixed kernel, and would show that the route needs an adaptive (nonlinear) flow, such as a capacity-limited max-flow or the tube flow of an optimal matching.

Sub-idea follow-up

  • Follow-up. Continuing: the converse of the Hopf–Lax bound was not attempted; the tube-flow sub-idea below is its primal form.
  • Follow-up. Continuing: the Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády baseline under smearing was not attempted; the reviewer's remark that \(\int|\Phi|\ge W_1\) relies on the same lower bound, which raises its priority.
  • Follow-up. Continuing: not attempted in this entry.

Sub-ideas

  • Decide whether the tube flow \(\Phi=\sum_i\Delta_i\int_0^1\kappa(x-X_i-t\Delta_i)\,dt\) of an optimal matching has interpolated density bounded by a constant times \(N\) for separable exponential costs (a displacement-interpolation \(L^\infty\) bound); if so, the flow route loses only a constant against the conjecture and is equivalent to it.

The dyadic flow bound fails above exponent 2; Talagrand–Yukich read

16 October 2026 · Working proofs; one flow only · Dyadic flow obstruction. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Chosen. This entry continues the queued check entry-2026-10-16-monograph-statements:s3 for the fourth entry of its spell, rather than the head s1. Its developments so far, read again before planning: the Hopf–Lax reduction ("The reduction is proved, including the endpoints and the comparison with weights at least linear"), the Leighton–Shor class test ("The reduced statement is not refuted by its first test") and the dyadic flow bound ("The item now has a primal criterion and a calibration"). The last left a heuristic, that the dyadic flow cannot reach the curve, on which the choice of the next construction depends; proving it decides whether the one explicit flow at hand, the dyadic one, can serve. The head s1 stays at the head.

Owed attempt. The latest follow-up named: "prove that every flow linear in the empirical measure, \(\Phi(x)=\sum_iK(x,X_i)-N\int K(x,z)dz\) with a fixed kernel, has Gaussian lower tails at a typical point up to the needed deviations". Made in part: the per-point lower tail is proved for every kernel bounded by \(c\) whose per-point variance \(s^2\) satisfies \(c^2\lesssim s^2/\log N\) (lem:block-lower-tail), and the full obstruction, a statement in probability about the flow cost, is proved for the dyadic kernel (thm:dyadic-flow-obstruction). For a general kernel neither the variance condition nor the concentration over points \(x\), which the dyadic proof gets from the independence of dyadic blocks, is proved.

Question. Is the dyadic flow's per-point value Gaussian in its tails, so that the flow bound with this flow fails at every pair of the curve? If yes, this route's next construction cannot be the dyadic flow.

General statement. thm:dyadic-flow-obstruction: for every fixed \(L>0\) and every coordinate exponent in \((2,\infty]\), with probability tending to one the cost term of the dyadic flow in that coordinate exceeds every fixed multiple of \(N\).

Block lower tail

Statement. Let \(\xi_1,\dots,\xi_n\) be i.i.d. real, \(\mathbb E\xi_i=0\), \(\operatorname{Var}\xi_i=\sigma^2>0\), \(|\xi_i|\le c\), \(S=\sum_i\xi_i\), \(s=\sigma\sqrt n\). If \(x\ge1\), \(m=\lceil128x^2\rceil\le n\) and \(c^2\le s^2/(2m)\), then \(P(S\ge xs)\ge64^{-m}\ge e^{-540x^2}\).

Proof. Split the indices into \(m\) blocks of sizes \(k_\ell\in\{\lfloor n/m\rfloor,\lceil n/m\rceil\}\); since \(n\ge m\), \(k_\ell\ge n/(2m)\), so the block sums \(B_\ell\) have variances \(\sigma_\ell^2=k_\ell\sigma^2\ge s^2/(2m)\ge c^2\). Then \(\mathbb EB_\ell^4=k_\ell\mathbb E\xi^4+3k_\ell(k_\ell-1)\sigma^4\le\sigma_\ell^2c^2+3\sigma_\ell^4\le4\sigma_\ell^4\). By Hölder, \(\sigma_\ell^2=\mathbb EB^2\le(\mathbb E|B|)^{2/3}(\mathbb EB^4)^{1/3}\), so \(\mathbb E|B_\ell|\ge\sigma_\ell^3/(\mathbb EB_\ell^4)^{1/2}\ge\sigma_\ell/2\), and \(\mathbb E(B_\ell)_+=\frac12\mathbb E|B_\ell|\ge\sigma_\ell/4\). Hence \(\mathbb E[B_\ell1\{B_\ell\ge\sigma_\ell/8\}]\ge\sigma_\ell/4-\sigma_\ell/8=\sigma_\ell/8\), and by Cauchy–Schwarz this is at most \(\sigma_\ell P(B_\ell\ge\sigma_\ell/8)^{1/2}\), so \(P(B_\ell\ge\sigma_\ell/8)\ge1/64\). The blocks are independent, and on the intersection \(S\ge\sum_\ell\sigma_\ell/8\ge m\cdot s/(8\sqrt{2m})=\sqrt m\,s/(8\sqrt2)\ge xs\). So \(P(S\ge xs)\ge64^{-m}\), and \(m\le129x^2\) gives \(64^{-m}\ge e^{-129\ln64\,x^2}\ge e^{-540x^2}\). \(\square\)

Coefficients of the dyadic square flow

Statement. In the dyadic square flow on a square \(S\) of side \(\rho\), each coordinate \(k\) of \(\Phi^S(x)\) is \(\sum_c\beta^{(k)}_c(x)e_c\) with \(\beta^{(k)}_c(x)=b^{(k)}_c(t)/\rho\), \(t=(x-\text{corner})/\rho\in[0,1)^2\), \(|b^{(k)}_c|\le2\) (in fact \(\le1\)), and the weight \(w_k(t)=\frac14\sum_c(b^{(k)}_c(t)-\bar b^{(k)}(t))^2\) is \(w_1(t)=2\min(t_1,1-t_1)^2\) and \(w_2(t)=\min(t_2,1-t_2)^2\). So \(\int_{[0,1]^2}w_1=1/6\), \(\int w_2=1/12\), and \(0\le w_k\le1/2\).

Proof. From the construction in the previous entry. Vertical part, \(t_2<1/2\): \(v=-2E_0t_2/\rho\), so the coefficients are \(-2t_2\) on the two bottom children and \(0\) on the top ones, mean \(-t_2\), deviations \(\pm t_2\), \(w_2=t_2^2\). For \(t_2\ge1/2\), written through the four \(e_c\): coefficients \(-1\) on the bottom children and \(1-2t_2\) on the top ones, mean \(-t_2\), deviations \(\pm(1-t_2)\), \(w_2=(1-t_2)^2\). Horizontal part on row \(b\), \(t_1<1/2\): \(k_b=-4d_{0,b}t_1/\rho=-2(e_{0,b}-e_{1,b})t_1/\rho\), coefficients \(\mp2t_1\) on that row and \(0\) on the other, mean \(0\), \(w_1=\frac14(4t_1^2+4t_1^2)=2t_1^2\); for \(t_1\ge1/2\), \(k_b=-4d_{0,b}(1-t_1)/\rho\) and \(w_1=2(1-t_1)^2\). The integrals are \(2\cdot2\int_0^{1/2}u^2du=1/6\) and \(2\int_0^{1/2}u^2du=1/12\). \(\square\)

Dyadic flow obstruction

Setting. As in thm:dyadic-flow-square-exponential, except that \(L>0\) is any fixed constant: \(X\) i.i.d. uniform, \(s=L\sqrt{\log N/N}\), dyadic depth \(J\) with \(s/64\le2^{-J}\le s/32\), and \(\Phi=\sum_{j<J}\Phi_j\) the dyadic flow carrying the level-\(J\) smearing of \(X\) to \(N\cdot\)Lebesgue. For \(\alpha\in(2,\infty)\), \(\psi_\alpha(u)=e^{u^\alpha}-1\); \(\psi_\infty(u)=N(u-1)_+\).

Statement. For each fixed \(L>0\), coordinate \(k\in\{1,2\}\), \(\alpha\in(2,\infty]\) and \(M>0\), with probability tending to one as \(N\to\infty\), \[I_k:=\int_\Omega\psi_\alpha\Big(\frac{4|\Phi_k(x)|}{L\sqrt{N\log N}}\Big)dx\ge M.\] Hence, with \(C(z)=\sum_k\psi_{\alpha_k}(|z_k|/s)\) and any \(\lambda'\) with \(N/K'\le\lambda'\le K'N\) for a fixed \(K'\ge1\) (the reduction uses \(\lambda'=N/4\)), the flow-bound value \(\lambda'\int C(\Phi/\lambda')\) of the dyadic flow exceeds every fixed multiple of \(N\) whenever some \(\alpha_k>2\), in particular at every pair of the curve \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2=1/2\): the argument \(|\Phi_k|/(\lambda's)\) is at least \(4|\Phi_k|/(4K'L\sqrt{N\log N})\), so the first part with \(4K'L\) in place of \(L\) applies, and \(\lambda'\ge N/K'\).

Proof. Point representation. Let \(j_1=\lfloor\frac18\log_2N\rfloor\) and, for \(x\in\Omega\), let \(T=S_{j_1}(x)\) and \(n=m(T)\). Given \(\mathcal F_{j_1}\) (the counts of all dyadic squares of level at most \(j_1\)), the points in \(T\) are i.i.d. uniform in \(T\), and \(B(x)=\sum_{j_1\le j<J}\Phi_j(x)=\sum_{Z\in T}K(x,Z)\) with \(K(x,z)=\sum_{j_1\le j<J}1\{z\in S_j(x)\}(\beta^{(j)}_{c_j(z)}(x)-\bar\beta^{(j)}(x))\), because \(e_c=\sum_{z\in S}(1\{z\in S_c\}-\frac14)\). For \(Z\) uniform in \(T\), \(\mathbb EK=0\), and the terms of different levels are orthogonal: for \(j<j'\), \(K_j\) is constant on \(S_{j'}(x)\), where \(K_{j'}\) is supported and has mean zero. So, for coordinate \(k\), \(\operatorname{Var}(K_k(x,Z))=\sum_{j_1\le j<J}\frac{\rho_j^2}{\rho_{j_1}^2}\cdot\frac{w_k(T_j(x))}{\rho_j^2}\), \(T_j(x)\) the relative position of \(x\) in \(S_j(x)\), and the conditional variance of \(B_k(x)\) is \(s_k(x)^2=\frac n{\rho_{j_1}^2}\sum_{j_1\le j<J}w_k(T_j(x))\). Each term is bounded by \(c=\sum_{j<J}4/\rho_j\le8\cdot2^J\le512\sqrt N/(L\sqrt{\log N})\).

Typical points. For \(x\) uniform in \(\Omega\), the relative positions \(T_j(x)\), \(j\ge0\), are independent and uniform (binary digits), so by Hoeffding's inequality, with \(0\le w_k\le1/2\) and \(\int w_k\ge1/12\), the set \(G\) of \(x\) with \(\sum_{j_1\le j<J}w_k(T_j(x))\ge(J-j_1)/24\) has \(\lambda(G)\to1\). For large \(N\), \(J-j_1\ge\frac14\log_2N\), so on \(G\) and on the \(\mathcal F_{j_1}\)-measurable event \(n\ge N|T|/2\), \(s_k(x)^2\ge\frac N2\cdot\frac{\log_2N}{96}\ge\frac{N\log N}{144}\).

Coarse levels do not help. \(A(x)=\Phi(x)-B(x)\) is \(\mathcal F_{j_1}\)-measurable. With \(\sigma=\operatorname{sign}A_k(x)\) (\(+1\) if zero), \(|\Phi_k(x)|\ge\sigma\Phi_k(x)\ge\sigma B_k(x)\). Lemma block-lower-tail applied, given \(\mathcal F_{j_1}\), to the \(n\) terms \(\sigma K_k(x,Z_i)\) gives \(P(|\Phi_k(x)|\ge ts_k(x)\mid\mathcal F_{j_1})\ge e^{-540t^2}\) whenever \(t\ge1\), \(\lceil128t^2\rceil\le n\) and \(c^2\le s_k(x)^2/(2\lceil128t^2\rceil)\). On \(G\) and \(n\ge N|T|/2\ge N^{3/4}/2\), the last two hold for large \(N\) (depending on \(L\)) whenever \(t\le\sqrt{\log N}\), since \(c^2\le512^2N/(L^2\log N)\) and \(s_k^2\ge N\log N/144\).

Many points. Fix \(t\in[1,\sqrt{\log N}]\) with \(q:=e^{-540t^2}\ge N^{-1/16}\), and let \(D=\{x:|\Phi_k(x)|\ge a\}\) with \(a\le t\sqrt{N\log N}/12\), so that \(D\supseteq\{x\in G:|\Phi_k(x)|\ge ts_k(x)\}\). On the event \(E\) that \(n=m(T)\ge N|T|/2\) for every square \(T\) of level \(j_1\), which is \(\mathcal F_{j_1}\)-measurable and has probability tending to one (Chernoff, \(N|T|\ge N^{3/4}\)), the variables \(\lambda(D\cap T)\) for the \(4^{j_1}\le N^{1/4}\) squares \(T\) are, given \(\mathcal F_{j_1}\), independent and at most \(|T|\), with conditional mean of their sum at least \(q\lambda(G)\ge p:=q/2\) for large \(N\), and conditional variance at most \(\sum_T|T|^2=4^{-j_1}\le4N^{-1/4}\). By Chebyshev, \(P(\lambda(D)<p/2\mid\mathcal F_{j_1})\le4N^{-1/4}/(p/2)^2=64N^{-1/4}q^{-2}\le64N^{-1/8}\) on \(E\). So \(\lambda(D)\ge q/4\) with probability tending to one.

Finite \(\alpha\). Take \(t=\tau\sqrt{\log N}\) with \(540\tau^2=1/16\), so \(q=N^{-1/16}\), and \(a=t\sqrt{N\log N}/12=\tau\sqrt N\log N/12\). Then \(\lambda(D)\ge N^{-1/16}/4\) with probability tending to one, and on \(D\) the argument of \(\psi_\alpha\) is at least \(4a/(L\sqrt{N\log N})=\frac{\tau}{3L}\sqrt{\log N}\), so \(I_k\ge\frac{N^{-1/16}}4\big(\exp\big((\tfrac\tau{3L})^\alpha(\log N)^{\alpha/2}\big)-1\big)\to\infty\), because \((\log N)^{\alpha/2}\) grows faster than \(\frac1{16}\log N\) for \(\alpha>2\).

\(\alpha=\infty\). Take \(t=\max(6L,1)\) and \(a=L\sqrt{N\log N}/2\le t\sqrt{N\log N}/12\). For large \(N\), \(t\le\sqrt{\log N}\) and \(q=e^{-540t^2}\ge N^{-1/16}\), so \(\lambda(D)\ge q/4=:p_L>0\) with probability tending to one; on \(D\) the argument is at least \(2\), so \(I_k\ge p_LN\to\infty\). \(\square\)

Scope. The theorem is about one flow. It shows that the flow bound, used with the dyadic Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády flow, gives nothing at any pair with an exponent above \(2\); it says nothing about the matching, nor about other flows. Lemma block-lower-tail gives the same Gaussian lower tail at a single point for every flow linear in the empirical measure whose kernel is bounded by \(c\) and whose per-point variance is \(s^2\) with \(c\sqrt{\log N}\lesssim s\); for a general kernel that variance condition and the concentration over \(x\), which here came from the independence of the level-\(j_1\) blocks, are not proved.

Degenerate cases. Lemma: \(x<1\) is excluded; \(n=m\) blocks of one term each need \(c^2\le s^2/(2m)=\sigma^2/2\), impossible since \(\sigma^2\le c^2\), so the lemma then says nothing, as it must for a single bounded variable. A constant-sign \(\xi\) is excluded by mean zero. Theorem: \(\alpha=2\) is excluded, and there the previous entry's theorem shows the cost is bounded for \(L\ge260\); points \(x\) on a dyadic line form a null set.

Nonvacuity. Lemma: Rademacher \(\xi_i=\pm1\), \(c=\sigma=1\), \(n=10^4\), \(s=100\), \(x=1\): \(m=128\le n\) and \(c^2=1\le s^2/256\approx39\). Theorem: every fixed \(L>0\) and \(\alpha\in(2,\infty]\), for instance \(L=260\) and the pair \((4,4)\) in either coordinate.

New mathematics. A self-contained lower tail for sums of bounded i.i.d. variables up to \(x\) of order \(s/c\), by blocking and Paley–Zygmund. With it, and the scale-invariant coefficients of the dyadic square flow (per-level weights with means \(1/6\) and \(1/12\)), the dyadic flow's value at a typical point has Gaussian lower tails at scale \(\sqrt{N\log N}\), on a set of points large enough that the flow bound's cost diverges at every exponent above \(2\), for every constant \(L\), with probability tending to one. The previous entry's heuristic is now a theorem for this flow. Talagrand and Yukich (1993) were read: they prove the isotropic two-sample pair \((2,2)\) by a fixed-point displacement argument analogous to the previous entry's.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The result is a negative statement about one construction: the dyadic flow, used in the flow bound, cannot give any pair of the curve. This route's next candidate is therefore a different flow, presumably adaptive, since the one linear flow at hand fails; nothing here shows that every linear flow fails, or that the conjecture needs any flow at all, the flow bound being only sufficient. Bearing. Nothing at the goal's parameters: every pair of the curve has an exponent above \(2\), where the theorem is negative and concerns one construction, not the matching. The cycle was spent on it because the result could have been the opposite: had the dyadic flow's per-point values had lighter than Gaussian tails, the flow bound would have given the conjecture at pairs of the curve.

For the next attempt, "self-balancing walk adaptive discrepancy bounded tails" and "sup norm flow divergence perimeter Strang max-flow min-cut" returned nothing on flows of point sets.

Review. The stand-in review after the previous entry raised four objections. (1) The linear-flow suggestion was named as the next step before it was verified: correct in part; the append tool required, and the previous entry carries, a Suggestion checked paragraph with the arithmetic for the dyadic kernel, which explicitly left the general-kernel variance unchecked; this entry proves the dyadic case only. (2) A rough scaling estimate for single-scale bumps was reported in chat during review without the Unreviewed prefix: correct; that estimate was never recorded and was superseded by lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound, which its entry's reviewer checked; the label rule exists, and a mechanical check for it is queued as the build unreviewed-label-check of the common-multiplier notebook. (3) The remark that boundary fluxes give no obstruction to sub-Gaussian tails was reported unlabelled and unrecorded: correct; it is recorded here as a heuristic only: the flux of a square's excess through its boundary bounds the average of \(|\Phi|\) along that boundary scale by scale, which does not by itself force Gaussian tails across scales. (4) The novelty of probability tending to one in thm:dyadic-flow-square-exponential was left open: correct; the paper that decides it could not be retrieved by script (a bot wall), a request was filed, and the user then supplied it; it is read below.

Talagrand and Yukich, 1993

Sources read. Talagrand and Yukich, "The integrability of the square exponential transportation cost", Ann. Appl. Probab. 3 (1993) 1100–1111, supplied by the user as a 12-page scan and read from its rendered pages 1–5: the four numbered and lettered theorems, Remarks (i)–(iii), the description of the AKT transformation, Proposition 1 and Lemma 1. The proofs of the remaining steps (pages 6–12) were not read.

What it states. Theorem 1: there is a constant \(K\) such that for all \(n\ge1\) there is a set \(A_n\) with \(P(A_n^c)\le n^{-2}\) and \(\mathbb E\big[\inf_\pi\frac1n\sum_i\exp\big((\|X_i-Y_{\pi(i)}\|/(K(\log n/n)^{1/2}))^2\big)1_{A_n}\big]\le K\), for two i.i.d. uniform samples and the Euclidean norm. Its method follows "the displacement of a fixed point" \(u\) through the AKT transformation, whose partition is adaptive (each half is rescaled so that its area equals its share of the points); conditioned appropriately, the successive displacements "become symmetric random variables, have zero correlation and also exhibit subgaussian character". It also records (page 1102): "In Talagrand (1993) it is conjectured that the exponent 2 of Theorem 1 may actually be increased to 4", which is the isotropic form of the pair \((4,4)\). The result it quotes from Talagrand (1993) is a two-sample analogue of [T4]'s Theorem 17.1.3 with exponent \(\beta<1/4\) in place of \(1/2\).

Comparison. The previous entry's thm:dyadic-flow-square-exponential uses an analogous mechanism, read here as an Eulerian counterpart (an interpretation, not stated in the paper): a martingale over dyadic levels with conditionally sub-Gaussian increments at a fixed point. As far as the statement of Theorem 1 goes: for a fixed constant inside the exponential, Theorem 1 with Markov's inequality bounds the failure probability only by a constant; enlarging the constant by a factor \(c\ge1\) and using \(\frac1n\sum e^{u^2/c}\le(\frac1n\sum e^{u^2})^{1/c}\) brings it to \(K2^{-c}+n^{-2}\), so every failure level \(\varepsilon\) is reached with a constant depending on \(\varepsilon\), but not probability tending to one at a fixed constant. In the two-sample setting the earlier theorem's coordinatewise conclusion (through the two-sample transfer) therefore adds only that form of the probability. In the evenly-spread setting Theorem 1 does not formally apply, and [T4]'s (4.131) gives probability \(9/10\); there the earlier theorem adds every failure level as well. The unread proof pages may give more. No novelty is claimed, and the earlier theorem's assessment is updated accordingly.

Suggestion checked. Of the reviewer's self-balancing suggestion below, one structural fact was checked: inside a square \(S\), given the boundary fluxes and the divergence, the mean of each coordinate of the flow is fixed, since \(\int_S\Phi_k=\int_{\partial S}x_k\,\Phi\cdot n-\int_Sx_k\,\operatorname{div}\Phi\) (integration by parts of \(\operatorname{div}(x_k\Phi)\)). So a choice of profile at level \(j\) can anti-correlate with the coarser field only in its variation within \(S\), not in its average over \(S\); the per-point gain must come from that variation. Not checked: that a greedy choice gives lighter tails, the claimed analogy with self-balancing walks, or the reviewer's Strang duality.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • Truncate-and-correct is a reparametrization of the open statement: the residual \(R=\Phi-\Phi^T\) vanishes on the boundary of \(\{|\Phi|>T\}\), so its divergence has zero mass on each component; routing it inside a component gains nothing at the coarse levels, and routing it outside is the global convex problem whose Fenchel–Rockafellar dual is the conjugate-class discrepancy restricted to the residual. Record. conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy and lem:flow-matching-bound bear on it; the search "sup norm flow divergence perimeter Strang max-flow min-cut" found nothing more.
  • Sup-norm flows: \(\inf\|\Phi\|_\infty\) subject to \(\operatorname{div}\Phi=f\) equals \(\sup_U|f(U)|/\operatorname{Per}(U)\) (Strang's max-flow min-cut duality), which Leighton–Shor puts at about \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4}\); a layered version, thresholds \(T_k=2^k\sqrt{N\log N}\) with each layer's flow supported on a set of measure about \(e^{-T_k^\alpha}\), could be tested. Record. lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound bears on the perimeter supremum; no flow-duality claim is registered (same search).
  • A multiscale self-balancing flow: at each level choose the profile of the four-child flux inside each square greedily to anti-correlate with the coarser field, as the Alweiss–Liu–Sawhney self-balancing walk turns Gaussian discrepancy into bounded tails; smallest test, a simulation of per-point tails against the fixed profile at moderate \(N\). Record. The search "self-balancing walk adaptive discrepancy bounded tails" returned nothing relevant.

Exponent pairs below the curve excluded, both settings

16 October 2026 · Working proof; every pair below the curve · Below-curve exclusion. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Question. The queue head entry-2026-10-16-monograph-statements:s1 asks whether some pair with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2<1/2\) and one exponent at most \(4\) is excluded, for instance \(\alpha_1>2\) at \(\alpha_2=\infty\), by an anisotropic form of the Leighton–Shor lower-bound construction. The common exponent cap excludes only pairs in \((4,\infty]^2\), and only in the weak form "not with probability tending to one", in the evenly-spread setting. The missing implication is whether the conjecture's curve is sharp off the diagonal: if a pair below the curve were attainable, the conjecture would not be the right statement there.

General statement. thm:below-curve-matching-exclusion: for every pair \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in(0,\infty]\) with \(1/\alpha_1+1/\alpha_2<1/2\) and every fixed \(L>0\), with probability tending to one no matching is good in both coordinates, both for evenly spread \(Y\) and for two independent samples.

Below-curve exclusion

Setting. \(X_1,\dots,X_N\) i.i.d. uniform on \(\Omega=[0,1]^2\); \(Y_1,\dots,Y_N\) either evenly spread or i.i.d. uniform and independent of \(X\). For \(L>0\) put \(s=L\sqrt{\log N/N}\). A matching \(\pi\) is good in coordinate \(j\) if \(\sum_i\exp[(|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|/s)^{\alpha_j}]\le2N\), or, for \(\alpha_j=\infty\), \(\max_i|X_i^j-Y^j_{\pi(i)}|\le s\) (the working context's definition). Write \(\beta_j=1/\alpha_j\in[0,\infty)\).

Statement. If \(\beta_1+\beta_2<1/2\), then for every \(L>0\), with probability tending to one as \(N\to\infty\), no matching is good in both coordinates.

Proof. Since \(\beta_1+\beta_2<1/2\), both \(\beta_j<1/2\). Both settings are symmetric under exchanging the coordinates, so assume \(\beta_1\ge\beta_2\) and put \(\kappa=(\log N)^{\beta_2-\beta_1}\in[(\log N)^{-1/2},1]\).

Step 1: excess. By Step 1 of lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound (the construction of Section 4.8 of [T4] with every tent multiplied by \(\kappa\), which needs \(\kappa\ge(\log N)^{-1/2}\)), with probability tending to one there is \(g:[0,1]\to[0,1]\), piecewise linear, with \(\int_0^1g'^2\le\kappa^2\), whose subgraph \(S=\{(x,y)\in\Omega:y\le g(x)\}\) satisfies \(\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in S\}\ge N\lambda(S)+\sqrt{\kappa N}(\log N)^{3/4}/L_1\), \(L_1\) universal. The function \(g\) depends on \(X\) only, and \(\int|g'|\le\kappa\), so its graph has length at most \(2\).

Step 2: the \(Y\) side. With probability tending to one, \(\operatorname{card}\{j:Y_j\in S\}\le N\lambda(S)+L_2\sqrt N\log\log N\). Evenly spread: the rectangles \(R_j\), of area \(1/N\) and diameter at most \(10/\sqrt N\), that meet \(S\) without lying in it meet the graph of \(g\), so they lie within distance \(10/\sqrt N\) of a curve of length at most \(2\), a set of area at most \(L_2/\sqrt N\) by Lemma 4.8.2 of [T4] (stated for the class \(\mathcal C\) of (4.117), to which \(g\) belongs: \(g(0)=g(1)=0\), \(0\le g\le1\), \(\int g'^2\le\kappa^2\le1\)), \(L_2\) universal; there are at most \(L_2\sqrt N\) of them, and the others contribute at most \(N\lambda(S)\). Two samples: given \(X\), \(\operatorname{card}\{j:Y_j\in S\}\) is binomial with mean \(N\lambda(S)\) and variance at most \(N\), and Chebyshev gives the bound with failure probability at most \((\log\log N)^{-2}\).

Step 3: crossing pairs. Fix a matching \(\pi\) good in both coordinates, and put \(\Delta_i=Y_{\pi(i)}-X_i\), \(u_j=(\log N)^{\beta_j}\) (so \(u_j=1\) when \(\alpha_j=\infty\)). For \(\alpha_j<\infty\), each \(i\) with \(|\Delta_i^j|>su_j\) contributes more than \(e^{\log N}=N\) to a sum at most \(2N\), so there is at most one; for \(\alpha_j=\infty\) there are none. Now \(\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in S\}-\operatorname{card}\{j:Y_j\in S\}\le\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in S,\,Y_{\pi(i)}\notin S\}\). If \(X_i=(a,b)\in S\), \(Y_{\pi(i)}=(a',b')\notin S\), \(|a'-a|\le su_1\) and \(|b'-b|\le su_2\), then \(b\le g(a)\) and \(b'>g(a')\), so \(g(a)-b<g(a)-g(a')+su_2\le\omega(a)+su_2\) with \(\omega(a)=\sup\{|g(a+t)-g(a)|:|t|\le su_1,\ a+t\in[0,1]\}\). So \(X_i\) lies in the layer \(\Lambda=\{(a,b)\in\Omega:g(a)-su_2-\omega(a)<b\le g(a)\}\), and \[\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in S\}-\operatorname{card}\{j:Y_j\in S\}\le\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in\Lambda\}+2.\]

Step 4: points in the layer. \(\Lambda\) depends on \(X\), so we count by a grid. Cut \(\Omega\) into columns \(V_k=[kh,(k+1)h)\times[0,1]\) and cells of side \(h=s\) (at most \((1/h+1)^2\le N\) cells for large \(N\)). With \(\mu=Nh^2=L^2\log N\) and \(K_0=e^2\mu+3\log N\), Chernoff's bound \(P(\operatorname{Bin}\ge t)\le(e\mu/t)^t\le e^{-t}\) for \(t\ge e^2\mu\) and a union bound give: with probability at least \(1-N^{-2}\) every cell holds at most \(K_0\) points of \(X\). In column \(k\), \(\Lambda\) lies between heights \(\min_{V_k}(g-su_2-\omega)\) and \(\max_{V_k}g\), an interval of length at most \(su_2+2O_k\), where \(O_k\) is the oscillation of \(g\) on \(W_k=[kh-su_1,(k+1)h+su_1]\cap[0,1]\); it meets at most \((su_2+2O_k)/h+2\) cells of the column. Since \(O_k\le\int_{W_k}|g'|\) and every point lies in at most \(2u_1+2\) of the windows \(W_k\), \(\sum_kO_k\le(2u_1+2)\int|g'|\le2(u_1+1)\kappa\). So \(\Lambda\) meets at most \((1/h+1)(u_2+2)+4(u_1+1)\kappa/h\le(4u_2+4(u_1+1)\kappa)/h\) cells once \(h\le1/3\) (using \(u_2\ge1\), so \((u_2+2)(1+h)\le3(1+h)u_2\le4u_2\)), and \[\operatorname{card}\{i:X_i\in\Lambda\}\le\frac{K_0}{s}\big(4u_2+4(u_1+1)\kappa\big)=\Big(e^2L+\frac3L\Big)\sqrt{N\log N}\,\big(4u_2+4\kappa u_1+4\kappa\big).\] Here \(\kappa u_1=(\log N)^{\beta_2}=u_2\) and \(\kappa\le1\le u_2\), so the right side is at most \(12(e^2L+3/L)\sqrt N(\log N)^{1/2+\beta_2}\).

Step 5: conclusion. On the intersection of the events of Steps 1, 2 and 4, which has probability tending to one, a matching good in both coordinates would give \[\frac{\sqrt N(\log N)^{3/4+(\beta_2-\beta_1)/2}}{L_1}-L_2\sqrt N\log\log N\le12\Big(e^2L+\frac3L\Big)\sqrt N(\log N)^{1/2+\beta_2}+2.\] The exponent on the left exceeds the one on the right by \(1/4-(\beta_1+\beta_2)/2>0\), and the left side's main term dominates \(\sqrt N\log\log N\) since its exponent is positive. So for \(N\) large (depending on \(L\) and the pair) the inequality fails, and no such matching exists. \(\square\)

What the bookkeeping shows on the curve. On the curve \(\beta_1+\beta_2=1/2\) the two exponents are equal: the excess and the layer bound are both of order \(\sqrt N(\log N)^{1/2+\beta_2}\), so the argument decides nothing there. Sharper constants (for instance a layer count without the term \(3\log N\) of \(K_0\)) could only exclude \(L\) below a universal constant, a lower bound on the conjecture's constant and not a refutation, since a refutation needs every \(L\). The construction is balanced exactly on the conjecture's curve, which is consistent with the conjecture; it neither proves nor refutes any pair on it.

Relation to earlier results. The theorem implies lem:common-exponent-cap (every pair in \((4,\infty]^2\) has \(\beta_1+\beta_2<1/2\), and "no good matching with probability tending to one" is stronger than "not with probability tending to one some good matching") and extends it to the two-sample setting and to every pair below the curve, for instance \((\alpha,\infty)\) for every \(\alpha>2\): Conjecture 5.5, the pair \((2,\infty)\), cannot be strengthened in its first exponent. It is the matching form of lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound(a), which shows the same threshold for the penalized discrepancy class; there the lower bound concerned a sufficient condition, here it concerns matchings directly, with the same Step 1. The passages of [T4] read (Sections 17.1–17.3, read in the monograph reading, which recorded no corresponding lower-bound argument there, and Section 4.8) and of the survey (Conjecture 5.8 and the paragraph before it, read by the author of this entry, not by its reviewer) do not state this necessity; the argument is a direct adaptation of [T4]'s proof of Theorem 4.8.1, and no novelty is claimed.

Degenerate cases. \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2=\infty\): \(\kappa=1\), \(u_1=u_2=1\), and the theorem says that no matching has both coordinate displacements at most \(L\sqrt{\log N/N}\), which the proof of [T4]'s Theorem 4.8.1 ((4.119) with (4.120)), with the dependency's repair of the low-level probabilities, also gives. Pairs on the curve are excluded by the strict inequality (see above). An exponent \(\alpha_j\le2\) is excluded since it forces \(\beta_1+\beta_2\ge1/2\). Small \(L\): the bound \(K_0\) keeps the term \(3\log N\), so the layer bound holds for every \(L>0\).

Nonvacuity. The pair \((3,\infty)\): \(\beta_1=1/3\), \(\beta_2=0\), \(\kappa=(\log N)^{-1/3}\), excess exponent \(3/4-1/6=7/12\) against layer exponent \(1/2\). The pair \((5,5)\): \(\kappa=1\), exponents \(3/4\) against \(1/2+1/5=7/10\).

New mathematics. Every exponent pair strictly below the conjecture's curve is excluded, with probability tending to one, for every constant and in both settings: the anisotropically scaled Leighton–Shor subgraph has an excess of \(X\) points that a matching with those displacement tails cannot carry across its graph. The proof bounds the crossing pairs by the points of an anisotropic boundary layer, counted by a grid, plus the at most two pairs with long displacements. On the curve the two sides balance exactly.

Goal progress. Unchanged. The theorem shows that the conjecture cannot be strengthened at any pair below the curve, in both settings, the question the item asked; it neither proves nor refutes the conjecture at any pair of the curve. Bearing. Nothing at the goal's parameters: on the curve the bound balances and decides nothing. The cycle was spent on it because it could have refuted pairs of the curve: had the layer been thinner or the excess larger by any unbounded factor, the same argument would have excluded pairs on the curve, refuting the conjecture there.

Record search. tools/search-claims.py for "matching lower bound subgraph Leighton Shor anisotropic exponent pair below curve excluded" returned lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound and lem:common-exponent-cap, both read above, and nothing else on matchings.

Verification gate. Dependencies read: Step 1 of lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound and its entry; (4.81)–(4.89) and Section 4.8 of [T4] in full; the good-matching definition and lem:common-exponent-cap in the monograph reading. One fresh-context medium reviewer received the draft, those excerpts and the claim spec; its report (review.txt) is kept with the results. It passed Steps 1–5, checking that only the inner claim of the dependency's Step 1 is used (so that lemma's hypotheses \(L\ge132\), \(\alpha_j\ge1\) are not needed), the exchange of coordinates in both settings, the Chernoff and union bounds, the column and window counts, the constant \(12\), and the extreme pairs; it found no unused hypothesis. Required changes made: \(g\) is placed in the class \(\mathcal C\) of (4.117) before Lemma 4.8.2 is applied; the lemma's constant renamed \(L_2\); at most one long displacement per coordinate; the expectation statement replaced by the proof of Theorem 4.8.1 in the degenerate case; the absence claims limited to the passages read, the survey's marked as the author's reading; the on-curve remark and the Bearing corrected. No follow-up pass: the corrections are wording and one citation hypothesis. Process audit. Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • No test-set argument that uses only the maximal displacements can refute a pair on the curve: rescaling each coordinate by \(u_j\) turns the max-only problem into the isotropic sup-norm Leighton–Shor problem on a rectangle of area \(1/(u_1u_2)\), whose predicted maximal displacement equals \(s/L\) on the curve, if the Leighton–Shor upper bound holds on rectangles of aspect ratio up to \((\log N)^{1/2}\) with a universal constant; a refutation would have to use the joint exponential moments. Record. The search "sup norm Leighton Shor rectangle aspect ratio maximal displacement joint exponential moments" returned only lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound and lem:common-exponent-cap.
  • Neither side of this argument can gain more than constants on the curve: a scaled Leighton–Shor upper bound caps the excess over scaled subgraphs, and optimizing the layer depth against the tails gives the same crossover at \(t\approx u_j\). Record. lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound bears on the excess side (same search).

Suggestion checked. Of the first suggestion, the scaling arithmetic was checked: dividing coordinate \(j\) by \(u_j\) maps \(\Omega\) to a rectangle of area \(A=1/(u_1u_2)=(\log N)^{-\beta_1-\beta_2}\) carrying \(N\) points, so the allowed displacements become \(s\) in both coordinates; a Leighton–Shor maximum at density \(N/A\) is of order \((\log N)^{3/4}\sqrt{A/N}=(\log N)^{3/4-(\beta_1+\beta_2)/2}/\sqrt N\), which equals \(\sqrt{\log N/N}=s/L\) exactly when \(\beta_1+\beta_2=1/2\), and exceeds every multiple of \(s\) below the curve, matching the theorem's threshold. Not checked: the Leighton–Shor upper bound on rectangles of aspect ratio up to \((\log N)^{1/2}\), on which the conclusion rests.

The proof relies on Step 1 of lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound, a recorded result, and on Lemma 4.8.2.

Not examined. The item names pairs with one exponent at most \(4\), the example \(\alpha_1>2\) at \(\alpha_2=\infty\), and an anisotropic form of the Leighton–Shor construction; all are examined, and the theorem covers every pair below the curve, the example included. Nothing it names is left.
