Third route review: a candidate coarea mechanism, with a test posed where gradient levels meet

16 October 2026 · Goal-level route review; no statement registered · Third route review. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

The line's general claim. The thread tests Talagrand's Ultimate Matching Conjecture (conj:ultimate-matching) on its curve; its route is the Hopf–Lax reduction to conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy and the flow form of lem:flow-matching-bound. Since the second review the route has been sharpened from both sides: the class form's refutation side, its order, and a sufficient condition for its converse.

What the goal needs. One matching at every pair of the curve with one universal \(L\), or a refutation at one pair; in the route's terms, a bound on the class (or a flow of bounded cost) at Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády size with a constant.

The six entries

The aligned superposition bound and the band overlap penalty decided the second review's test in the class form, for vertically scaled, positively oriented members: superposing Leighton–Shor test functions at several aspects gains at most \(1+o(1)\) over the best member. The Lipschitz baseline forced the class bound's order \(\sqrt{N\log N}\) at every pair, endpoints included. The threshold flow was dropped: a layered capacity needs a cut bound the record lacks. The tube density converse and the segment flow cost showed that a good matching of bounded tube density gives the class bound, and a flow of bounded cost through its segment flux. Every entry left the goal unchanged.

Falsification attempt. A refutation through the class form now needs a single test function beyond \(\frac L2\sqrt{N\log N}\) that is not a large-gradient superposition gaining on its members, and it refutes only the sufficient statement, the converse being proved only under the density condition; a refutation of the conjecture itself still needs a mixed level outside the low-level mixed counts or the coupling of levels in one matching. No candidate was tried in this review: the one family examined, band functions of width \(w\) over Leighton–Shor graphs, is bounded by the truncation argument of the Verification gate (unverified there) at \(\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\) per unit of perimeter, \(2^{-j}\) the width, which at amplitude and gradient within the class is of order at most \(\sqrt{N\log N}\) by that heuristic (the cost per unit length is about \(A(j+\log A)^{1/4}\) at amplitude \(A\), and the ratio \(\sqrt N(j^{1/2}+\sqrt{\log N}/j^{1/4})\)); the constant was not computed.

Assessment of this line. The line has made its route precise: the class bound is pinned in order, its refutation side reduced to single functions, and its relation to the goal reduced to one question (do good matchings have bounded tube density, or give bounded-cost flows). It has not produced a mechanism for any upper bound above exponent \(2\), and the open-item count has stayed at four since the first review, twelve research entries back: the line is not converging on its open statements, which is why this review is goal-level.

Obstacle

The line is stuck on producing, at one pair of the curve, a single object that controls all levels at once: a matching good at every level, or equivalently (up to the density condition) a flow whose \(\psi_4\)-Orlicz cost is bounded. Every construction on record is either linear in the empirical measure (Gaussian per-point values, which fail above exponent \(2\)) or serves one level, or two in the low-level mixed counts; the record has no nonlinear construction with a provable bound at every level, and the converse that would let a good matching certify the class needs a density control the record lacks. On the dual side the record has no upper-bound mechanism for the class at all.

Outside leads

  • Finite convex duality for Beckmann's problem on the grid (Fenchel–Rockafellar; the version needed is proved here). For a finite convex cost \(F\) on grid edge flows and the grid divergence \(A\), \(\inf\{F(\Phi):A\Phi=f\}=\sup_g\{\langle g,f\rangle-F^*(A^{\mathsf T}g)\}\) whenever \(f\) has total mass zero. It targets conjugate-class-discrepancy through C ⇒ F. Answers. It changes no implication toward the goal: C ⇒ M is already proved (lem:flow-matching-bound's bound is itself a dual supremum), and F ⇒ C with the segment flux already reduce the converse to the step from a matching to a flow. What it adds is on the refutation side: with C ⇔ F up to discretization, the class value at a pair is a finite convex min-cost-flow value, so a lower bound on every grid flow's cost, or a computed optimal grid flow, decides the grid class value there; deciding the continuous class needs the transfer the Break point leaves unchecked. Its gain over the record is small: a lower bound on all flows is the dual supremum restated. Test. Passed. Proof of the duality used: the value function \(v(f')=\inf\{F(\Phi):A\Phi=f'\}\) is convex and finite on the subspace of mass-zero \(f'\) (the grid is connected, \(F\) finite), hence continuous there and subdifferentiable at \(f\); a subgradient \(g\) gives \(F(\Phi)\ge v(f)+\langle g,A\Phi-f\rangle\) for all \(\Phi\), that is \(\langle g,f\rangle-F^*(A^{\mathsf T}g)\ge v(f)\), and weak duality gives the reverse. Break point. The grid problem is not the continuous one: a grid function \(g\) extended piecewise linearly on the triangles of the grid has, on each triangle with axis edges, gradient components equal to grid differences over \(\delta\), so its continuous penalty equals the grid penalty; but its pairing with the cell-smeared measures is a fixed average of vertex values (weights \(2,1,2,1\) over \(6\) on a split square), not \(\langle g,f\rangle\), and converting a grid flow on edges back into a bounded continuous flow for lem:flow-matching-bound was not checked.
  • Cyclical monotonicity of optimal matchings (the swap inequality, proved here in the form used). An optimal matching for a cost \(c\) cannot gain by exchanging two partners; for the quadratic cost this gives \(\langle a,a+b\rangle\ge0\) and hence separation of the moving points, \(|a+tb|\ge(1-t)|a|\), checked in the segment flow entry. It targets the converse's density condition. Answers. If optimal matchings for the exponential cost have separated moving points, their tube density is bounded by the cell density and the converse holds, so the route asks exactly the goal. Test. Not run: the test made (separation for the quadratic cost) concerns another cost; for the exponential cost no separation statement was attempted, and the item stays with the queued sub-idea entry-2026-10-16-monograph-statements:s3, whose latest follow-up names it. What was found: for a separable cost \(\sum_j\psi_j(|z_j|/s)\) the swap inequality gives separation only within the ratio of \(\psi''\) over the displacements involved; for \(\psi_4(u)=e^{u^4}-1\), \(\psi''(u)=(12u^2+16u^6)e^{u^4}\), which tends to \(0\) as \(u\to0\) and grows like \(e^{u^4}\): the ratio is unbounded between short and long displacements, so the quadratic argument does not transfer as it stands.
  • Ambrosio, Stra and Trevisan, "A PDE approach to a 2-dimensional matching problem" (arXiv:1611.04960; its main theorem read in this cycle): for the quadratic cost, \(\frac n{\log n}\mathbb EW_2^2(\mu^n,\nu^n)\to\frac1{2\pi}\), proved through the Poisson equation for the heat-regularized empirical difference and a Dacorogna–Moser coupling. It targets conjugate-class-discrepancy through a flow: \(\nabla f^{n,t}\). Answers. A flow defined by an elliptic equation is one object at all scales, which the obstacle asks for. Test. Not run: the flow \(\nabla f^{n,t}\) is linear in the empirical measure, the class of flows for which the dyadic flow obstruction proves Gaussian per-point lower tails only for the dyadic kernel; its per-point tails were not computed for the heat-regularized Poisson kernel. Retrieval. tools/arxiv_source.py fetched arXiv:1611.04960 and the main theorem and the strategy section were read; the per-point estimates of its later sections were not. Not picked, so dropped.

Absurd bridges

  • Congested traffic and Wardrop equilibria (transportation engineering; Brasco, Carlier and Santambrogio, "Congested traffic dynamics, weak flows and very degenerate elliptic equations", J. Math. Pures Appl. 2010, read in this cycle: Theorems 2.1 and 3.2). Theorem 3.2: for densities in \(L^p\), the minimal-flow problem \(\min\int H(\sigma)\), \(\operatorname{div}\sigma=\mu_0-\mu_1\), has the same value as the path problem over probability measures \(Q\) on curves whose traffic intensity \(i_Q\) is penalized by \(H\), the optimal \(Q\) being built from the optimal \(\sigma\) along \(\mu_t=(1-t)\mu_0+t\mu_1\) by the superposition principle; Theorem 2.1 gives \(\sigma=\nabla H^*(\nabla u)\) with \(u\) solving a very degenerate elliptic equation. Translation: our flow form is Beckmann's congested problem with \(H(\sigma)=\lambda'C(\sigma/\lambda')\), and traffic intensity is our tube density. Answers. It would answer the converse's density condition if the theory bounded the traffic intensity of a pattern built from a matching; Theorem 3.2 goes the other way, from an optimal flow to a traffic pattern, a direction the segment flux already gives (intensity at least \(|\sigma|\)). Test. Not run: what was checked is the theorem's hypotheses against ours, not the translation. Their analysis is for isotropic \(H(\sigma)=\frac1p|\sigma|^p+\lambda|\sigma|\), \(p\in(1,2]\), and densities in \(L^p\); ours is anisotropic and exponential, and the follow-up of Brasco and Carlier on anisotropic costs was not read. Theorem 3.2's construction couples smeared measures, not points, so it gives traffic patterns, not matchings. Retrieval. The paper was fetched from Carlier's page at CEREMADE (found by a web search) and its Theorems 2.1 and 3.2 were read; the anisotropic follow-up was not fetched. Not picked, so dropped.
  • Moment characterization of exponential Orlicz norms and \(p\)-Laplacian (nonlinear electrical) networks. A function \(f\) has \(\int e^{|f|^\alpha}\le2\) up to constants exactly when \(\|f\|_p\lesssim p^{1/\alpha}\) for all \(p\ge1\) (checked here in one direction: \(|f|^p\le(p/(e\alpha))^{p/\alpha}e^{|f|^\alpha}\), so \(\|f\|_p\le2^{1/p}(p/(e\alpha))^{1/\alpha}\); the other direction by expanding \(e^{|f|^\alpha}\) in moments). Translation: a flow of bounded \(\psi_4\)-cost is one flow whose \(L^p\) norms, in the reduction's units, grow at most like \(p^{1/4}\) for all \(p\le\log N\); for each fixed \(p\) the optimal flow is \(p'\)-harmonic, \(\Phi=|\nabla u|^{p'-2}\nabla u\) with \(\operatorname{div}\Phi=f\) (standard \(L^p\) duality for the divergence constraint; Theorem 2.1 above assumes \(\lambda>0\) and does not cover it). Answers. It would split the obstacle into a family of classical nonlinear potential problems, one per \(p\), with the coupling as the demand that one flow be nearly optimal for all \(p\) at once. Test. Falsified as an answer: the per-\(p\) statements (\(\min\|\Phi\|_p\lesssim p^{1/4}\) for each \(p\)) are not on record, and they are only necessary: the obstacle reappears as simultaneity across \(p\), the same coupling of levels in another form, so the translation restates the open problem.

One Outside lead passed, the grid duality, and is picked; no bridge passed, so none is picked. The other items are dropped.

Across the goal

  • ultimate-matching (the full target): unchanged since the thread opened; the six entries of this period went to its sufficient statement; eighteen research entries in the thread so far.
  • conjugate-class-discrepancy (sufficient): unchanged in status; its order forced at every pair, its refutation through superpositions closed in the class form for vertically scaled, positively oriented members, a sufficient condition for its converse; six cycles this period.
  • shor-exponential (Conjecture 5.5, the pair \((2,\infty)\)): unchanged; no cycles this period; the Lipschitz baseline covers its endpoint for the class bound's order.
  • grid-discrepancy (Research Problem 17.2.2 of [T4]): unchanged; no cycles this period.

Architecture review

Necessary target. The two-sample statement itself: for every pair \(\alpha_1,\alpha_2\in[2,\infty]\) on the curve and one universal \(L\), with \(X\), \(Y\) independent uniform samples, with probability tending to one a matching good in both coordinates; its negation, for every \(L\) some pair with \(\liminf\) of that probability below one. The evenly-spread form is a convenience: it implies the target (the transfer goes one way only). Downstream. Through lem:two-sample-matching-transfer the evenly-spread form gives the two-sample goal with constant \(2L\): the goal. Through cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction, conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy gives it: the goal. Through lem:flow-matching-bound, a flow of bounded cost gives it: the goal. Conversely, through lem:tube-density-converse and lem:segment-flow-cost, the goal plus a good matching of bounded tube density gives the class bound and a bounded-cost flow up to constants and grid: weaker than an equivalence.

Extra requirements. The route asks for a bound uniform over the conjugate class (or a flow of bounded cost); the goal asks for one matching. By the two lemmas above, the extra demand is at most the density condition on one good matching. Whether that demand is real is a question about optimal matchings: if a good matching exists, an optimal one for the cost \(C(z)=\sum_j\psi_{\alpha_j}(|z_j|/s)\) has \(\sum_j\sum_i\psi_{\alpha_j}\le2N\), so each coordinate's exponential sum is at most \(3N\), and it is good at constant \(2L\) (since \(\psi(x/c)\le\psi(x)/c\) for \(c\ge1\)); if optimal matchings have bounded tube density, the route asks exactly the goal up to constants.

Independent alternative. Before seeing the route, the medium reviewer received the goal, the survey's Section 5, the registered statements and the open conjectures with their evidence, and the earlier stage-one proposals so as not to repeat them (stage-one record). Its premise: flows linear in the sample have Gaussian marginals, so a proof must route nonlinearly using the sample. Its proposals, ranked: (1) a coarea splitting of a test function by gradient level, the level \(2^k\) treated as a boundary at scales coarser than \(2^{-k}\) (Leighton–Shor chaining over only those \(k\) scales, about \(\sqrt N\,k^{3/4}\) per unit length) and as a Lipschitz function at finer scales (Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády, about \(\sqrt{N\log N}\)), against a cost per unit length times \(k^{1/4}\) at \((4,4)\), which balances on the curve; (2) the variable-capacity max-flow min-cut form, which is the threshold flow dropped this period for want of a layered cut bound; (3) an entropy-number calibration of the class as a logarithmic Orlicz–Sobolev ball, heuristically showing Dudley's integral reaches \(\sqrt p\) exactly when \(\alpha\le2\). Comparison: (2) is on record and was dropped; (3) is a calibration of method limits, consistent with the dyadic flow obstruction; (1) is new: a candidate dual-side mechanism for an upper bound, aimed at the obstacle, where the route's own dual form had none. Its risk is the assembly: the level sets of a general \(G\) cross gradient levels, and the bound must hold uniformly over the layers.

Rank normalization. No dimension counts arise. The analogous question, whether the matching's cost and the flow's cost are measured in the same units, is answered by lem:segment-flow-cost: they are, at density level \(M\) and cost scale \(\lambda M/\lambda'\), when the tube density is at most \(M\).

Decision. Core: conjugate-class-discrepancy stays the core statement, worked next through the independent alternative's coarea mechanism, a candidate (heuristic, unverified) for an upper bound above exponent \(2\), the line's first, the obstacle on the dual side being the absence of one. Lemma first: the truncated Leighton–Shor bound for band functions over Leighton–Shor graphs, which the stage-two reviewer argued in a few lines up to details (Verification gate), is the next entry's lemma; the class bound for \(G\) at one gradient level is a conjectured lemma, unverified, and not immediate (inside a cell of side \(2^{-k}\) the oscillation of \(G\) is not bounded by the cell's gradient mass over the side); the decisive test is the step after them. The converse question (do optimal matchings have bounded tube density) stays queued with the item entry-2026-10-16-monograph-statements:s3, whose latest follow-up names it; it bounds the route's extra demand but would not prove anything at the goal's parameters. The two other open statements receive no cycles: Conjecture 5.5 is covered by the same route at its endpoint, and Research Problem 17.2.2 is [T4]'s own route, which [T4] reports blocked by the unknown size of its class. A Switch was weighed: the per-level and mixed-level results suggested working the conjecture directly through matchings, but every direct construction on record serves one level, and the record has no mechanism for the coupling there either.

Decisive test. Trivial cases. The quantity is \(D^{(2)}\), the supremum of \(\int G\,d(\mu_X-\mu_Y)\) over the class of conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy at \((4,4)\) restricted to \(G\) whose gradient, where nonzero, has its size in two dyadic levels \([2^{k_1},2^{k_1+1})\) and \([2^{k_2},2^{k_2+1})\), \(k_1<k_2\), against \(K\sqrt{N\log N}\) with \(K\) independent of \((k_1,k_2)\). Constant \(G\) gives zero, and reflections of the square map the class to itself. A split of \(G\) into single-level pieces with additive cost would reduce it to the single-level case, but a split by values (\(\min(G,t)\)) keeps both levels in each piece, a split by regions is not Lipschitz, and a partition of unity \(\varphi\) adds terms \(G\nabla\varphi\) the cost does not control; so no trivial instance decides it. The uniform-in-\(m\) version is not posed: gradient sizes form a continuum, so \(\sup_mD^{(m)}\) is the class value at \((4,4)\) by approximation, the open statement itself. Power. The record has no upper bound on the class. The Lipschitz baseline bounds the full class value from below by \(\sqrt{N\log N}/(4L_0)\); whether its witnesses have gradients at two levels was not checked, so no lower bound on \(D^{(2)}\) is claimed. No computation: the outcomes differ by a factor depending on \(k_2-k_1\), at most about \(\log N\), a few units at reachable sizes. Attempt. Tried before posing it: average \(G\) on a mesh chosen per level, \(w_i=2^{-k_i}\), and bound the averaged part by the truncated bound and the remainder cellwise, as in the stage-two reviewer's band argument. A coarse cell meeting the high-gradient region has remainder oscillation at most \(w_12^{k_1}\) plus \(2^{k_2}\) times the length of the high-gradient region on a segment through the cell, so the charge is the gradient mass along segments, not the region's boundary length (the reviewer's correction of the first attempt). A Calderón–Zygmund stop on \(\operatorname{side}(Q)\cdot\operatorname{avg}_Q|\nabla G|\le1\) gives only \(L^1\) control of \(G-\operatorname{avg}_QG\) by Poincaré, not sup control; inside each stopped cell the remainder lies in a \(W^{1,1}\) ball, whose discrepancy carries the Leighton–Shor factor \((\log N)^{3/4}\), not \(\sqrt{\log N}\). It stopped there: the remainders inside stopped cells, not the interfaces. Test disposition. New operation. Operation. Prove \(D^{(2)}\le K\sqrt{N\log N}\) at \((4,4)\) with \(K\) independent of \((k_1,k_2)\), bounding the stopped-cell remainders through the band truncation applied to their level sets inside the cells (coarea within the cells) rather than through Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády; or find \(G\) at two levels whose stopped-cell remainders carry the \((\log N)^{3/4}\) factor at a cost the class allows. Prediction. If the cell-wise coarea closes the recursion with a constant loss at two levels, the mechanism assembles levels pairwise, and the next question is whether the loss stays bounded over many levels; if some two-level \(G\) carries the factor \((\log N)^{3/4}/\sqrt{\log N}\) through its cell remainders, the mechanism fails as posed, and either the class bound fails at \((4,4)\) (which refutes only the sufficient statement, not the conjecture) or the mesh must follow the level sets of \(G\). Record search. tools/search-claims.py "truncated Leighton Shor band averaged discrepancy coarea gradient level scales coarser than width" returned lem:anisotropic-leighton-shor-bound, conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy and lem:band-overlap-penalty; "two gradient levels coarea variable mesh cell average interface discrepancy class bound single level" returned conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy, lem:segment-flow-cost and cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction; none bounds the class on a restricted gradient set.

Builds. This period queued seven builds, passed-mark-target-check with this review, and made one (finisher-not-run-retrieval, closed); six remain queued. passed-mark-target-check waits for the same framework turn as the others, since a finisher change needs the whole suite and its own timed run. The others wait, each with its reason, dated 16 October 2026: reviewed-text-hash (records the reviewed draft's hash and compares it at the finisher) waits for the framework turn after this review, since it touches the reviewer-brief hook and the finisher together; error-scale-line, hypothesis-instance-check and equivalence-word-check all extend the append tool's summing-up checks and wait to be built together in that turn, so that they share one sentence classifier; append-time-placement-checks waits with them, as it moves a finisher check into the same tool.

New mathematics. None registered; the leads' and bridges' calculations are recorded with their items.

Goal progress. Unchanged. Bearing. Nothing at the goal's parameters: the review proves nothing; it was spent to decide whether the line should keep its core statement, given four open items unchanged over twelve research entries, and to name the test that would change that.

Review. The stand-in review did not run at the last checkpoint; nothing is owed.

Verification gate. Stage one: the medium reviewer proposed the alternatives from the goal and the registered results alone (record linked above). Stage two: the same reviewer read the draft, the commit message, the sources read for the leads (Brasco–Carlier–Santambrogio Theorems 2.1 and 3.2; Ambrosio–Stra–Trevisan's main theorem) and Section 4.7 of [T4], and failed it as drafted. It confirmed the grid duality proof, the piecewise linear weights, the \(\psi_4''\) formula, the quadratic separation, the moment bound and the stage-one representation. Corrections made: the grid duality's Answers no longer claims a change toward the goal; cyclical monotonicity and congested traffic are Not run (their tests concerned another cost, or the theorem's hypotheses alone) and the \(p\)-Laplacian bridge is Falsified as a restatement, so only the grid duality is picked and the Ambrosio–Stra–Trevisan lead is dropped rather than queued; the Falsification attempt says no candidate was tried; the Obstacle says "the record lacks"; the thread's count of research entries comes from the notebook; the Necessary target is the two-sample statement, the evenly-spread form a convenience; the optimal matching is good at constant \(2L\); the coarea mechanism is called a candidate; the Builds counts are reconciled; and the decisive test moved. Attempt checked. The reviewer restricted the chaining of Section 4.7 to a mesh \(2^{-j}\): curves of length at most \(2^k\) traced on that mesh number at most \(4^{2^{k+j+1}}\), so Lemma 4.7.9 applies with \(m\approx k+j\); \(\gamma_{1,2}\le L2^{2k}\) (Proposition 4.7.10) does not depend on the mesh; \(\gamma_1(d_\infty)\le L2^{k+j}\); so unions of mesh cells have discrepancy at most \(L\ell\sqrt N j^{3/4}\) for boundary length \(\ell\). Splitting \(G\) into its mesh average (level sets of average perimeter about \(\ell\)) and a remainder Lipschitz on each cell (Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády rescaled) gives, up to details, \(B_w\le K\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\). The original test, the truncated bound itself, was thereby answered in a few lines, and it also showed the draft's trivial case at width of order one wrong (there \(B_w\asymp\sqrt N\), the class being one-variable Lipschitz functions); so the truncated bound for bands becomes the next entry's lemma, the single-level class bound a conjectured lemma, and the decisive test moved to the assembly across gradient levels, which the reviewer named the step where the mechanism can fail. The reviewer read the corrected draft and passed it with corrections, all made in its wording: the grid duality decides the grid class value only; the single-level bound is not its argument; "uniform in \(m\)" restates the open statement at \((4,4)\), since gradient sizes form a continuum, so the decisive instance is \(m=2\) with \(K\) independent of \((k_1,k_2)\); the interface charge is the gradient mass along segments, not boundary length; a Calderón–Zygmund stop gives only \(L^1\) control, so the risk is recursion inside the stopped cells; the baseline's lower bound is restricted to many levels; the falsification constant is an order; the title says the test is posed. Process audit. It found the picks resting on Passed marks that did not test the translation, the heuristic adopted as Core without trying the step the draft's own Attempt named although the source was at hand, endpoint orders quoted unchecked, an unsourced count, and a commit message contradicting the draft; the title was a placeholder, written after the review from the body; strength count 7, all corrected. On the corrected draft it found a suggestion of its own promoted to a lemma unverified, a test restating the open statement through a parameter, the title stating the test as done, and the commit message implying the single-level bound settled; strength count 6, all corrected.

Process assessment. Outcome words (Passed, Core, "first mechanism") were written before the content they label was examined, and a test was posed whose step I had not tried with the source in hand. Prevention. The Passed mark resting on a test of another object or of the theorem alone passed because the finisher checks only that a Break point exists; the build passed-mark-target-check, queued with this review, makes the finisher refuse such a mark (it waits for the framework turn the Builds paragraph names, since a finisher change needs the whole suite and its own timed run on the record). The unchecked endpoint orders and the unsourced count fall under the existing rule that sentences holding a count are read beside their source (tools/draft_claim_sentences.py), which was not run on the first draft before the brief; it was run on the corrected one.

Meta. The habit is labelling before examining: an outcome word, a pick, a decision or a title is written while drafting, from the hoped-for result, and the evidence is then written to fill it. It showed here in the Passed marks and the Core decision, and earlier today in titles rewritten after review and in an equivalence claimed from one direction. The change is the drafting order, now a rule in AGENTS.md: an item's outcome word is written last, from what its Break point found, and a review's Decision only after its Attempt has tried the named step with the sources at hand.

Leighton–Shor discrepancy on a coarser mesh for curves and cell functions

16 October 2026 · Working proof; two-level test not decided · Lemma first, then the two-level test. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Where we stand. Unchanged: The entry adds a tool lemma and an undecided test; the goal's state in Where we stand is unchanged.

Question. The third route review asked first for the Leighton–Shor bound truncated at a coarser mesh, argued by its reviewer up to details, and then for the class bound at \((4,4)\) for \(G\) at two gradient levels with a constant independent of the levels. The missing implication is conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy at \((4,4)\); this entry proves the truncated bound in a form that applies to every function constant on the cells of a mesh, and makes the two-level attempt.

General statement. lem:mesh-function-discrepancy, for every \(N\), every mesh \(2^{-j}\) between \(1/2\) and the Leighton–Shor scale, and every function constant on the mesh cells.

Mesh-function discrepancy

Setting. \(X_1,\dots,X_N\) i.i.d. uniform on \([0,1]^2\). As in Section 4.7 of [T4], \(\ell_1\) is the largest integer with \(2^{-\ell_1}\ge(\log N)^{3/4}/\sqrt N\). For an integer \(j\) with \(1\le j\le\ell_1\), \(\mathcal G_j\) is the grid of mesh \(2^{-j}\) (the points with \(2^jx_1\in\mathbb Z\) or \(2^jx_2\in\mathbb Z\)), whose open squares are the cells. For \(H\) constant on each cell and a real \(c\), \[V_j^c(H)=\sum_{e\text{ inner edge}}2^{-j}|H(Q_e)-H(Q'_e)|+\sum_{e\text{ edge on }\partial[0,1]^2}2^{-j}|H(Q_e)-c|,\] \(Q_e,Q'_e\) the cells beside \(e\), and \(V_j(H)=\inf_cV_j^c(H)\).

Statement. There is a universal \(L\) such that with probability tending to one, simultaneously for every \(j\) with \(1\le j\le\ell_1\): (a) every closed simple curve \(C\) traced on \(\mathcal G_j\) has \[\Big|\sum_{i\le N}\big(1_{\mathring C}(X_i)-\lambda(\mathring C)\big)\Big|\le L\sqrt N\,\ell(C)\Big(\big(\log_2(2^{j+3}\ell(C))\big)^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\Big);\] (b) every \(H\) constant on the cells of \(\mathcal G_j\) has \[\Big|\sum_{i\le N}H(X_i)-N\int H\Big|\le L\sqrt N\big(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\big)\,V_j(H).\] In (a) the factor is the logarithm of the curve's length in mesh units, so a short curve on a fine mesh pays only for the scales between its length and the mesh. (c) For every \(H\) constant on the cells, \(V_j(H)\le4\,V^{\mathrm{in}}_j(H)\), \(V^{\mathrm{in}}_j\) the inner part of \(V_j^c\) (the sum over inner edges); so in (b) the boundary ring costs at most a constant factor. At \(j=\ell_1\), (a) has Theorem 4.7.2's order \((\log N)^{3/4}\) for curves, with the weaker probability \(1-LN^{-2}\log N\).

Proof. Step 1: curves on a coarser mesh. For a vertex \(\tau\) of \(\mathcal G_j\) and an integer \(k\) with \(2-j\le k\le j+2\), let \(\mathcal C_j(\tau,k)\) be the closed simple curves traced on \(\mathcal G_j\) through \(\tau\) of length at most \(2^k\) (every closed simple curve on \(\mathcal G_j\) has length between \(4\cdot2^{-j}\) and \(2(2^j+1)\le2^{j+2}\), so these \(k\) cover them all). We show that with probability at least \(1-LN^{-3}\), every \(C\in\mathcal C_j(\tau,k)\) has \[\Big|\sum_{i\le N}\big(1_{\mathring C}(X_i)-\lambda(\mathring C)\big)\Big|\le L2^k\sqrt N\big((k+j+1)^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\big).\tag{1}\] With \(k\) the least integer with \(\ell(C)\le2^k\), so \(2^k\le2\ell(C)\) and \(k+j+1\le\log_2(2^{j+3}\ell(C))\), this is (a). A curve of \(\mathcal C_j(\tau,k)\) has at most \(2^{k+j}\) edges, so as in Lemma 4.7.8, \(\operatorname{card}\mathcal C_j(\tau,k)\le4^{2^{k+j}}=N_{m}\) with \(m=k+j+1\le2j+3\) (\(N_m=2^{2^m}\)). Since \(j\le\ell_1\), \(\mathcal G_j\subset\mathcal G_{\ell_1}\), so \(\mathcal C_j(\tau,k)\subset\mathcal C(\tau,k)\), the class of Proposition 4.7.10, and \(\gamma_{1,2}(\mathcal C_j(\tau,k),d_1)\le\gamma_{1,2}(\mathcal C(\tau,k),d_1)\le L2^{2k}\) (restricting an admissible sequence to a subset does not increase diameters). Proposition 4.7.10 is used here for \(k\) down to \(2-j\), below the range \(k\ge-1\) the monograph needs; its proof (Lemma 4.7.11, Section B.2 of [T4], read in this cycle) parametrizes a curve of length \(2^k\) by \(f\) through the factor \(2^{k+1}\) and bounds the swept area by \(L2^{2k}\|f_0-f_1\|_2\), with no restriction on \(k\), and Lemma 4.7.12 carries it to \(\gamma_{1,2}\). Lemma 4.7.9 with this \(m\) gives, for the class \(\mathcal F\) of the functions \(1_{\mathring C}\), \(\gamma_2(\mathcal F,d_2)=\gamma_2(\mathcal C_j(\tau,k),\sqrt{d_1})\le m^{3/4}\gamma_{1,2}^{1/2}\le L2^k(k+j+1)^{3/4}\); and as in Proposition 4.7.7, \(\gamma_1(\mathcal F,d_\infty)\le L2^m=L2^{k+j+1}\le L2^k\sqrt N\), since \(2^j\le2^{\ell_1}\le\sqrt N\). Theorem 4.5.16 needs \(0\) in the class; adding it changes \(\gamma_2(\mathcal F,d_2)\) by at most \(L\,d_2(0,\mathcal F)\le L2^k\) (the area of a curve of length \(2^k\) is at most \(2^{2k}\)) and \(\gamma_1(\mathcal F,d_\infty)\) only through \(N_m\) becoming \(N_{m+1}\), so it gives \(\mathbb E\sup_{C}|\sum_i(1_{\mathring C}(X_i)-\lambda(\mathring C))|\le L2^k\sqrt N(k+j+1)^{3/4}\). For \(k\ge0\), the bounded-differences inequality (4.107) with \(u=L2^k\sqrt N\sqrt{\log N}\) adds this deviation with probability at least \(1-2\exp(-L^22^{2k}\log N/2)\ge1-N^{-3}\) for \(L\ge3\). For \(k<0\) the curves lie in the square \(V\) of centre \(\tau\) and side \(2^{k+1}\); as in the proof of Proposition 4.7.3, conditionally on \(I=\{i:X_i\in V\}\) the same inequality holds with \(\operatorname{card}I\) in place of \(N\), which bounds \(\sum_{i\in I}(1_{\mathring C}(X_i)-\lambda(\mathring C)/\lambda(V))\) as in (1) when \(\operatorname{card}I\le L2^{2k}N\); the points outside \(V\) leave the term \((\operatorname{card}I-N\lambda(V))\lambda(\mathring C)/\lambda(V)\), at most \(|\operatorname{card}I-N\lambda(V)|\), which Bernstein's inequality bounds by \(5\cdot2^k\sqrt{N\log N}\) with probability at least \(1-2N^{-3}\) (variance \(N\lambda(V)\le2^{2k+2}N\), so the exponent is at least \(\frac{25}8\log N\) up to the Bernstein correction, and this deviation is below the variance for large \(N\) since \(2^k\sqrt N\ge4(\log N)^{3/4}\)); and \(\mathbb P(\operatorname{card}I\ge L2^{2k}N)\le\exp(-N2^{2k})\le\exp(-16N2^{-2j})\le\exp(-16(\log N)^{3/2})\) because \(k\ge2-j\) and \(j\le\ell_1\). There are at most \(\sum_{j\le\ell_1}(2^j+1)^2(2j+5)\le LN\log N\) triples \((j,\tau,k)\), so (1) holds for all of them with probability at least \(1-LN^{-2}\log N\).

Step 2: unions of cells. Let \(A\) be a union of cells of \(\mathcal G_j\) and orient its boundary edges with \(A\) on the left. At each vertex as many oriented boundary edges enter as leave, so the edges split into closed directed walks; a walk that visits a vertex twice splits there into two closed walks, so the edges split into closed simple curves \(C_1,\dots,C_r\) traced on \(\mathcal G_j\), each edge in exactly one, \(\sum_s\ell(C_s)=\ell(\partial A)\). For \(x\) off the grid, the winding number of the 1-chain \(\sum_sC_s=\partial A\) about \(x\) is \(1_A(x)\), and that of an oriented simple closed curve is \(\pm1\) inside and \(0\) outside, so \(1_A=\sum_s\varepsilon_s1_{\mathring C_s}\) off the grid, \(\varepsilon_s=\pm1\). The \(X_i\) avoid the grid almost surely, and by (a), with \(\log_2(2^{j+3}\ell(C_s))\le2j+5\) since \(\ell(C_s)\le2^{j+2}\), \[\Big|\sum_i1_A(X_i)-N\lambda(A)\Big|\le\sum_s\Big|\sum_i\big(1_{\mathring C_s}(X_i)-\lambda(\mathring C_s)\big)\Big|\le L\sqrt N\big(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\big)\ell(\partial A).\]

Step 3: layer cake. Constants have zero discrepancy, so for every \(c\), \(H-c=\int_c^\infty1_{\{H>t\}}dt-\int_{-\infty}^c1_{\{H\le t\}}dt\), and \(\{H>t\}\) and \(\{H\le t\}\) are unions of cells (off the grid). An inner edge lies on the boundary of \(\{H>t\}\) for \(t\) between the values of its two cells, an edge on \(\partial[0,1]^2\) for \(t\) between \(c\) and its cell's value, so \(\int\ell(\partial\{H>t\})dt\) over \(t\ge c\) plus \(\int\ell(\partial\{H\le t\})dt\) over \(t<c\) equals \(V^c_j(H)\). Step 2 inside the integrals and the infimum over \(c\) give (b).

Step 4: the boundary ring. Let \(A\) be a union of cells with \(\lambda(A)\le\frac12\); write \(h\) and \(v\) for the lengths of its horizontal and vertical inner boundary edges, \(F_c\) for the total width of the columns of cells wholly in \(A\) and \(F_r\) for the total height of the rows wholly in \(A\). In a column whose bottom cell lies in \(A\), going up, either every cell lies in \(A\) or a first horizontal inner edge has a cell of \(A\) below and one outside above; going down from a top cell in \(A\) finds an edge with \(A\) above, a different edge. So the boundary edges of \(A\) on the bottom and top sides have length at most \(h+2F_c\), and on the left and right sides at most \(v+2F_r\). Since \(\lambda(A)\le\frac12\), \(F_r\le\frac12\); if \(F_c>0\), every row not wholly in \(A\) contains a cell of a full column and a cell outside \(A\), hence a vertical inner boundary edge, and these rows have total height at least \(\frac12\), so \(F_c\le\frac12\le v\); likewise \(F_r\le h\). The ring of \(A\) has length at most \(3(h+v)\). Take \(c\) a median of \(H\): for \(t\ge c\), \(\lambda\{H>t\}\le\frac12\), and for \(t<c\), \(\lambda\{H\le t\}\le\frac12\). In Step 3's layer cake the ring of each set is at most three times its inner boundary, so \(V^c_j(H)\le4V^{\mathrm{in}}_j(H)\), which is (c). \(\square\)

Hypotheses, each with its step. \(X_i\) i.i.d. uniform: Theorem 4.5.16 and the bounded-differences inequality (4.107) in Step 1, which need independence and the uniform law, nothing about the \(X_i\) beyond that. \(j\le\ell_1\): Step 1, twice, through \(2^j\le\sqrt N\) (the \(\gamma_1\) bound) and \(N2^{-2j}\ge(\log N)^{3/2}\) (the count of points in small squares for \(k<0\)); the weakest form is these two inequalities. \(j\ge1\): only so that the grid has more than one cell; (a) and (b) hold for \(j=0\) trivially. \(H\) constant on cells: Step 3, where the level sets must be unions of cells. Conclusions (a) and (b) hold for every \(j\) in the range and every curve or cell function simultaneously; (c) is deterministic and holds for every cell function of every mesh.

Corollary for cell averages. For Lipschitz \(G\) and \(E_jG\) its average on each cell, the inner part of \(V_j^c(E_jG)\) is at most \(2\int(|\partial_1G|+|\partial_2G|)\). Proof: for cells \(Q,Q'=Q+we\) side by side, \(w=2^{-j}\), \(e\) a unit coordinate vector, \(|E_jG(Q)-E_jG(Q')|=|\operatorname{avg}_{z\in Q}\int_0^1\partial_eG(z+twe)\,w\,dt|\le\frac1w\int_0^1\int_{Q+twe}|\partial_eG|\,dt\le\frac1w\int_{Q\cup Q'}|\partial_eG|\); times the edge length \(w\), and each point lies in at most two such pairs per direction. With (c), \(V_j(E_jG)\le8\int(|\partial_1G|+|\partial_2G|)\).

Degenerate cases. \(H\) constant: \(V_j^c(H)=0\) at \(c=H\), and the left side is \(0\). \(j=\ell_1\): Theorem 4.7.2's order \((\log N)^{3/4}\), since \(\ell_1^{3/4}\) is of that order. \(j=1\): four cells; the bound is \(L\sqrt{N\log N}\,V_1(H)\); the \(\sqrt{\log N}\) term comes from the union bound over the triples \((j,\tau,k)\) in Step 1, not from the chaining. A mesh finer than \(2^{-\ell_1}\): excluded; Step 1's count of points in small squares needs \(N2^{-2j}\ge(\log N)^{3/2}\).

Nonvacuity. The lemma does not depend on the exponent pair; for context, on the curve's parameters, with \(N\) large: \(j=\lfloor\frac13\log_2N\rfloor\), which is at most \(\ell_1\) once \(N^{-1/3}\ge(\log N)^{3/4}/\sqrt N\), and \(H\) the indicator of the left half of the square, with \(V_j(H)\le3\) (inner variation \(1\), boundary ring \(2\) at \(c=\frac12\)), within (c)'s \(4\cdot1\); (b) bounds its discrepancy by \(3L\sqrt N((\frac13\log_2N)^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\). For (a), the boundary of a single cell of \(\mathcal G_j\), of length \(2^{2-j}\), has factor \(\log_2(2^{j+3}2^{2-j})=5\).

Architecture test

Architecture test. Not decided. The third route review's test (the class bound at \((4,4)\) for \(G\) at two gradient levels, constant independent of the levels) was attempted and neither outcome occurred: no bound and no counterexample. What was tried: split \(G\) as \(E_jG+R\), \(R=G-E_jG\), at a mesh \(2^{-j}\). By (b), (c) and the Corollary the cell-averaged part has discrepancy at most \(L\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\|\nabla G\|_{L^1}\), whatever the gradient levels. At \((4,4)\) the class allows \(\|\nabla G\|_{L^1}\) of order \(k^{-1/4}\) for \(G\) whose gradient sits at level \(2^k\), since \(\psi^*_4(y)\) is of order \(y(\log y)^{1/4}\) for large \(y\); so this part is of order \(\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})k^{-1/4}\), at most of order \(\sqrt{N\log N}\) when \(j\) is at most of order \(k^{1/3}(\log N)^{2/3}\) (order estimates; constants not computed). For two levels \(k_1<k_2\), part (b) at a single mesh is too weak: a mesh fine enough for the remainder at level \(k_2\) (an expectation, not argued) charges the coarse level \(j^{3/4}\) against its weight \(k_1^{1/4}\). The remainder \(R\) has mean zero on each cell; bounding each cell by the Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády estimate at scale \(yw\), \(w=2^{-j}\), and summing gives about \(y\,\lambda(\mathcal N_w(\operatorname{supp}\nabla G))\sqrt{N\log N}\), \(\mathcal N_w\) the \(w\)-neighbourhood, which is of the needed order exactly when that neighbourhood has area comparable to the support (a reviewer's sketch, unverified, including whether the per-cell tails hold simultaneously). The attempt stopped at supports thinner than the mesh: there the remainder holds structure finer than \(w\), and those thin, wiggly supports are the Leighton–Shor objects. Part (a), whose factor is the logarithm of a boundary component's length in mesh units, is the candidate tool for that case, applied to level-set components on a finer mesh; it was not applied in this entry, and no argument for that step was found.

Record search. tools/search-claims.py "Leighton Shor mesh coarser grid curves traced union of cells discrepancy perimeter layer cake" returned lem:anisotropic-leighton-shor-bound, conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy and lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound, none a mesh-truncated bound. tools/claim_resemblance.py on the spec: conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy (0.28, related in the spec), cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction (0.25) and lem:tube-density-converse (0.18), none a discrepancy bound for sets or cell functions.

New mathematics. lem:mesh-function-discrepancy: on a mesh \(2^{-j}\), a curve's Leighton–Shor factor is the logarithm of its length in mesh units plus \(\sqrt{\log N}\); a function constant on the cells has discrepancy at most \(L\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\) times its cell variation, whose boundary ring is at most three times its inner part (median constant); the cell averages of a Lipschitz \(G\) have variation at most \(8\|\nabla G\|_{L^1}\).

Goal progress. Unchanged: no implication toward conj:ultimate-matching changed; the lemma is a tool for the coarea mechanism, whose assembly is not done. Bearing. On the curve's parameters at \((4,4)\), the cell-averaged part of any \(G\) in the class, at mesh \(2^{-j}\), has discrepancy at most \(L\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\|\nabla G\|_{L^1}\). Not covered. The remainder \(G-E_jG\) inside the cells, for every shape of the gradient's support.

Review. The stand-in review of the last checkpoint raised five objections. (1) A deferred owed attempt whose reason called for running it got an instance fix only: right; queued as the build owed-deferral-brief, which puts each deferral beside its named attempt in the reviewer brief. (2) The Passed-mark check could have been built in the turn, the suite taking under ten seconds: right; built and committed as the finisher check closing passed-mark-target-check. (3) Named attempts whose outcome is already fixed were left to the reviewer: right in part; a mechanical check cannot read whether a bound decides an attempt, so the same queued build carries these sentences to the brief. (4) A Meta promising better use of an existing rule committed nothing: right; the Meta of the third route review names a rule added to AGENTS.md. (5) The stage-one lead was adopted as the decisive test before the Attempt was made: right; AGENTS.md now says that a Decision is written after its Attempt has tried the named step with the sources at hand.

Meta. The right objections share one way of working: when a mistake is found, I choose the cheapest response that lets the turn end (a queued item, a sentence promising care, the reviewer as the safeguard) rather than the change the user's stated priority asks for, a built check. It shows also in deferring owed attempts and in adopting another's lead without trying it. The change: a prevention is built in the turn unless the entry names what blocks it, and the reviewer now audits each queued prevention for that reason (.claude/agents/medium-reviewer.md).

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • A smaller deviation term: in Step 1 take \(u=L2^k\sqrt N\sqrt{j+\log\log N}\); each triple then fails with probability at most \(e^{-L'(j+\log\log N)}\), the sum over \(j\) of \(4^j(2j+5)e^{-L'j}(\log N)^{-L'}\) tends to \(0\), and (b) would hold with \(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log\log N}\), removing the \(\sqrt{\log N}\,k^{-1/4}\) term from the attempt's estimate. Record. tools/search-claims.py "Leighton Shor mesh coarser grid curves traced union of cells discrepancy perimeter layer cake" found no claim with this deviation term.

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: Section 4.7 of [T4] (Theorem 4.7.2, Propositions 4.7.3–4.7.10, Lemmas 4.7.8–4.7.9, (4.107)), Theorem 4.5.16, and Section B.2 (the proof of Lemma 4.7.11, for its range in \(k\)). One fresh-context medium reviewer read the draft, spec, commit message and these excerpts, found Steps 1–3 and the Corollary's inner bound correct and no unused hypothesis, and passed it with corrections, all made: the remark that the boundary ring is not controlled was false, and Step 4 now proves (c) (the reviewer pointed to the median and the relative isoperimetric inequality; the grid proof here is mine); Step 1 bounds the outside-\(V\) term for \(k<0\) by Bernstein's inequality, justifies adding \(0\) to the class, and states the range of Proposition 4.7.10 from Section B.2; the comparison with Theorem 4.7.2 is restricted to curves with its weaker probability; in the Architecture test a false bump estimate and a false packing premise are removed, and "one mesh does not serve both levels" is narrowed to the weakness of (b) at one mesh; the Nonvacuity no longer ties the lemma to an exponent pair. The same reviewer read the corrected draft: Step 4 correct (column and row walks, \(F_c,F_r\le\lambda(A)\le\frac12\), the median, \(V\le4V^{\mathrm{in}}\), the Corollary's \(8\) and the Nonvacuity's \(3\le4\)); the adding-\(0\) sentence correct; the Bernstein deviation needed the factor \(5\), now in; it accepted the Section B.2 summary without reading B.2. Process audit. It proposed the title "Leighton–Shor discrepancy on a coarser mesh for curves and cell functions", which the title follows; strength count 4 on the first draft ("Theorem 4.7.2 up to the constant", the commit line on one mesh, the spec's "extremal objects", "(a) is the tool … for both") and 2 on the corrected one (a check credited to the reviewer that it had not made, and Not covered with the commit message implying the thick-support case proved), all corrected; it found the ring question unasked, the median being the obvious check.

Process assessment. A negative remark (the ring "not bounded in general") was written without trying the obvious construction against it, and a heuristic rested on a premise (diameter bounds length) never checked on an example. Prevention. The reviewer definition now audits a statement shaped by its prompting instance whose other inputs were not tried (.claude/agents/medium-reviewer.md, committed this cycle); a negative claim about what a quantity does "in general" is such a statement, its other inputs being the free choices (here the constant \(c\)).

Meta. The habit is asserting a limit of a method before trying to beat it: "not bounded in general", "one mesh does not serve", "the extremal objects", each written where an attempt of a few lines would have tested it. It shows also in deferrals and in tests posed without attempts. The change: a sentence saying something fails, or cannot be had, carries the attempt that failed or is marked untested; AGENTS.md's rule that a general belief is no evidence covers it, and the Meta of this entry adds it to the reviewer's audit list (.claude/agents/medium-reviewer.md).

Discrepancy of Lipschitz functions split into cell averages and cellwise Lipschitz constants

16 October 2026 · Working proof; against Lebesgue measure · Single gradient level. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Where we stand. Unchanged: The entry adds a tool lemma and an undecided test; the goal's state in Where we stand is unchanged.

Question. The mesh-function discrepancy entry bounded the cell-averaged part of a class function and left the remainder inside the cells open. Does the class bound at \((4,4)\) hold for \(G\) at one gradient level? The missing implication is conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy at \((4,4)\); this entry bounds the remainder by the local Lipschitz constants and finds which single-level functions that settles.

General statement. lem:cellwise-lipschitz-split, for every Lipschitz function on the square and every mesh between \(1/2\) and the Leighton–Shor scale.

Cellwise Lipschitz split

Setting. As in lem:mesh-function-discrepancy: \(X_1,\dots,X_N\) i.i.d. uniform on \([0,1]^2\), \(\ell_1\) the Leighton–Shor scale, \(\mathcal G_j\) the grid of mesh \(w=2^{-j}\), \(1\le j\le\ell_1\). For Lipschitz \(G\), \(\|\nabla G\|_1=\int(|\partial_1G|+|\partial_2G|)\), and \(\Lambda_j(G)=\sum_Q\operatorname{Lip}(G|_Q)\,\lambda(Q)\) over the cells \(Q\), \(\operatorname{Lip}\) the Euclidean Lipschitz constant (zero on a cell where \(G\) is constant).

Statement. There is a universal \(L\) such that with probability tending to one, for every \(j\) with \(1\le j\le\ell_1\) and every bounded \(G\) on \([0,1]^2\) that is Lipschitz on each open cell of \(\mathcal G_j\), \[\Big|\sum_{i\le N}G(X_i)-N\int G\Big|\le L\sqrt N\big(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\big)V_j(E_jG)+L\sqrt{N\log N}\,\Lambda_j(G),\] \(E_jG\) the average of \(G\) on each cell and \(V_j\) the cell variation of lem:mesh-function-discrepancy. For \(G\) Lipschitz on \([0,1]^2\), \(V_j(E_jG)\le8\|\nabla G\|_1\), so the first term is at most \(8L\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\|\nabla G\|_1\).

Proof. Write \(G=E_jG+R\), \(E_jG\) the average of \(G\) on each cell. Since \(\int E_jG=\int G\), lem:mesh-function-discrepancy (b) bounds \(|\sum_iE_jG(X_i)-N\int G|\) by the first term; for Lipschitz \(G\), (c) and that lemma's corollary give \(V_j(E_jG)\le8\|\nabla G\|_1\), the only step that uses Lipschitz continuity across cell edges. The remainder has \(\int_QR=0\) on each cell, so \(\sum_iR(X_i)-N\int R=\sum_Q\sum_{X_i\in Q}R(X_i)\). Fix a cell \(Q\) with \(n_Q\) points and \(\varphi_Q\) the affine map of \(Q\) onto \([0,1]^2\); conditionally on \(n_Q=n\), the points \(\varphi_Q(X_i)\), \(X_i\in Q\), are i.i.d. uniform. Let \(\mathcal C\) be the 1-Lipschitz functions on \([0,1]^2\) and \(Z_Q=\sup_{f\in\mathcal C}|\sum_{X_i\in Q}(f(\varphi_QX_i)-\int f)|\). Theorem 4.5.3 of [T4], (4.38), gives \(\mathbb E[Z_Q\mid n_Q=n]\le L\sqrt{n\log n}\) for \(n\ge2\) (the class is symmetric, so the absolute value costs nothing); for \(n=1\), \(Z_Q\le\sqrt2\), and for \(n=0\), \(Z_Q=0\); and a point moved changes \(Z_Q\) by at most \(\sqrt2\), the oscillation of a function of \(\mathcal C\); the bounded-differences inequality then gives \(Z_Q\le L\sqrt{n\log n}+\sqrt{12\,n\log N}\) with conditional probability at least \(1-2N^{-3}\). Since \(Nw^2\ge N2^{-2\ell_1}\ge(\log N)^{3/2}\), Chernoff's bound gives \(n_Q\le2Nw^2\) with probability at least \(1-\exp(-(\log N)^{3/2}/3)\). There are at most \(\sum_{j\le\ell_1}4^j\le2N\) cells in all, so with probability at least \(1-LN^{-2}\) both hold for every cell of every mesh, and \(Z_Q\le L\,w\sqrt{N\log N}\). On a cell \(Q\) with \(M_Q=\operatorname{Lip}(G|_Q)>0\), \(f=R\circ\varphi_Q^{-1}/(M_Qw)\) is \(1\)-Lipschitz with \(\int f=0\), so \(|\sum_{X_i\in Q}R(X_i)|=M_Qw\,|\sum_{X_i\in Q}f(\varphi_QX_i)|\le M_Qw\,Z_Q\le L\,M_Q\,w^2\sqrt{N\log N}\); on a cell with \(M_Q=0\), \(R=0\). Summing over cells gives the second term. \(\square\)

Hypotheses, each with its step. \(X_i\) i.i.d. uniform: the conditional uniformity in each cell, Theorem 4.5.3 and the bounded differences, and lem:mesh-function-discrepancy. \(j\le\ell_1\): \(Nw^2\ge(\log N)^{3/2}\) for the cell counts, and lem:mesh-function-discrepancy's range. \(G\) Lipschitz on each open cell and bounded: the rescaled functions \(f\) and the finiteness of \(V_j(E_jG)\); Lipschitz continuity across edges is used only for \(V_j(E_jG)\le8\|\nabla G\|_1\). The remainder bound needs only \(Nw^2\ge C\log N\). The conclusion holds for every \(j\) in the range and every such \(G\) simultaneously.

One gradient level at \((4,4)\)

Corollary. Let \(y\ge e^{16}\) with \(y\le2^{\ell_1}\), and let \(G\) be Lipschitz with \(\int\sum_i\psi^*_4(|\partial_iG|)\le2\) and each \(|\partial_iG|\in\{0\}\cup[y,2y]\) almost everywhere; let \(S\) be the set where \(\nabla G\ne0\), and let \(j\) be any mesh index with \(1\le j\le j^*=\min(\ell_1,\lfloor(\log N)^{2/3}(\log y)^{1/3}\rfloor)\). Then \(\|\nabla G\|_1\le4(\log y)^{-1/4}\) and \[\Big|\sum_iG(X_i)-N\int G\Big|\le L\sqrt{N\log N}\,\big(1+y\,\lambda(U_j)\big),\] \(U_j\) the union of the cells of mesh \(2^{-j}\) on which \(G\) is not constant; in particular, if \(\lambda(U_j)\le K\lambda(S)\), the discrepancy is at most \(L(1+K)\sqrt{N\log N}\).

Proof. For \(x\ge e^{16}\), taking \(t=(\log x)^{1/4}\) in \(\psi_4^*(x)=\sup_t(tx-e^{t^4}+1)\) gives \(\psi_4^*(x)\ge x(\log x)^{1/4}-x\ge\frac12x(\log x)^{1/4}\), since \((\log x)^{1/4}\ge2\). So \(|\partial_iG|\le2\psi_4^*(|\partial_iG|)(\log y)^{-1/4}\) wherever \(\partial_iG\ne0\), and \(\|\nabla G\|_1\le4(\log y)^{-1/4}\). For \(j\le j^*\), \(j^{3/4}(\log y)^{-1/4}\le(\log N)^{1/2}\), so the first term of the lemma is at most \(32L\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})(\log y)^{-1/4}\le64L\sqrt{N\log N}\). Since \(\log y\le\log N\), \(j^*\) is at least of order \(\log y\), and \(j^*=\ell_1\) when \(y\ge N^c\) for a constant \(c\). Every cell has \(\operatorname{Lip}(G|_Q)\le2\sqrt2y\), so the second term is at most \(2\sqrt2L\sqrt{N\log N}\,y\lambda(U_j)\), which is the display. And \(y\lambda(S)\le\|\nabla G\|_1\le4(\log y)^{-1/4}\le2\), since at least one partial is at least \(y\) on \(S\); so \(\lambda(U_j)\le K\lambda(S)\) gives \(y\lambda(U_j)\le2K\); a finer mesh only shrinks \(U_j\), so the best choice is \(j=j^*\). \(\square\)

Degenerate cases. \(G\) constant: \(\|\nabla G\|_1=\Lambda_j=0\) and the left side is \(0\). \(G\) linear: \(\Lambda_j(G)=|\nabla G|\) for every \(j\), and at \(j=1\) the bound is of order \(\sqrt{N\log N}\,|\nabla G|\), the order of (4.38); for \(j\) beyond \((\log N)^{2/3}\) the first term exceeds it. \(j=\ell_1\): the second term is the cellwise Ajtai–Komlós–Tusnády bound at the finest mesh, \(\Lambda_{\ell_1}(G)\) close to \(\int\operatorname{Lip}\). A support thinner than the finest allowed mesh (a band of width \(\varepsilon\ll2^{-j^*}\) and length \(P\)): \(\lambda(U_{j^*})\) is of order \(P2^{-j^*}\), much larger than \(\lambda(S)=\varepsilon P\), and the corollary's second term is not bounded by the target; the corollary gives nothing there. When \(y\ge N^c\), \(2^{-j^*}=2^{-\ell_1}\), about \((\log N)^{3/4}/\sqrt N\).

Nonvacuity. On the curve's parameters at \((4,4)\): \(y=2^{\lfloor\ell_1/2\rfloor}\) (at least \(e^{16}\) for large \(N\)) and \(G(x)=\max(0,1-y\,\|x-c\|_\infty)\) for a centre \(c\): on each of its four triangles one partial is \(\pm y\) and the other \(0\); its support is a square of side \(2/y\), so the cost is \(\frac4{y^2}\psi_4^*(y)\le\frac4y(2\log y)^{1/4}\), below \(2\) for large \(N\) (for \(t\ge(2\log y)^{1/4}\), \(e^{t^4}\ge y^2\ge ty\), so the supremum defining \(\psi_4^*(y)\) is attained below that \(t\)); at the mesh \(2^{-j}=1/y\) (\(y\) a power of \(2\), and \(\log_2y\le j^*\)) the square meets at most \(5\) cells per side, \(\lambda(U_j)\le25/y^2\), while \(\lambda(S)=4/y^2\), so \(K=25/4\).

Architecture test

Architecture test. Not decided. The third route review's test (the class bound at \((4,4)\) for \(G\) at two gradient levels, constant independent of the levels) was attempted again; neither outcome occurred. The lemma is level-free; by order estimates, not proved, its two terms indicate where two levels \(k_1<k_2\) strain a single mesh: a mesh fine enough for the fine level's support charges the coarse level's gradient mass \(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\) against its cost weight \(k_1^{1/4}\), which binds only when \(j^3\) is much larger than \(k_1(\log N)^2\); a coarse mesh gives the fine level's cells Lipschitz constant \(2^{k_2}\) on an area set by the coarse cells meeting its support. A mesh adapted to the level, fine where the gradient is large and coarse where it is small, would separate the two charges; it needs a version of lem:mesh-function-discrepancy for cell functions on a quadtree partition, whose curves are traced on cells of several sizes. That version was not attempted.

Record search. tools/search-claims.py "cellwise Lipschitz constant remainder cell average Ajtai Komlos Tusnady per cell discrepancy gradient support neighbourhood" returned conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy, lem:leighton-shor-class-lower-bound and lem:mesh-function-discrepancy, none a cellwise bound. The reviewer's searches found two more that bear on it: thm:dyadic-flow-square-exponential, whose dyadic flow is a quadtree object for the next step, and lem:tube-density-converse, which defines the cell-uniform smearings needed for the conversion to \(\mu_X-\mu_Y\). tools/claim_resemblance.py on the spec: lem:mesh-function-discrepancy (0.49) and conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy (0.36), both related in the spec, and cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction (0.26); the first is the averaged part this lemma cites.

New mathematics. lem:cellwise-lipschitz-split: the discrepancy of any Lipschitz function is at most \(L\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\) times its gradient mass plus \(L\sqrt{N\log N}\) times the area-weighted sum of its Lipschitz constants on the cells of mesh \(2^{-j}\) (for bounded \(G\) Lipschitz on each cell, with the cell variation of its averages in the first term); at \((4,4)\) it bounds, against Lebesgue measure, the discrepancy of single-level class functions whose support is thick at the mesh \(2^{-j^*}\) by order \(\sqrt{N\log N}\).

Goal progress. Unchanged: no implication toward conj:ultimate-matching changed. Bearing. On the curve's parameters at \((4,4)\), every single-level \(G\) (partials in \(\{0\}\cup[y,2y]\), \(e^{16}\le y\le2^{\ell_1}\)) in the class whose cells of mesh \(2^{-j^*}\) meeting its support have area at most \(K\) times the support has \(|\sum_iG(X_i)-N\int G|\le L(1+K)\sqrt{N\log N}\). Not covered. Supports thinner than \(2^{-j^*}\), two or more levels, gradients below \(e^{16}\) or above \(2^{\ell_1}\), and the conversion from \(X\) against Lebesgue measure to the class's cell-smeared \(\mu_X-\mu_Y\), which was not attempted.

Review. The stand-in review did not run at the last checkpoint; nothing is owed.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • The conversion on the \(X\) side: \(|\sum_iG(X_i)-\int G\,d\mu_X|\le\sqrt2\,s\sum_i\operatorname{Lip}(G\text{ on the cell of }X_i)\), \(s\) the class's grid side, which is of order \(\sqrt{N\log N}\,y\,\lambda(U_s)\), the same form as the corollary's second term; the \(Y\) side depends on the evenly spread definition. Record. lem:tube-density-converse defines the cell-uniform smearings; cor:exponential-matching-discrepancy-reduction states the class with them.
  • A quadtree version of lem:mesh-function-discrepancy: the plain version gains nothing (every quadtree edge lies on the finest grid); a gain would come from counting curves by their quadtree edges, at most \((3j_{\max})^E\) curves with \(E\) edges of dyadic lengths, giving a factor \((\log E+\log\log N)^{3/4}\) per component; at two levels the difficulty moves to the interface term \(\int_{\partial A_1}|H-c|\) at the fine factor. Record. thm:dyadic-flow-square-exponential (the dyadic flow, a quadtree object); no claim counts curves on a quadtree.

Verification gate. Dependencies re-read: lem:mesh-function-discrepancy's full entry, Theorem 4.5.3 of [T4] with (4.38), and (4.107). One fresh-context medium reviewer read the draft, spec, commit message and these excerpts. It found the lemma's proof correct (the split, the conditional uniformity, (4.38) with the absolute value, the bounded-differences constant, Chernoff, the cell count, the rescaling and the sum) and the corollary's steps correct, and passed it with corrections, all made in its wording: the cases \(n_Q\le1\) added; the linear degenerate case restricted to \(j=1\); the lemma stated in its free general version (bounded \(G\), Lipschitz on each cell, first term \(V_j(E_jG)\)); the corollary for every \(j\le j^*\) instead of \(j=\lceil\log_2y\rceil\), with Bearing, Not covered and the thin-band case restated at \(2^{-j^*}\); the partial-derivative index renamed; the Architecture test's charges labelled order estimates with the slack \(\sqrt{\log N}\); the conversion to \(\mu_X-\mu_Y\) said not attempted; the two records its search found added. Process assessment. The corollary fixed the mesh at the instance's natural value instead of the range its proof allows, and the summary sentences called a bound against Lebesgue measure the class order. Prevention. Both fall under audit items committed this session in .claude/agents/medium-reviewer.md (a statement shaped by its prompting instance whose other inputs were not tried; a summary sentence dropping its source's qualifier), which this review applied; and AGENTS.md's rule to name for each hypothesis the weakest form its step needs, which the Hypotheses paragraph now follows.

Meta. The habit is fixing a free parameter at the first value that works and then writing every downstream sentence at that value. The same habit stated the mesh lemma with \(j\) where \(k+j\) was available. The change: when a statement has a free parameter, I write the range its proof allows before choosing a value; the reviewer now audits "a parameter fixed at one value where the proof allows a range" (.claude/agents/medium-reviewer.md).

Leighton–Shor discrepancy on graded partitions mixing a coarse and a fine mesh

16 October 2026 · Working proof; two-level test not decided · Two meshes. Produced by Claude Code (claude-opus-5-5, reasoning effort set by the session).

Where we stand. Unchanged: The entry adds a tool lemma and an undecided test; the goal's state in Where we stand is unchanged.

Question. The cellwise Lipschitz split bounds the averaged part on a single mesh with the finest mesh's factor on every gradient level. Can a partition that is fine only where needed charge each part its own factor? The missing implication is conj:conjugate-class-discrepancy at \((4,4)\) for two gradient levels, the third route review's decisive test; this entry proves the needed partition bound and reports the attempt.

General statement. lem:two-mesh-partition-discrepancy, for every pair of meshes up to the Leighton–Shor scale, every graded partition (dyadic squares of sides between \(2^{-j_2}\) and \(2^{-j_1}\)) and every function constant on its cells.

Two-mesh partition discrepancy

Setting. As in lem:mesh-function-discrepancy, with \(1\le j_1\le j_2\le\ell_1\). A graded partition \(\mathcal P\) is a partition of \([0,1]^2\) into dyadic squares of sides between \(2^{-j_2}\) and \(2^{-j_1}\); the two-mesh partitions (some cells of \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\) kept, each other one replaced by its cells of \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\)) are the case of two sizes. The edges of \(\mathcal P\) are the maximal segments of cell sides containing no cell vertex in their interior; they lie on \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\). An edge is coarse if it is a whole edge of \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\) (then both endpoints are in \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\)), and fine otherwise. For \(H\) constant on the cells and a real \(c\), \(V^c(H)\) is the sum over inner edges of length times jump plus the sum over boundary edges of length times \(|H-c|\), as in lem:mesh-function-discrepancy, and \(V^c_{\mathrm f}(H)\) the same sum over fine edges only.

Statement. There is a universal \(L\) such that with probability tending to one, for all \(j_1\le j_2\) in \([1,\ell_1]\), every graded partition \(\mathcal P\), every \(H\) constant on its cells and every \(c\), \[\Big|\sum_{i\le N}H(X_i)-N\int H\Big|\le L\sqrt N\Big(\big(j_1^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\big)V^c(H)+\big(j_2^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N}\big)V^c_{\mathrm f}(H)\Big).\] In particular, for a closed simple curve \(C\) traced on the edges of \(\mathcal P\), with \(\ell_{\mathrm f}(C)\) the length of its fine edges, \(|\sum_i(1_{\mathring C}(X_i)-\lambda(\mathring C))|\le L\sqrt N((j_1^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\ell(C)+(j_2^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\ell_{\mathrm f}(C))\).

Proof. We work on the event of lem:mesh-function-discrepancy, whose part (a) gives, for a closed simple curve on \(\mathcal G_j\), the bound \(L\sqrt N\ell(C)(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\) after \(\log_2(2^{j+3}\ell(C))\le2j+5\).

Step 1: closed chains. Let \(Z\) be a closed 1-chain with integer coefficients on the edges of \(\mathcal G_j\), with mass \(\|Z\|\) (the sum of \(|\)coefficient\(|\) times length). Cancelling opposite orientations does not increase the mass; the oriented edges, with multiplicity, then have as many entering as leaving at each vertex, so they split into closed directed walks, and a walk visiting a vertex twice splits there; so \(Z=\sum_sC_s\) with \(C_s\) oriented closed simple curves on \(\mathcal G_j\) and \(\sum_s\ell(C_s)\le\|Z\|\). The winding number of \(Z\) about a point off the grid is \(w_Z=\sum_s\varepsilon_s1_{\mathring C_s}\), \(\varepsilon_s=\pm1\), so \(|\sum_iw_Z(X_i)-N\int w_Z|\le L\sqrt N(j^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\|Z\|\).

Step 2: a curve on \(\mathcal P\). Let \(C\) be an oriented closed simple curve on the edges of \(\mathcal P\). Its edges lie on \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\). If it has no coarse edge, it is a curve on \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\) with \(\ell=\ell_{\mathrm f}\), and (a) applies. Otherwise its fine edges form maximal runs \(P\), each from an endpoint of a coarse edge to an endpoint of a coarse edge, both vertices of \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\). Only these two facts about \(\mathcal P\) are used: its edges lie on \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\), and its coarse edges are whole edges of \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\). For each run let \(S_P\) be the staircase on \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\) from the start of \(P\) to its end, horizontal then vertical, of length \(|q-p|_1\le\ell(P)\), since \(P\) is an axis-parallel path from \(p\) to \(q\). Then \(C=Z_1+\sum_P(P-S_P)\) as chains, with \(Z_1\) the coarse edges of \(C\) plus all the \(S_P\): \(Z_1\) is a closed chain on \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\) of mass at most \(\ell(C)\), and each \(P-S_P\) is a closed chain on \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\) (which contains \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\)) of mass at most \(2\ell(P)\). Winding numbers are additive in the chain, so \(1_{\mathring C}=w_{Z_1}+\sum_Pw_{P-S_P}\) off the grid, and Step 1 at \(j_1\) and at \(j_2\) gives the curve bound with \(2\ell_{\mathrm f}(C)\), the constant \(2\) going into \(L\).

Step 3: functions. For a union \(A\) of cells of \(\mathcal P\), the oriented boundary edges of \(\mathcal P\) split into closed simple curves on the edges of \(\mathcal P\), each edge in exactly one, as in Step 2 of lem:mesh-function-discrepancy; so \(\sum_s\ell(C_s)=\ell(\partial A)\), \(\sum_s\ell_{\mathrm f}(C_s)=\ell_{\mathrm f}(\partial A)\), and \(1_A=\sum_s\varepsilon_s1_{\mathring C_s}\). The layer cake of that lemma's Step 3, with the same \(c\) for both sums, gives the statement, since an edge lies on \(\partial\{H>t\}\) or \(\partial\{H\le t\}\) for \(t\) in an interval of length its jump (or \(|H-c|\) on the boundary). \(\square\)

Hypotheses, each with its step. The event of lem:mesh-function-discrepancy (a), at \(j_1\) and \(j_2\): Steps 1 and 2; it carries the hypotheses \(X_i\) i.i.d. uniform and \(j_2\le\ell_1\). The partition's form: Step 2 uses only that the edges lie on \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\) and that coarse edges are whole edges of \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\), so a fine run ends where coarse edges begin, at vertices of \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\); every partition into dyadic squares of sides between \(2^{-j_2}\) and \(2^{-j_1}\) has both. Nothing else: no property of \(H\) beyond being constant on cells. The conclusion holds simultaneously for all pairs of meshes, partitions, functions and constants.

Degenerate cases. \(j_1=j_2\): every partition is \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\), \(V_{\mathrm f}\le V\), and the bound is lem:mesh-function-discrepancy (b) up to the constant. All cells of side \(2^{-j_2}\): \(V_{\mathrm f}=V\) and it is (b) at \(j_2\). All cells of side \(2^{-j_1}\): \(V_{\mathrm f}=0\) and it is (b) at \(j_1\). \(H\) constant: both variations vanish at \(c=H\).

Nonvacuity. On the curve's parameters, for context (the lemma does not depend on the pair), with \(N\) large so that \(2\le j_2\le\ell_1\): \(j_1=1\), \(j_2=\lfloor\frac13\log_2N\rfloor\), \(\mathcal P\) keeping the three cells of \(\mathcal G_1\) other than the lower left one. For \(H\) the indicator of the lower left cell of \(\mathcal G_{j_2}\), \(V^0(H)=V^0_{\mathrm f}(H)=4\cdot2^{-j_2}\) and the discrepancy is at most \(8L\sqrt N2^{-j_2}((\frac13\log_2N)^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\). For \(H\) the indicator of the upper right kept cell, all its edges are coarse, \(V^0(H)=2\) and \(V^0_{\mathrm f}(H)=0\), so the bound is \(2L\sqrt N(1+\sqrt{\log N})\), which (b) at \(j_1\) already gives. A witness only the lemma reaches is the sum of the two indicators: \(V^0=2+4\cdot2^{-j_2}\) and \(V^0_{\mathrm f}=4\cdot2^{-j_2}\), so the bound is \(L\sqrt N((1+\sqrt{\log N})(2+4\cdot2^{-j_2})+(j_2^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})4\cdot2^{-j_2})\); (b) at \(j_1\) does not apply (the sum is not constant on the cells of \(\mathcal G_1\)), and (b) at \(j_2\) would charge \(j_2^{3/4}\) on the \(2\).

Architecture test

Architecture test. Not decided. The third route review's test (the class bound at \((4,4)\) for \(G\) at two gradient levels \(y_1<y_2\), constant independent of the levels) was attempted with the lemma; neither outcome occurred. The attempt: keep the cells of \(\mathcal G_{j_1}\) that do not meet the level-\(y_2\) support \(S_2\), subdivide the set \(U\) of those that do, take \(H\) the partition averages of \(G\) and the remainder \(G-H\) cell by cell as in lem:cellwise-lipschitz-split (that its per-cell argument extends to the cells of a mixed partition is expected, not checked). The translation sweep bounds the jumps across coarse edges and across edges between fine cells by the gradient mass of the two cells. Across the fine edges of a kept cell \(Q\) beside a subdivided cell \(Q'\), \(|\operatorname{avg}_Q G-\operatorname{avg}_qG|\le|\operatorname{avg}_QG-\operatorname{avg}_{Q'}G|+\operatorname{osc}_{Q'}G\), so summed over the side, of length \(2^{-j_1}\), the jump is at most \(\int_{Q\cup Q'}|\partial G|+2^{-j_1}\operatorname{osc}_{Q'}G\). So, up to the boundary ring in both (the analogue of part (c) on a mixed partition was not proved, and fine boundary edges count in \(V^c_{\mathrm f}\)), \(V^c\) is at most a constant times \(\|\nabla G\|_1\) plus the interface sum \(I=\sum_{Q'}2^{-j_1}\operatorname{osc}_{Q'}G\) over subdivided cells beside kept ones, and \(V^c_{\mathrm f}\) at most a constant times \(\|\nabla G\|_{L^1(U')}+I\), \(U'\) the union of \(U\) and the kept cells beside it. At \((4,4)\), by the order estimates of the earlier entries (not proved here), the fine factor then falls on two quantities beyond the level-\(y_2\) mass: (i) the level-\(y_1\) gradient mass inside \(U'\), charged \(j_2^{3/4}\) against its weight \((\log y_1)^{1/4}\), and (ii) the interface sum \(I\). Only (i) is exhibited: many small level-\(y_2\) bumps scattered over a level-\(y_1\) ramp make \(U\) the whole square, which puts the ramp's whole mass at the fine factor (and makes \(I=0\)); no example with \(I\) large was computed. It stopped there. A graded partition, fine only within distance about the fine mesh of \(S_2\) and coarsening geometrically away from it, would make (i) local; it needs Step 2 iterated over several meshes, which was not attempted.

Record search. tools/search-claims.py "two mesh partition coarse fine cells staircase chord winding number chain decomposition Leighton Shor quadtree" returned lem:mesh-function-discrepancy, lem:anisotropic-leighton-shor-bound and lem:cellwise-lipschitz-split, none a bound on a mixed partition. tools/claim_resemblance.py on the spec: lem:mesh-function-discrepancy (0.59) and lem:cellwise-lipschitz-split (0.43), both related in the spec, and thm:low-level-mixed-counts (0.19); the first is the single-mesh case, which this lemma contains at \(j_1=j_2\).

New mathematics. lem:two-mesh-partition-discrepancy: on a graded partition (dyadic squares of sides between \(2^{-j_2}\) and \(2^{-j_1}\)), a cell function's discrepancy is at most \(L\sqrt N\) times its total variation at the coarse factor plus its variation across fine edges at the fine factor; for curves, only the fine length pays the fine factor, by closing each fine run with a coarse staircase.

Goal progress. Unchanged: no implication toward conj:ultimate-matching changed. Bearing. On the curve's parameters, any function constant on a graded partition, in particular the partition averages of a class function at \((4,4)\), has discrepancy at most \(L\sqrt N((j_1^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})V^c+(j_2^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})V^c_{\mathrm f})\). Not covered. The bound of the partition averages' variations by the class's cost, the boundary ring on a mixed partition (part (c) of lem:mesh-function-discrepancy is for a single mesh), and the remainders inside the cells of a mixed partition.

Review. The stand-in review did not run at the last checkpoint; nothing is owed.

Reviewer's suggestions, unverified

  • A cascade over geometric levels \(j_{k+1}\ge2j_k\): applying the staircase step from the finest level down, each edge \(e\) is charged \(\sum_{k\le\mathrm{level}(e)}2(j_k^{3/4}+\sqrt{\log N})\), whose first part is within a constant of \(j(e)^{3/4}\) and whose second part is \(K\sqrt{\log N}\), \(K\le\log\log N\) the number of levels, a loss at the target's order unless the per-level deviation is reduced to \(\sqrt{j_k+\log\log N}\) as the mesh-function entry's reviewer suggested. Record. tools/search-claims.py "graded partition quadtree adaptive mesh dyadic refinement discrepancy" (the reviewer's search) returned lem:cellwise-lipschitz-split and lem:mesh-function-discrepancy only.
  • For the interface sum: \(\operatorname{osc}_{Q'}G\le\sqrt2\,2^{-j_1}\operatorname{Lip}(G|_{Q'})\), so \(I\) is at most of order \(y_2\) times the area of the interface cells, small when they are few compared with the support. Record. lem:cellwise-lipschitz-split, whose second term has the same form.

Verification gate. Dependency re-read: lem:mesh-function-discrepancy's full entry (parts (a)–(c), Steps 2–3).
